A Complementary Approach to Incorrectness Typing DOI: XXXXXXX.XXXXXXX
Celia Mengyue Li, Sophie Pull, Steven Ramsay

Abstract
. We introduce a new two-sided type system for verifying the correctness and incorrectness of functional programs with atoms and pattern matching. A key idea in the work is that types should range over sets of normal forms, rather than sets of values, and this allows us to define a complement operator on types that acts as a negation on typing formulas. We show that the complement allows us to derive a wide range of refutation principles within the system, including the type-theoretic analogue of co-implication, and we use them to certify that a number of Erlang-like programs go wrong. An expressive axiomatisation of the complement operator via subtyping is shown decidable, and the type system as a whole is shown to be not only sound, but also complete for normal forms.



1.
Introduction

Since at least Milner’s influential 1978 paper
(
Milner 1978
)
, an important role of type systems in programming languages has been to provide a formal mechanism for reasoning statically about how programs will behave at runtime. In this role, types
A
are understood as a syntax for describing properties of (the behaviour of) program expressions
M
, and a derivation of the typing
M:A
amounts to a proof that
M
satisfies
A
.

It is often said that a typical purpose of such reasoning systems is the detection of runtime errors
(
Dunfield and Krishnaswami 2021
;
Pitts 2012
)
. However, in a traditional type system, the means by which this benefit is realised is somewhat indirect, because the type system is designed only to certify the absence of such errors. When a program is not well typed, it may be that it harbours a runtime error, but it could also be that the program simply lies beyond reasoning power of the type system.

To be able to detect runtime errors, we need a system that can reason about programs that go wrong. However, such a capacity is incompatible with the prevailing approach to type systems for programming languages. Typically, the soundness of the type system implies that
well-typed programs cannot go wrong
, and since it is also typical that we can only construct type derivations for well-typed programs, it follows that we cannot derive properties of a program that goes wrong. Such programs simply don’t exist as far as the type system is concerned.

An alternative approach was proposed by Ramsay and Walpole in their POPL’24 paper
(
Ramsay and Walpole 2024
)
. Their
two-sided type systems
allow for an extended form of typing judgement, whose shape is generally as follows:

M_{1}:A_{1},\,\ldots,\,M_{k}:A_{k}\mathrel{\vdash}N_{1}:B_{1},\,\ldots,\,N_{m}:B_{m}

Two-sided type systems lift the restriction that typing formulas
M:A
on the left of the turnstile must be variable typings (i.e.
M
must be a variable), and the restriction that there must be exactly one typing formula on the right-hand side. However, the meaning of such a judgement smoothly generalises that of the traditional typing judgement: if
all
of the
M_{i}
reduce to values of type
A_{i}
, then
some

N_{i}
is sure to reduce to a value of type
B_{i}
(or diverge in the process).

In addition to the usual kinds of typing judgements, which appear as a special case, two-sided judgements can express that certain conditions are
necessary
for proper evaluation. For example, consider a typical definition of exponentiation
x^{y}
as a fixed point involving multiplication, subtraction and an equality test (defined only) on integers
1
1

1

In this paper we write the integer literal corresponding to
n
as
\underline{n}
:
\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{power}}}\coloneqq\mathsf{fix}\,f.\,\lambda xy.\,\mathsf{if}\,y=\underline{0}\,\mathsf{then}\,\underline{1}\,\mathsf{else}\,x*(f\,x\,(y-1))
. In the system of this paper, it is both true and provable that:

\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{power}}}\,x\,y:\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\vdash}y:\mathsf{Int}

In other words,
if
the term
\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{power}}}\,x\,y
reduces to a value,
then
the second input,
y
,
must
have been an integer. By contrast, however, the following judgement, is
not
true:

\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{power}}}\,x\,y:\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\vdash}x:\mathsf{Int}

It states that, for the power function to produce an integer value, the first input must have been an integer:
if

\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{power}}}\,x\,y
evaluates to a integer,
then

x
is an integer.
A quick perusal of the definition of
power
, however, suffices to convince oneself that it is not
necessary
; the term
\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{power}}}\,(\lambda z.\,z)\,\underline{0}
will evaluate to a integer value without complaint.

Two-sided type systems sidestep the problem of
\mathrel{\vdash}M:A
being derivable only for
M
that cannot go wrong by allowing to prove a kind of negation
M:A\mathrel{\vdash}\
. The meaning of this judgement is: if
M
evaluates to a value of type
A
then false, or simply
M
cannot evaluate to a value of type
A
. With a type
\mathsf{Ok}
representing all values, one can prove, for example:

\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{power}}}\,\underline{0}\,(\lambda z.\,z):\mathsf{Ok}\mathrel{\vdash}

Since
\mathsf{Ok}
is the type of
all
values, a proof of this judgement is a certificate that exponentiation of zero and the identity function,
in that order
, either diverges or is sure to go wrong.

However, the original development of two-sided type systems in
(
Ramsay and Walpole 2024
)
had some significant weaknesses, which we shall summarise here, and discuss in detail in the main body of the paper. First, although the form of judgment is as above, most of the metatheory was developed only under the
single-subject restriction
in which at most one of the
M_{1},\ldots,M_{k},N_{1},\ldots,N_{m}
is not a variable. Second, although the two-sided setup naturally lends itself to a notion of complement on types, it was accommodated at the expense of a weaker soundness guarantee. Third, the systems of that work are formed from a large number of primitive rules, some overlapping, and with many not having a clear justification for their inclusion in the theory.

1.1.
A Two-Sided System with Complement on Normal Forms

In this paper, we present a new two-sided type system which both overcomes these weaknesses and is an interesting type system in its own right. As well as certifying that programs cannot go wrong (as usual), the system can also prove that programs cannot go right. This latter capacity gives it a similar power to O’Hearn’s Incorrectness Logic for first-order imperative programs
(
O’Hearn 2019
)
, and Lindahl and Sagonas’ Success Types for Erlang
(
Lindahl and Sagonas 2006
)
(though neither of these have the former capacity). Inspired by this connection, we use our system to certify the defectiveness of a number of textbook Erlang examples due to
(
Hébert 2013
)
, including the following program, which is a textbook example used to demonstrate the limitations of Dialyzer’s type inference, and which our system can prove to be incorrect:

⬇

let

money

=

\lambda xy.\,(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{give},(x,y))

in

let

item

=

\lambda x.

match

x

with

\left|\begin{array}[]{lcl}(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{count},\,(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{give},\,(y,\,z)))&\mapsto&y;\\
(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{account},\,(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{give},\,(y,\,z)))&\mapsto&z;\quad\mathsf{in}\end{array}\right.

let

op

=

\lambda xy.\,x+y

in

let

tup

=

\textit{money}\,\underline{5}\,\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{you}

in

op

(
item

(
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{count}
,

tup
))

(
item

(
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{account}
,

tup
))

The reasoning encoded in the typing derivation is roughly as follows. The expression in the innermost body of the let,
\textit{op}\,(\textit{item}\,(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{count},\,\mathit{tup}))\,(\textit{item},\,(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{account},\,\mathit{tup}))
is guaranteed to crash if
all
of the following are true:

(1)

op
is a function that requires that its second argument evaluates to an integer

(2)

and,
item
is a function that, to return an integer, requires an input that does not reduce to a value of shape
(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{account},\,(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{give},\,(P,\,Q)))
, unless
Q
is an integer

(3)

and,
tup
does reduce to a value of shape
(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{give},\,(P,\,Q))
, where
Q
is not an integer.

Since it is also possible to derive a typing for each of
op
,
item
and
tup
that certifies that they
do
satisfy (i), (ii) and (iii), we must conclude that the program as a whole did not evaluate to a value.

A key new idea in our work is that types should not be limited to describing sets of values, but instead should describe sets of normal forms more generally. To motivate this idea, let us make a little more precise what is meant by
going wrong
. In general, each type system has its own notion of what it means to go wrong, because type systems have been designed to guarantee various different notions of safety, such as the classical type safety of Milner, memory safety, thread safety and so on. However, it seems reasonable to consider safety properties in general, that is, that programs do not reach some kind of
bad state
.

An elegant way to define bad states in a functional programming languages is as those program expressions that are neither values nor that can make a reduction step: we will call them
stuck terms
. In the language we study, we will consider both a classical notion of type safety, so that e.g. applying a number to a function,
\underline{3}\,(\lambda x.\,x)
, is a stuck term, and we will consider pattern-matching safety, so that, e.g. failing to match any branch in a case expression,
\mathsf{match}\,(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a},\,\underline{3})\,\mathsf{with}\,\{(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{b},\,x)\mapsto x\}
, is also stuck.

In the traditional approach to safety via type systems, one would establish a soundness theorem to ensure that any typable program
M:A
cannot reduce to a stuck term. In other words, if typable program
M
normalises then its normal form must be a value of the type
A
. This is therefore consistent with the usual semantic interpretation of
A
as a certain set of values (modulo divergence). Since, for incorrectness, we want to reason about programs that go wrong, it follows that we should allow types that number among their inhabitants also the stuck terms. Since stuck terms and values, together, constitute all possible normal forms, we see the type system as a formal system for reasoning about how terms will reduce to normal form, in general, and types simply as sets of normal forms.

The interpretation of types as sets of normal forms allows us to axiomatise a complement operator on types, the type
{A}^{c}
is interpreted as all normal forms that are not in the type
A
,
without
weakening our soundness guarantees. Moreover, alongside a reasonably expressive subtyping relation, it allows us to form the type system from a small number of well-motivated rules, and yet induce an extremely rich theory – we show that a wide variety of interesting typing principles are derivable, including analogues of all the key refutational rules of
(
Ramsay and Walpole 2024
)
.

In fact, complement with respect to normal forms arises naturally in any consideration of typing for incorrectness, and not only in the two-sided setting. If we want to be able to assign types to terms that go wrong, then we must be able to describe, within the type system, the conditions under which terms of different shapes should be considered stuck. For example, an integer addition
M+N
is stuck if
M
is stuck, or if
M
is some value other than an integer. In other words,
M+N
is stuck whenever
M
is some
normal form
that is
not
in the type
\mathsf{Int}
of integer values, that is, when
M:{\mathsf{Int}}^{c}
is derivable. Similarly, the second projection
\pi_{2}(M)
of some term
M
is stuck whenever
M
is either itself stuck or when
M
is some value other than a pair value. In other words, when
M
is some
normal form
that is
not
in the type of all pair values, or
M:{\mathsf{PairVal}}^{c}
for short. Function applications
M\,N
are stuck whenever
M
is a normal form
not
in the type of all functions, and so on. In fact, we are able to show that not only is our type system sound, but it admits a complete classification of normal forms: every normal form can be proven either to be a value or to be a stuck term. By contrast, most type systems in the literature do not satisfy even the value part of this property, that is, it is typical that there are values that are not well typed.

Contributions

In sum, our contributions are as follows:

(1)

We define a two-sided type system for a functional programming language with atoms and pattern matching. The system exploits two-sidedness to give a natural and expressive rule for typing pattern-matching. Unlike
(
Ramsay and Walpole 2024
)
, the system is formed from a small number of well-motivated rules.

(2)

We show that the refutational power of the system is, nevertheless, excellent, by deriving (analogues of) the key refutation typing rules from the original work (Theorem
4.1
). Moreover, we show the potential of the idea by certifying that a sequence of textbook Erlang programs due to
Hébert 2013
all go wrong.

(3)

We introduce the coarrow type
A\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$/\penalty$\cr$\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}$\crcr}}}}B
as a type-theoretic counterpart to coimplication (as featured in co- and bi-intuitionistic logic
(
Rauszer 1974
;
Crolard 2001
;
Tranchini 2017
)
), and show that it is the appropriate conceptual tool with which to reason refutationally about functions.

(4)

We show soundness of the subtype relation via a natural set-theoretic model (Theorem
6.1
), and we show its decidability by describing an equivalent alternative characterisation (Theorem
6.3
), whose decision problem can be reduced to bottom-up proof search (Theorem
6.4
).

(5)

We show that the system is equivalent to a one-sided system with multiple conclusions (Theorem
7.2
), that is, in every judgement
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta
, the assumptions
\Gamma
may be taken to comprise only typings of shape
x:A
. By introducing a new technical device for working with non-single-subject
\Delta
, we argue progress and preservation for the one-sided system, and thus obtain soundness of the original system (Theorem
7.3
).

Outline

The rest of the paper is structured as follows. We introduce the programming language and its semantics in Section
2
; the types, subtyping relation and type system in Section
3
. We then derive the key principles of refutation and put them to work certifying Hébert’s Erlang examples in Section
4
. This leads into a discussion of how to express necessity in conjunction with curried functions and the introduction of the coarrow as counterpart to coimplication in Section
5
. We then develop the metatheory of subtyping in Section
6
and of the type system in Section
7
. We conclude with a discussion of related work in Section
8
. Proofs are contained in the supplementary materials.

2.
A Functional Programming Language

We define a functional language with integers, pairing, recursive functions, Erlang-style atoms and matching.

Definition 2.1 (Terms and Patterns).

Assume a denumerable set of term variables
x
,
y
,
z
and a denumerable set of atoms
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}
,
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{b}
,
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{c}
including the distinguished atoms
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{true}
and
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{false}
.
The patterns of the language, typically
p
,
q
are:

p,q\Coloneqq x\mid\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}\mid(p,q)\\

Patterns are required to be
linear
in the sense that each variable occurs in
p
at most once.

The
terms
of the language, typically
M
,
N
,
P
,
Q
, are defined by the following grammar. Here,
\underline{n}
denotes the numeral for integer
n
, the symbol
\otimes
represents any reasonable choice of binary operations on integers, e.g.
+
,
-
,
*
, and
\Join
any reasonable choice of binary relations on integers, e.g.
\leq
,
=
and so on.

M,N\Coloneqq x\mid\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}\mid\underline{n}\mid(M,N)\mid\lambda x.\,\;\!\!M\mid M\;\!N\mid\mathsf{fix}\,x.\,\;\!\!M\mid{M}\mathord{\otimes}{N}\mid M\mathord{\Join}N\mid\mathsf{match}\,M\,\mathsf{with}\,\{\mid_{i=1}^{k}\;\!\!p_{i}\!\mapsto\!N_{i}\}\\

The patterns
p_{1},\,\ldots,\,p_{k}
in a match expression are required to be
disjoint
, that is, a given term will match at most one.
We consider terms identified up to renaming of bound variables and adopt the usual conventions regarding their treatment. The set of free variables of a term
M
is written
\mathsf{FV}(M)
. A term with no free variables is said to be
closed
.

A
term substitution
, typically
\sigma
,
\tau
, is a finite map from term variables to terms. We write the application of a substitution
\sigma
to a term
M
postfix, as in
M\sigma
. We use
\textbf{dom}(\sigma)
for the domain of the map
\sigma
.
We write concrete substitutions explicitly as a list of maplets, as in
[M_{1}/x_{1},\ldots,M_{n}/x_{n}]
.

Finally, the
values
, typically
V
,
W
, are those
closed
terms that can be described by the following:

V,\,W\Coloneqq\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}\mid\underline{n}\mid(V,\,W)\mid\lambda x.\,M

Most of the language is standard, we mention only that: atoms
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}
are literals without internal structure, they may only be compared via pattern matching, and the pattern match expression
\mathsf{match}\,M\,\mathsf{with}\,\{\mid_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\mapsto N_{i}\}
scrutinises the term
M
, and if
M
matches one of the (disjoint) patterns
p_{i}
then it will evaluate as
N_{i}
.

Abbreviations

For
i\in[1,2]
we write
\pi_{i}(M)
as an abbreviation for
\mathsf{match}\,M\,\mathsf{with}\,\{(x_{1},\,x_{2})\mapsto x_{i}\}
. We write
div
for
\mathsf{fix}\,x.\,x
. We write
\mathsf{if}\,M\,\mathsf{then}\,N\,\mathsf{else}\,P
as an abbreviation for
\mathsf{match}\,M\,\mathsf{with}\,\{\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{true}\mapsto N\mid\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{false}\mapsto P\}
.

The language reduces according to a call-by-name strategy
2
2

2

This matters little for the development, but we were interested to explore CBN as
(
Ramsay and Walpole 2024
)
anticipates some difficulty when changing the meaning of typing formulas on the left of the turnstile to accommodate the fact that they may not evaluate. However, we observed that the system is already sound for call-by-name without any change to the semantics, the crux of which is contained in Remark
3
. However, in the interests of keeping the semantics of pattern matching simple, and a little closer to that of Erlang, we require that pairing and matching are strict. We reduce the left-component of a pair first, and only if it reduces to a value do we reduce the second component.

Definition 2.2 (Reduction).

The
evaluation contexts
are defined by the following grammar:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathcal{E},\,\mathcal{F}&\Coloneqq&[\cdot]\mid\mathcal{E}\,N\mid(\mathcal{E},N)\mid(V,\mathcal{E})\mid\mathcal{E}\otimes N\mid\underline{n}\otimes\mathcal{E}\mid\mathcal{E}\Join N\mid\underline{n}\Join\mathcal{E}\mid\mathsf{match}\,\mathcal{E}\,\mathsf{with}\,\{\mid_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\mapsto N_{i}\}\end{array}

Given a context
\mathcal{E}
, we write
\mathcal{E}\mathcal{[}M]
for the term obtained by replacing the hole
[\cdot]
by
M
. The
one-step reduction relation
,
M\,\ \mathord{\rhd}\,\ N
, is the binary relation on terms obtained as the closure of the following schema under evaluation contexts.

\begin{array}[]{rclcrcl}(\lambda x.\,M)\,N&\,\ \mathord{\rhd}&M[N/x]&&\mathsf{fix}\,x.\,M&\,\ \mathord{\rhd}&M[\mathsf{fix}\,x.\,M/x]\\[5.69054pt]
\pi_{i}(V_{1},V_{2})&\,\ \mathord{\rhd}&V_{i}\quad\;\;\,(i\in\{1,2\})&&\underline{n}\otimes\underline{m}&\,\ \mathord{\rhd}&\underline{n\otimes m}\\[5.69054pt]
\mathsf{match}\,V\,\mathsf{with}\,\{\mid_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\mapsto N_{i}\}&\,\ \mathord{\rhd}&p_{j}\sigma\quad(V=N_{j}\sigma)&&\underline{n}\Join\underline{m}&\,\ \mathord{\rhd}&\mbox{\textquotesingle}{(n\Join m)}\end{array}

The terms on the left-hand side of the schema are said to be
redexes
.
We write
\mathord{\rhd}^{\!*}
for the reflexive transitive closure.

Definition 2.3 (Normal forms, values, stuck terms and going wrong).

A term that cannot make a step is said to be in
normal form
, and we reserve the letter
U
as a metavariable for those terms that are in normal form.
Closed
normal forms can be partitioned into two disjoint sets: the values, which we have already introduced, and the stuck terms.
A closed term is said to be a
stuck term
, for which we use the metavariables
S
and
T
, just if it is a normal form but
not
a value. A term is said to
go wrong
just if it reduces to a stuck term, and to
evaluate
just if it reduces to a value.

Lemma 2.4 (Stuck Terms).

The stuck terms may equivalently be described as those closed terms that can be described by the following grammar:

\begin{array}[]{rcll}S,\,T&\Coloneqq&(S,\,M)\mid(V,\,S)&\\
&\mid&U\otimes M\mid\underline{n}\otimes U\mid U\Join M\mid\underline{n}\Join U&\text{when $U$ is not of shape $\underline{n}$}\\
&\mid&U\,M&\text{when $U$ is not of shape $\lambda x.\,M$}\\
&\mid&\mathsf{match}\,U\,\mathsf{with}\,\{\mid_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\mapsto N_{i}\}&\text{when $U$ is a value and $\forall i.\,\forall\sigma.\,U\neq p_{i}\sigma$, or $U$ stuck}\end{array}

Observe the use of negation in the above definition: it is this that will later make the complement operator on types a particularly useful conceptual tool in reasoning about terms that go wrong. For example, we will be able to encode the side condition on stuck terms of shape
U\,M
by the typing formula
U:{\mathsf{Fun}}^{c}
. Note also that, in stuck terms of shape
(S,\,M)
, the term
M
in the second component is arbitrary – it may not even be a normal form. For example,
(\underline{1}\,(\lambda x.\,x),\,\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{div}}})
is stuck, because pairs are evaluated eagerly from left-to-right, reduction stops once the first component becomes stuck.

3.
Two-Sided System

We start by defining the language of types and associated pattern type substitutions.

Definition 3.1 (Types).

The types of the system, typically
A
,
B
,
C
, are given by the following:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}A,B&\Coloneqq&\mathsf{Top}\mid\mathsf{Ok}\mid\mathsf{Atom}{}\mid\mathsf{Int}{}\mid\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}\mid(A,\,B)\mid A\to B\mid\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}A_{i}\mid{A}^{c}\end{array}

We define a
pattern type substitution
as a finite mapping, typically
\theta
, from
term
variables to types. Like term substitutions, we will often write pattern type substitutions concretely using the notation
[A_{1}/x_{1},\ldots,A_{n}/x_{n}]
, and apply them postfix.

The types can be assigned meanings as sets of closed normal forms. Making precise this meaning is useful for intuition, and it will be used be used formally in the statement and proof of the soundness of subtyping, Theorem
6.1
. For each type
A
, we define the set of closed normal forms
[\![A]\!]
and the set of closed terms
[\![A]\!]_{\ast}
, by mutual induction on
A
:

\begin{array}[]{rclcrcl}[\![A]\!]_{\ast}&=&\lx@intercol\{\,M\mid\text{if $M$ reduces to a normal form $U$, then $U\in[\![A]\!]$}\,\}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[5.69054pt]
[\![\mathsf{Top}]\!]&=&\{\,U\mid\text{$U$ is a closed normal form}\,\}&&[\![\mathsf{Int}]\!]&=&\{\,\underline{n}\mid n\in\mathbb{Z}\,\}\\
[\![\mathsf{Ok}]\!]&=&\{\,V\mid\text{$V$ is a value}\,\}&&[\![\textstyle\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}A_{i}]\!]&=&\textstyle\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}[\![A_{i}]\!]\\
[\![\mathsf{Atom}]\!]&=&\{\,\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}\mid\text{$\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}$ is an atom}\,\}&&[\![{A}^{c}]\!]&=&[\![\mathsf{Top}]\!]\setminus[\![A]\!]\\
[\![A\to B]\!]&=&\{\,\lambda x.\,M\mid\forall N\in[\![A]\!]_{\ast}.\>M[N/x]\in[\![B]\!]_{\ast}\,\}&&[\![\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}]\!]&=&\{\,\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}\,\}\\
[\![(A,\,B)]\!]&=&\lx@intercol\{\,(V,\,U)\mid V\in[\![A]\!]\cap[\![\mathsf{Ok}]\!],\,U\in[\![B]\!]\,\}\cup\{(S,\,N)\mid S\in[\![A]\!]\cap[\![{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}]\!],\,N\in[\![B]\!]_{\ast}\,\}\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}

As can be seen from the semantics of types, the idea is that each type represents a certain set of normal forms.
\mathsf{Top}
is the type of all normal forms,
\mathsf{Ok}
is the type of all values,
\mathsf{Atom}
the type of all atoms and
\mathsf{Int}
the type of all integers.

We use the syntax
(A,\,B)
for the type of pairs whose components are of type
A
and
B
respectively, rather than, say
A\times B
. This is convenient for our later discussion of the typing of pattern matching, because it means that we can discuss a term instance
p\sigma
of a pattern
p
, and its type
p\theta
using the same notation and a
pattern type substitution
. For example, we will be able to prove that
\mathrel{\vdash}(x,y)[(\lambda z.\,z)/x,\,\underline{2}/y]:(x,y)[(\mathsf{Int}\to\mathsf{Int})/x,\,\mathsf{Int}/y]
. Note that the type
(A,\,B)
does not necessarily contain only values – it can be that an element of this type is a stuck term whenever either
A
or
B
may contain a stuck term. In particular, one can see from the definition that the right summand contains all stuck terms of shape
(S,\,N)
where
S
is itself stuck and
N
is arbitrary (may not even be a normal form). Also, it may be that
(A,\,B)
is empty in the case that
A
is empty (e.g. when
A={\mathsf{Top}}^{c}
).

As usual, the type
A\to B
represents those functions that guarantee to map a given term of type
A
into a normal form of type
B
, or diverge in the process. Note that we use the term form of the semantics
[\![A]\!]_{\ast}
for both argument and result since our calling convention is call-by-name (but see also Remark
3
). Note that the type
A\to B
is
never
empty, since it always contains
\lambda x.\,\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{div}}}
– a function that inevitably diverges certainly satisfies the condition on membership.

The semantics of types justifies the following further abbreviations:

\displaystyle\mathsf{Bot}

\displaystyle\coloneqq{\mathsf{Top}}^{c}

\displaystyle\mathsf{Pair}

\displaystyle\coloneqq(\mathsf{Top},\,\mathsf{Top})

\displaystyle\mathsf{PairVal}

\displaystyle\coloneqq(\mathsf{Ok},\,\mathsf{Ok})

\displaystyle\mathsf{Fun}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathsf{Bot}\to\mathsf{Top}

\displaystyle A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B

\displaystyle\coloneqq{A}^{c}\to{B}^{c}

\displaystyle\textstyle\bigwedge_{i=1}^{k}A_{i}

\displaystyle\coloneqq{(\textstyle\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}{A_{i}}^{c})}^{c}

\displaystyle\mathsf{Bool}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{true}\vee\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{false}

Notice that
\mathsf{Pair}
is the type of all normal forms constructed using pairing, and thus includes those pairings that describe stuck terms. By contrast,
\mathsf{PairVal}
is the type of all pair values (pairs whose components are again values). Note also that we are able to construct a type whose interpretation is exactly the set of all functions,
\mathsf{Fun}
, simply as an abbreviation. This would be difficult if our types were restricted to sets of values only, since a function
\lambda x.\,M
, whose
M
immediately goes wrong, would not live inside any type of shape
A\to B
.

{remark}

The type
A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B
, pronounced
A

only to

B
, which was introduced in
(
Ramsay and Walpole 2024
)
, is defined simply as an abbreviation for
{A}^{c}\to{B}^{c}
. This makes sense because the defining condition for
A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B
is “if
M[N/x]
reduces to a normal form in
[\![B]\!]
then
N\in[\![A]\!]_{\ast}
” and this is the contrapositive of the condition “if
N\notin[\![A]\!]_{\ast}
then
M[N/x]\in[\![{B}^{c}]\!]_{\ast}
”. Unfolding the definitions, this latter condition is equivalent to “if
N
reduces to a normal form in
[\![{A}^{c}]\!]
then
M[N/x]\in[\![{B}^{c}]\!]_{\ast}
”. Finally, note that, a basic property of reduction in our language is that any abstraction
\lambda x.\,M
will satisfy this condition iff it satisfies the condition “if
N\in[\![{A}^{c}]\!]_{\ast}
then
M[N/x]\in[\![{B}^{c}]\!]_{\ast}
”. To see this, suppose
\lambda x.\,M
, on input
N
that reduces to an
{A}^{c}
normal form, either diverges or returns a
{B}^{c}
normal form. Then
\lambda x.\,M
, on input
N
which
either diverges or
reduces to an
{A}^{c}
normal form, must still in the end either diverge or return a
{B}^{c}
normal form.

3.1.
Subtype Relation

A key component of the type system is a subtype relation,
A\sqsubseteq B
, which axiomatises some key properties of complement. Later, in Theorem
6.1
, we make precise the idea that the meaning of such an assertion is
[\![A]\!]\subseteq[\![B]\!]
.

\frac{}{A\sqsubseteq A}\quad\textsc{Refl}\frac{}{A\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Top}}\quad\textsc{Top}\frac{}{\mathsf{Int}\vee\mathsf{PairVal}\vee\mathsf{Fun}\vee\mathsf{Atom}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}}\quad\textsc{Ok}\frac{}{A\sqsubseteq{B}^{c}}\quad\textsc{Disj}\;A\mathrel{||}B\frac{}{\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Atom}}\quad\textsc{Atom}\\
\frac{}{({A}^{c},\,\mathsf{Top})\vee(\mathsf{Ok},\,{B}^{c})\sqsubseteq{(A,\,B)}^{c}}\quad\textsc{PairC}\frac{A\sqsubseteq A^{\prime}\quad B\sqsubseteq B^{\prime}}{(A,\,B)\sqsubseteq(A^{\prime},\,B^{\prime})}\quad\textsc{Pair}\frac{A^{\prime}\sqsubseteq A\quad B\sqsubseteq B^{\prime}}{A\to B\sqsubseteq A^{\prime}\to B^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{Fun}\\
\frac{B_{i}\sqsubseteq A\;(\forall i\in[1,k])}{\textstyle\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}B_{i}\sqsubseteq A}\quad\textsc{UnionL}\frac{A\sqsubseteq B_{j}}{A\sqsubseteq\textstyle\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}B_{i}}\quad\textsc{UnionR}\;j\in[1,k]\frac{{B}^{c}\sqsubseteq A}{{A}^{c}\sqsubseteq B}\quad\textsc{CompL}\frac{B\sqsubseteq{A}^{c}}{A\sqsubseteq{B}^{c}}\quad\textsc{CompR}\frac{A\sqsubseteq CC\sqsubseteq B}{A\sqsubseteq B}\quad\textsc{Trans}

Figure 1
.
Subtype relation on types

Definition 3.2 (Subtyping Relations).

The subtyping relation is defined inductively by the rules in
Figure

1
. The relation
A\mathrel{||}B
holds just if
A\neq B
and,
either

A
and
B
are both atoms
or

A,B\in\{\mathsf{Int},\mathsf{Pair},\mathsf{Fun},\mathsf{Atom}\}
.
Define
subtype equivalence
, written
A\mathrel{\sim}B
, just if
A\sqsubseteq B
and
B\sqsubseteq A
. Since the subtype relation is, by definition, reflexive and transitive, it is immediate that
\sim
is an equivalence on types.

The rules
(Refl)
,
(Trans)
,
(Pair)
,
(Fun)
and
(Atom)
are standard. The rule
(Top)
introduces a universal type, and when combined with
(CompL)
, provides the dual notation of the bottom type,
\mathsf{Bot}
, which is a subtype of any other type.

The rule
(Ok)
allows us, together with
(UnionR)
and
(Trans)
, to derive that each of
\mathsf{Int},\mathsf{PairVal},\mathsf{Fun}
and
\mathsf{Atom}
is a subtype of
\mathsf{Ok}
i.e any normal form which is of these types is a value. The rule
(Disj)
expresses the disjointness of certain types using the complement operator, for example
\mathsf{Int}
,
\mathsf{Pair}
,
\mathsf{Fun}
,
\mathsf{Atom}
, as well as distinct atoms
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a},\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{b}
and so on, are provably disjoint from one another . This is extended in
(CompR)
- if
A
is disjoint from
B
, then their complements must be contained in the other. These two rules ensure that our complement operator behaves like an order-reversing involution, up to equivalence.

In the rule
(PairC)
, we have
\mathsf{Ok}
instead of
\mathsf{Top}
in the second pair to mirror the asymmetric definition of
[\![(A,B)]\!]
. It is not necessarily true that
(\mathsf{Top},{B}^{c})\sqsubseteq{(A,B)}^{c}
since, if it was, we would be able to derive for example
(\underline{3}\ (\lambda x.\,x),\,\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{div}}})\in[\![(\mathsf{Top},\,{\mathsf{Int}}^{c})]\!]\subseteq[\![{({\mathsf{Ok}}^{c},\,\mathsf{Int})}^{c}]\!]
, but we know that
(\underline{3}\ (\lambda x.\,x),\,\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{div}}})\in[\![({\mathsf{Ok}}^{c},\,\mathsf{Int})]\!]
.

The rules
(UnionL)
and
(UnionR)
follow naturally from a set theoretic perspective. They explain the union type as a least upper bound on types.

3.2.
Typing Relation

We view the type system as a formal mechanism for establishing the truth of properties of the form “term
M
either diverges, or reduces to a normal form of the type
A
”, written
M:A
.

Definition 3.3 (Typing Formula).

A
typing formula
(or simply
typing
) is an expression of the form
M:A
, where
M
is a term, and
A
is a type. In particular, if
M
is a term variable, i.e. the typing formula has the form
x:A
, we call this a
variable typing
. The free variables of a typing formula
M:A
are just
\mathsf{FV}(M)
. We extend the notion to sets of formulas, writing
\mathsf{FV}(\Delta)
for
\textstyle\bigcup_{M:A\in\Delta}\mathsf{FV}(M)
.

The type system allows for proving sequents of the form
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta
with meaning, informally: if all the formulas in
\Gamma
are true then some formula in
\Delta
is true
3
3

3

We will not formalise this interpretation directly, and instead soundness will be given via progress and preservation theorems.
.

Definition 3.4 (Typing Judgement and Derivability).

A
typing judgement
is an assertion of the form
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta
, where
\Gamma
and
\Delta
are possibly empty, finite sets of typing formulas. A type judgement is said to be
derivable
just if it is possible to construct a proof tree concluding the judgement using the rules of Figure
2
. Excepting the
(Var)
rule, the conclusion of each rule distinguishes a single typing, which is called the
principal formula
of the rule.

The side condition on the
(Match)
rule requires that
\mathsf{PatSubst}(p,\,\theta)
, which holds just if
\theta
is a pattern type substitution with
\textbf{dom}(\theta)=\mathsf{FV}(p)
and, for all types
A
in the range of
\theta
,
A\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}
.

\frac{}{\Gamma,\,x:A\mathrel{\vdash}x:A,\,\Delta}\quad\textsc{Var}\frac{}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:\mathsf{Top},\,\Delta}\quad\textsc{Top}\frac{}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}n:\mathsf{Int},\,\Delta}\quad\textsc{Num}\frac{}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}:\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a},\,\Delta}\quad\textsc{Atom}\\
\frac{\Gamma,\,M:B\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}{\Gamma,\,M:A\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}\quad\textsc{SubL}\;A\sqsubseteq B\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:B,\,\Delta}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:A,\,\Delta}\quad\textsc{SubR}\;B\sqsubseteq A\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:A,\,\Delta}{\Gamma,\,M:{A}^{c}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}\quad\textsc{CompL}\frac{\Gamma,\,M:A\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:{A}^{c},\,\Delta}\quad\textsc{CompR}\\
\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:\mathsf{Int},\,\Delta\quad\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}N:\mathsf{Int},\,\Delta}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M\otimes N:\mathsf{Int},\,\Delta}\quad\textsc{BinOp}\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:\mathsf{Int},\,\Delta\quad\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}N:\mathsf{Int},\,\Delta}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M\Join N:\mathsf{Bool},\,\Delta}\quad\textsc{RelOp}\\
\frac{\Gamma,\,x:A\mathrel{\vdash}M:A,\,\Delta}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\mathsf{fix}\,x.\,M:A,\,\Delta}\quad\textsc{Fix}\;x\notin\mathsf{FV}{(\Gamma,\Delta)}\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:A,\,\Delta\quad\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}N:B,\,\Delta}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}{(M,\,N):(A,\,B)},\,\Delta}\quad\textsc{Pair}\\
\frac{\Gamma,\,x:A\mathrel{\vdash}M:B,\,\Delta}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\lambda x.\,M:A\to B,\,\Delta}\quad\textsc{Abs}\;x\notin\mathsf{FV}{(\Gamma,\Delta)}\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:B\to A,\,\Delta\quad\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}N:B,\,\Delta}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M\,N:A,\,\Delta}\quad\textsc{App}\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:\textstyle\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\theta_{i},\,\Delta\quad\!\!\!\!\Gamma,\theta_{i},\,M:p_{i}\theta_{i}\mathrel{\vdash}N_{i}:A,\,\Delta\;\;(\forall i\in[1,k])}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\mathsf{match}\,M\,\mathsf{with}\,\{\mid_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\mapsto N_{i}\}:A,\,\Delta}\quad\textsc{Match}\;\begin{array}[]{l}\forall i\in[1,k].\>\mathsf{PatSubst}(p_{i},\,\theta_{i})\\
\quad\wedge\ \mathsf{FV}(p_{i})\cap\mathsf{FV}(\Gamma,\Delta)=\varnothing\end{array}\\
\frac{\Gamma,\,M:\mathsf{Int},\,N:\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}{\Gamma,\,M\otimes N:\mathsf{Ok}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}\quad\textsc{OpE}\frac{\Gamma,\,M:\mathsf{Int},\,N:\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}{\Gamma,\,M\Join N:\mathsf{Ok}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}\quad\textsc{RelOpE}\frac{\Gamma,\,M:\mathsf{Fun}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}{\Gamma,\,M\,N:\mathsf{Ok}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}\quad\textsc{AppE}\\
\frac{\Gamma,\,M:\textstyle\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}(p_{i}[\mathsf{Ok}/x\mid x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})])\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}{\Gamma,\,\mathsf{match}\,M\,\mathsf{with}\,\{|_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\mapsto N_{i}\}:\mathsf{Ok}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}\quad\textsc{MatchE}\frac{\Gamma,\,M:\mathsf{Ok},\,N:\mathsf{Ok}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}{\Gamma,\,(M,\,N):\mathsf{Ok}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}\quad\textsc{PairE}

Figure 2
.
Two-Sided Type System.

Two-sided type systems are construed as a kind of sequent calculus for typing formulas. That is, the rules of the proof system can be divided into those that explain how to
verify
a formula of a certain shape on the right-hand side of the sequent, and those that explain how to
refute
a formula of a certain shape on the left.

Since the right rules explain how to verify a typing, they are just the familiar rules of a traditional type system, but generalised slightly to allow for an arbitrary context
\Delta
on the right (though one could instead consider an intuitionistic sequent calculus and drop this generalisation). Therefore we will not explain the standard rules
(Top)
,
(Num)
,
(Atom)
,
(SubR)
,
(BinOp)
,
(RelOp)
,
(Fix)
,
(Pair)
,
(Abs)
and
(App)
.

The Match Rule

In the
(Match)
rule, we abuse notation by considering a pattern-type substitution
\theta
, which is formally a finite map from term variables to types, also as a set of typings
\{x:A\mid\theta(x)=A\}
. The rule allows free choice of a pattern substitution
\theta
, so long as it satisfies the side condition
\mathsf{PatSubst}(p,\,\theta)
. This requires that any type
\theta(x)
chosen as the type of a free variable
x
of pattern
p
provably does not contain any stuck term. This is essential for soundness, because otherwise it could be that the scrutinised term
M
could go wrong even when
\mathrel{\vdash}M:\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\theta_{i}
, for example the judgement
\mathrel{\vdash}\mathsf{match}\,(1,2)\ 3\,\mathsf{with}\,\{x\mapsto 1\}:\mathsf{Int}
would be provable.

\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}{\Gamma,\,M:A\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}\quad\textsc{WeakL}\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:A,\,\Delta}\quad\textsc{WeakR}\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:(A_{1},\,A_{2}),\,\Delta}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\pi_{i}(M):A_{i},\,\Delta}\quad\textsc{Prj$i$}\;A_{1},\,A_{2}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}\frac{\Gamma,\,M:\mathsf{PairVal}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}{\Gamma,\,\pi_{i}(M):\mathsf{Ok}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}\quad\textsc{PrjE$i$}\\
\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:\mathsf{Bool},\,\Delta\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}N:A,\,\Delta\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}P:A,\,\Delta}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\mathsf{if}\,M\,\mathsf{then}\,N\,\mathsf{else}\,P:A,\,\Delta}\quad\textsc{If}\frac{\Gamma,\,M:\mathsf{Bool}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}{\Gamma,\,\mathsf{if}\,M\,\mathsf{then}\,N\,\mathsf{else}\,P:\mathsf{Ok}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}\quad\textsc{IfE}

Figure 3
.
Admissible rules for weakening, projections and conditionals.

To understand the mechanics of the
(Match)
rule, let us first observe that all the rules of Figure
3
are admissible.
Combining
(Match)
and
(WeakL)
, it follows that the less expressive, but perhaps more familiar, one-sided rule for matching is also admissible.

\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:\textstyle\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}\theta_{i}p_{i}\quad\Gamma,\,\theta_{i}\mathrel{\vdash}N_{i}:A\;(\forall i\in[1,k])}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\mathsf{match}\,M\,\mathsf{with}\,\{|_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\mapsto N_{i}\}:A}\quad\textsc{Match${}_{0}$}\;\begin{array}[]{l}\forall i\in[1,k].\>\mathsf{PatSubst}(p_{i},\,\theta_{i})\\
\quad\wedge\ \mathsf{FV}(p_{i})\cap\mathsf{FV}(\Gamma,\Delta)=\varnothing\end{array}

This simpler match rule is sufficient when every case of the match produces a conclusion of the desired type
A
, such as the following instance with
\theta_{1}=\varnothing
,
\theta_{2}=[\mathsf{Int}/n]
and
\Gamma=\{x:(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{data},\,\mathsf{Int})\}
:

\frac{\frac{\frac{}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}x:(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{data},\,\mathsf{Int})}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{left=Var}}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}x:\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{err}\vee(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{data},\,\mathsf{Int})}\quad\textsc{left=SubR}\quad\frac{}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}0:\mathsf{Int}}\quad\textsc{left=Num}\quad\frac{}{\Gamma,\,n:\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\vdash}n:\mathsf{Int}}\quad\textsc{left=Var}}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\mathsf{match}\,x\,\mathsf{with}\,\{\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{err}\mapsto 0\mid(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{data},n)\mapsto n\}:\mathsf{Int}}\quad\textsc{left=Match${}_{0}$}

However, this simpler form of rule is insufficient when there is some match case that produces a result of an incompatible type, yet the case does not apply. For example, if we were to modify the above so that errors are propagated.
Here, we can use the extra left assumption of the second premise in the
(Match)
rule—specifically,
x:\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{err}
— to refute that the case ever occurred.. Writing
A
for the type
(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{data},\,\mathsf{Int})
, we have:

\frac{\frac{\frac{}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}x:A}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{left=Var}}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}x:\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{err}\vee A}\quad\textsc{left=SubR}\quad\frac{\frac{\frac{}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}x:A,\,\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{err}:\mathsf{Int}}\hskip 7.3612pt\textsc{Left=Var}}{\Gamma,\,x:{A}^{c}\mathrel{\vdash}\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{err}:\mathsf{Int}}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{Left=CompL}}{\Gamma,\,x:\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{err}\mathrel{\vdash}\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{err}:\mathsf{Int}}\quad\textsc{left=SubL}\quad\frac{}{\Gamma,\,n:\mathsf{Int},\,x:A\mathrel{\vdash}n:\mathsf{Int}}\quad\textsc{left=Var}}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\mathsf{match}\,x\,\mathsf{with}\,\{\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{err}\mapsto\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{err}\mid(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{data},n)\mapsto n\}:\mathsf{Int}}

In the previous example, the guard expression
x
did not occur in the branches of the match expression. When it does, the
(Match)
rule provides the assumptions
M:p_{i}\theta_{i}
, describing the branch condition, to refine the type of the guard inside the branches. This is essential for typing the following innocent-looking variation, in which we assume only that the guard matches one of the patterns (and that the data is an integer in the second case), i.e.
\Gamma=\{x:\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{err}\vee(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{data},\,\mathsf{Int})\}
, and again writing
A
to abbreviate
(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{data},\,\mathsf{Int})
:

\frac{\frac{}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}x:\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{err}\vee A}\quad\textsc{left=Var}\quad\frac{}{\Gamma,\,x:\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{err}\mathrel{\vdash}0:\mathsf{Int}}\quad\textsc{left=Num}\quad\frac{\frac{}{\Gamma,\,n:\mathsf{Int},\,x:A\mathrel{\vdash}x:A}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{left=Var}}{\Gamma,\,n:\mathsf{Int},\,x:A\mathrel{\vdash}\pi_{2}(x):\mathsf{Int}}\quad\textsc{left=Prj2}}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\mathsf{match}\,x\,\mathsf{with}\,\{\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{err}\mapsto 0\mid(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{data},n)\mapsto\pi_{2}(x)\}:\mathsf{Int}}

This way we can capture properties of the behaviour of matching expressions. For example, the following are all derivable types for
M=\lambda x.\,\mathsf{match}\,x\,\mathsf{with}\,\{\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{err}\mapsto\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{err}\mid(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{data},n)\mapsto n\}
:

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vdash}M

\displaystyle:\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{err}\to\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{err}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vdash}M

\displaystyle:(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{data},\,\mathsf{Int})\to\mathsf{Int}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vdash}M

\displaystyle:\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{err}\vee(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{data},\,\mathsf{Int})\to\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{err}\vee\mathsf{Int}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vdash}M

\displaystyle:{(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{err}\vee(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{data},\,\mathsf{Ok}))}^{c}\to{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}

The bottom-right typing allows us to certainly conclude that the application of this function to a term of type
(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{dat},\,\mathsf{Int})
, that is, where we have misspelled the atom, will not reduce to a value; e.g.

\mathrel{\vdash}(\lambda x.\,\mathsf{match}\,x\,\mathsf{with}\,\{\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{err}\mapsto\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{err}\mid(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{data},n)\mapsto n\})\,(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{dat},\,3):{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}

However, we will look in detail at how the system can be used for refuting that a term reduces to a value in the sequel.

The CompR Rule

The
(CompR)
explains that to verify
M:{A}^{c}
, it suffices to refute
M:A
. This is because, verifying
M\in[\![{A}^{c}]\!]_{\ast}
amounts to showing that
M
either diverges or reduces to a normal form of type
{A}^{c}
, and refuting
M:A
amounts to showing that
M
does not reduce to a normal form of the type
A
. Since every term either diverges or normalises, it is straightforward to see that these are the exact same property.

{remark}

A weakness of Ramsay and Walpole’s work is that a complement operator was not supported by the type system. In particular, as discussed in
(
Ramsay and Walpole 2024
)
, the rule
(CompR)
was not sound, that is, its conclusion does not necessarily follow from its premise. This is because in that work, types are sets of
values
, and therefore the complement of a type
A
are those
values
that are not in the type
A
. Thus it does
not
suffice to refute
M:A
in order to verify
M:{A}^{c}
, e.g. when
M
goes wrong.
Ramsay and Walpole remedied this unsoundness by considering a system with an alternative semantics, inspired by success types
(
Lindahl and Sagonas 2006
)
.
However, this semantics has a fundamental shortcoming – verifying a typing no longer excludes the possibility that it may go wrong, so it is impossible to use this system to show that a given program cannot go wrong. Like success types, one can
only
certify that a program does
not
reduce to a value.

4.
Refutation

\frac{}{\Gamma,\,M:\mathsf{Bot}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}\quad\textsc{BotL}\frac{}{\Gamma,\,n:{\mathsf{Int}}^{c}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}\quad\textsc{NumL}\frac{}{\Gamma,\,\lambda x.\,M:{\mathsf{Fun}}^{c}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}\quad\textsc{FunL}\frac{}{\Gamma,\,\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}:{\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}}^{c}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}\quad\textsc{AtomL}\\
\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:B\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A,\,\Delta\quad\Gamma,\,N:B\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}{\Gamma,\,M\,N:A\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}\quad\textsc{AppL}\frac{\Gamma,\,M:A\mathrel{\vdash}x:A,\,\Delta}{\Gamma,\,\mathsf{fix}\,x.\,M:A\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}\quad\textsc{FixL}\;x\notin\mathsf{FV}(\Gamma,\Delta)\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:{A}^{c},\,\Delta}{\Gamma,\,M:A\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}\quad\textsc{SwapL}\frac{\Gamma,\,M:{A}^{c}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:A,\,\Delta}\quad\textsc{SwapR}\frac{\Gamma,\,M:\mathsf{Bool}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta\Gamma,\,N_{i}:A\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta\;(\forall i)}{\Gamma,\,\mathsf{if}\,M\,\mathsf{then}\,N_{1}\,\mathsf{else}\,N_{2}:A\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}\quad\textsc{IfL}\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:({A}^{c},\,B),\,\Delta}{\Gamma,\,\pi_{1}(M):A\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}\quad\textsc{PrjL1}\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:(\mathsf{Ok},\,{B}^{c}),\,\Delta}{\Gamma,\,\pi_{2}(M):B\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}\quad\textsc{PrjL2}\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:\textstyle\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\theta_{i}\quad\Gamma,\,M:p_{i}\theta_{i},\,N_{i}:A\mathrel{\vdash}{\theta_{i}}^{c},\,\Delta\;\;(\forall i\in[1,k])}{\Gamma,\,\mathsf{match}\,M\,\mathsf{with}\,\{\mid_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\mapsto N_{i}\}:A\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}\quad\textsc{MatchL}\;\mathsf{FV}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k})\cap\mathsf{FV}(\Gamma,\Delta)=\varnothing\\
\frac{}{\Gamma,\,(M,N):{\mathsf{Pair}}^{c}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}\quad\textsc{PairL1}\frac{\Gamma,\,M:A\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}{\Gamma,\,(M,\,N):(A,\,B)\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}\quad\textsc{PairLL}\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:\mathsf{Ok},\,\Delta\Gamma,\,N:B\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}{\Gamma,\,(M,\,N):(A,\,B)\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}\quad\textsc{PairRL}

Figure 4
.
Derivable rules related to refutation.

The most interesting feature of the type system is the ability to deduce consequences of terms reducing to a normal form of a certain type and, ultimately, to refute typing formulas (by deducing absurdity as a consequence).
As expressed by the soundness of the type system (Theorem
7.3
), refuting a typing
M:\mathsf{Ok}
, that is, proving a judgement
M:\mathsf{Ok}\mathrel{\vdash}\
with
M:\mathsf{Ok}
on the left-hand side and nothing on the right, gives a guarantee that
M
will
not
reduce to a value — in other words,
M
either diverges or it goes wrong. Thus, refutation of type assignments provides a mechanism for finding genuine bugs - with no false positives (assuming one counts divergence as a kind of bug).

The
left
rules of the system (those for which the principal formula in the conclusion of the rule is on the left of the turnstile) explain how to refute a typing formula. Using
(OpE)
, one can directly refute that applying an arithmetic operation to an argument that is a function evaluates to a value, or, using
(AppE)
, one can refute that applying a integer as if it were a function would evaluate to a value.

\frac{\frac{\frac{\frac{\frac{}{\underline{3}:\mathsf{Int},\,x:\mathsf{Bot}\mathrel{\vdash}x:\mathsf{Top}}\hskip 7.3612pt\textsc{Left=Top}}{\underline{3}:\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\vdash}\lambda x.\,x:\mathsf{Fun}}\hskip 7.3612pt\textsc{Left=Abs}}{\underline{3}:\mathsf{Int},\,(\lambda x.\,x):{\mathsf{Fun}}^{c}\mathrel{\vdash}}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{Left=CompL}}{\underline{3}:\mathsf{Int},\,(\lambda x.\,x):\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\vdash}}\quad\textsc{Left=SubL}}{\underline{3}\otimes(\lambda x.\,x):\mathsf{Ok}\mathrel{\vdash}}\quad\textsc{left=OpE}\frac{\frac{\frac{\frac{}{\mathrel{\vdash}\underline{3}:\mathsf{Int}}\hskip 7.3612pt\textsc{Left=Num}}{\underline{3}:{\mathsf{Int}}^{c}\mathrel{\vdash}}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{Left=CompL}}{\underline{3}:\mathsf{Fun}\mathrel{\vdash}}\quad\textsc{Left=SubL}}{\underline{3}\,(\underline{1},\,\underline{2}):\mathsf{Ok}\mathrel{\vdash}}\quad\textsc{left=AppE}

Similarly, by
(MatchE)
and
(PairE)
, we can refute reduction to a value for e.g. scrutinizing a term that does not match any pattern, or trying to form a pair where one of the components does not evaluate.

However, in each case, it is the term itself that directly goes wrong. What about if we want to show that, for example, a redex
(\lambda x.\,M)\,N
is contracted successfully, but that something in the body
M[N/x]
will later get stuck; or that a match expression
\mathsf{match}\,\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}\,\mathsf{with}\,\{\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}\mapsto N\mid\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{b}\mapsto P\}
successfully evaluates one of the branches, but that evaluation of the branch gets stuck? For this purpose we introduce the left rules of Figure
4
. However, we will later show that these rules are inherent to the system: each is already derivable from the rules of Figure
2
.

The rule
(BotL)
is dual to
(Top)
, and expresses that one may always refute that a term evaluates to a normal form inside the empty type. The rules
(NumL)
,
(FunL)
,
(AtomL)
and
(PairL1)
express the self-evident facts that one can refute that a normal form lives inside the complement of its natural type.

The
(AppL)
rule gives a principle for refuting an application when the operator is, in fact, known to evaluate to a function. It explains that, to refute that application
M\,N
evaluates to an
A
, we can show that
M
is a function which, to deliver an
A
, necessarily requires a
B
as input, i.e.
M:B\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A
, and then we need only also show that the actual parameter
N
does not evaluate to a
B
. Note that, by the abbreviation,
B\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A
is
{B}^{c}\to{A}^{c}
so it literally states that every input outside of
B
will result in an output outside of
A
, which is equivalent to the contrapositive (see also Remark
3
).

The rule
(FixL)
gives a principle for refuting that a fixpoint
\mathsf{fix}\,x.\,M
evaluates to an
A
. To do so, we need only show that the argument being an
A
is a consequence of the body evaluating to an
A
. If the fixpoint is thought of as the limit of some chain of approximations, then the rule allows for working backwards along the chain, showing that when some better approximation is known to be outside of
A
, then the approximation preceding it was necessarily outside of
A
too.

The rule
(MatchL)
gives a principle for refuting that a match expression
\mathsf{match}\,M\,\mathsf{with}\,\{\mid_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\mapsto N_{i}\}
evaluates to an
A
when control does get passed to the branches because the scrutinee matches some pattern. Note that, for convenience, when
\theta
is used as a set of variable typings, we use the abbreviation
{\theta}^{c}
for the set
\{x:{A}^{c}\mid x:A\in\theta\}
. To justify the refutation, we must show that
M
will evaluate to some value matching a pattern and then, for each branch
i\in[1,k]
, we refute that the branch returned an
A
by either:

•

refuting
N_{i}:A
, thus proving directly that this branch did not return an
A

•

or, refuting
M:p_{i}\theta
, thus proving that this branch could not have been taken

•

or, proving
{\theta_{i}}^{c}
, thus showing that (although the branch may return an
A
) a consequence of the branch returning an
A
is that the assignment of types to the pattern variables is inconsistent with the actual matching
\theta
.

Note that, since
{\theta}^{c}
appears on the right of the judgement, it suffices only to show that
some
pattern variable is necessarily inconsistent with the actual matching.

The rules
(PairLL)
and
(PairRL)
gives the familiar principle that to refute that a pair
(M_{1},\,M_{2})
evaluates to a pair of an
A_{1}
and an
A_{2}
specifically, it suffices to refute that one of the components
M_{i}
evaluates to an
A_{i}
, but to reflect the asymmetric definition of
[\![(A,\,B)]\!]
(see also the discussion on the subtyping rule
(PairC)
in
Section

3.1
), the first component must be a value. The rules
(PrjL
i
)
give a principle for refuting that a projection
\pi_{i}(M)
yields a normal form of some type
A
. In this case, it suffices to show that the pairing will have an incompatible component in position
i
. The rule
(IfL)
allows to refute that a conditional reduces to an
A
by refuting that either branch can reduce to an
A
.

Theorem 4.1.

All the rules of Figure
4
are derivable.

Proof.

First observe that the swapping rules are derivable:

\frac{\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:{A}^{c}\Delta}{\Gamma,\,M:{({A}^{c})}^{c}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}\quad\textsc{left=CompL}}{\Gamma,\,M:A\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}\quad\textsc{left=SubL}\frac{\frac{\Gamma,\,M:{A}^{c}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:{({A}^{c})}^{c},\,\Delta}\quad\textsc{left=CompR}}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:A,\,\Delta}\quad\textsc{left=SubR}

All remaining rules are obtained by reformulating a rule of Figure
2
using
(CompL)
,
(CompR)
,
(SwapL)
and
(SwapR)
, e.g.:

(FixL)
:

Suppose
\Gamma,\,M:A\mathrel{\vdash}x:A,\,\Delta
. Then by
(CompL)
and
(CompR)
,
\Gamma,\,x:{A}^{c}\mathrel{\vdash}M:{A}^{c},\,\Delta
. Then
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\mathsf{fix}\,x.\,M:{A}^{c},\,\Delta
by
(Fix)
, and the result follows from
(SwapL)
.

∎

{remark}

Using the method from the proof of
Theorem

4.1
, we can derive from
(Abs)
a rule for refuting the typing of an abstraction:

\frac{\Gamma,\,M:{B}^{c}\mathrel{\vdash}x:{A}^{c},\,\Delta}{\Gamma,,\lambda x.\,M:{(A\to B)}^{c}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}\quad\textsc{left=AbsL}

This rule is equivalent to the
(CoAbs)
rule from
Figure

5
, which we will discuss further in
Section

5
.

One might also expect a left rule for refuting formulas of shape
M:A\to B
. Semantically, to refute
M:A\to B
requires showing there exists some
N\in[\![A]\!]_{\ast}
such that
M\,N\notin[\![B]\!]_{\ast}
. This suggests a rule like:

\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}N:A,\,\Delta\quad\Gamma,\,M\,N:B\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}{\Gamma,\,{M}:A\to B\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}\quad\textsc{left=Arr-Left}

However, it would violate a nice property of our rules, namely that every term occurring in a premise of the rule is a subterm of a term occurring in the conclusion of the rule. Thus, we exclude such a rule for refuting arrow types.

{remark}

Our type system in Figure
2
contains just one left rule for each shape of stuck term, explaining the conditions under which such a term would get stuck. In contrast, in the two-sided systems of
(
Ramsay and Walpole 2024
)
, there are a large number of primitive left rules, whose choice appears quite arbitrary and lack a convincing justification for their inclusion. For example, the two-sided system for PCF includes a rule
(Dis)
for refuting that a term
M
evaluates to a value of some type by means of verifying that it either diverges or evaluates to a value of some disjoint type, but they choose
not
to include a rule like our derived rule
(PairL1)
. The reason given is that there is much overlap between these rules, for example they can both be used to show
(\underline{1},\,\underline{2}):\mathsf{Nat}\mathrel{\vdash}
. However, they are ultimately incomparable in expressive power,
(Dis)
is applicable even when the term subject
M
is not literally a pair, whereas
(PairL1)
applies even when the subject
(P,\,Q)
may not evaluate to a pair value, for example when
P
or
Q
goes wrong. In our system, however, both rules are derivable so there is no choice to make – the system of Figure
2
inherently contains both reasoning principles already.

Note, there are other differences between the rules of our system and analogous rules in the system of
(
Ramsay and Walpole 2024
)
, which stem from the change to allow the typing of normal forms. For example, the rule
(IfzL2)
of
(
Ramsay and Walpole 2024
)
has an analogue in our
(IfL)
. However, the latter has an additional premise
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:\mathsf{Bool},\,\Delta
. Otherwise judgements such as
\mathsf{if}\ \underline{2}\ \mathsf{then}\ \underline{3}\ \mathsf{else}\ \underline{4}:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}\mathrel{\vdash}
would be derivable, and thus the system would be unsound.

4.1.
Hébert’s Examples

We will demonstrate some of these rules by examining the following sequence of three programs taken from Fred Hébert’s introductory text on Erlang
(
Hébert 2013
)
, where they are used to illustrate the power, and limitations, of success types and the Dialyzer for finding bugs. Although Erlang is a call-by-value language and ours is not, it makes no essential difference to these examples since our pattern matching is strict.

For the purpose of presenting the examples, a let construct is useful. As is well known, this can be defined as an abbreviation
\mathsf{let}\,x=M\,\mathsf{in}\,N
for the redex
(\lambda x.\,N)M
. Thus, it is easy to see that the following typing rules are derivable:

\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:B,\,\Delta\Gamma,\,x:B\mathrel{\vdash}N:A,\,\Delta}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\mathsf{let}\,x=M\,\mathsf{in}\,N:A,\,\Delta}\quad\textsc{Let}\frac{\Gamma,\,N:A\mathrel{\vdash}x:B,\,\Delta\Gamma,\,M:B\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}{\Gamma,\,\mathsf{let}\,x=M\,\mathsf{in}\,N:A\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}\quad\textsc{LetL}

The first of these rules should be familiar. The second explains how to refute that a let reduces to a normal form of type
A
. To do so, we can first derive necessary conditions on
x
, namely that it has some type
B
, in order for the term
N
to reduce to a
A
. Then we need only show that the term
M
whose result is bound by
x

doesn’t
satisfy this necessary type
B
.

The first program simply defines a wrapper around addition and misapplies it to an atom.

\mathsf{let}\,\textit{op}=\lambda xy.\,x+y\,\mathsf{in}\,\textit{op}\,\underline{5}\,\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{you}

First observe that, if applying
op
to
\underline{5}
and
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{you}
were to yield a value, then it must be that
op
is
not
a function that
necessarily
needs it’s second argument to be an integer. We will express this property, that a curried function requires its second argument to be an integer, by the type
\mathsf{Top}\to\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Ok}
. We will let the reasoning by which we chose this type remain a mystery for the moment, except to say that this type represents those functions which, if they are given any first argument, either diverge or return a function that necessarily requires its argument to be an integer (to return a value). We will return to this topic in Section
5
. For brevity, let
\Gamma\coloneqq\{\textit{op}:\mathsf{Top}\to\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Ok}\}
and
\Delta\coloneqq\{\textit{op}:{(\mathsf{Top}\to\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Ok})}^{c}\}
.

\frac{\frac{\frac{\frac{}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\textit{op}:\mathsf{Top}\to\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Ok}}\hskip 7.3612pt\textsc{Left=Var}\frac{}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\underline{5}:\mathsf{Top}}\hskip 7.3612pt\textsc{Left=Top}}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\textit{op}\,\underline{5}:\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Ok}}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{Left=App}}{\mathrel{\vdash}\textit{op}{}\,\underline{5}:\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Ok},\,\Delta}\quad\textsc{Left=CompR}\frac{\frac{}{\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{you}:{(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{you})}^{c}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{Left=AtomL}}{\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{you}:\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}\quad\textsc{left=SubL}}{\textit{op}{}\,\underline{5}\,\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{you}:\mathsf{Ok}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}\quad\textsc{left=AppL}

Next, observe that, by definition,
op

does
necessarily require its second argument to be an integer (in order to return a value), i.e. it is straightforward to derive:
\mathrel{\vdash}\lambda xy.\,x+y:\mathsf{Top}\to\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Ok}
.

Then we can put these two derivations together to refute that this program reduces to a value:

\frac{\textit{op}\,\underline{5}\,\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{you}:\mathsf{Ok}\mathrel{\vdash}\textit{op}:{(\mathsf{Top}\to\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Ok})}^{c}\frac{\mathrel{\vdash}\lambda xy.\,x+y:\mathsf{Top}\to\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Ok}}{\lambda xy.\,x+y:{(\mathsf{Top}\to\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Ok})}^{c}\mathrel{\vdash}}\quad\textsc{left=CompL}}{\mathsf{let}\,\textit{op}=\lambda xy.\,x+y\,\mathsf{in}\,\textit{op}\,\underline{5}\,\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{you}:\mathsf{Ok}\mathrel{\vdash}}\quad\textsc{left=LetL}

The second program in Hébert’s sequence expresses the same problem more indirectly. There are defined functions
count
and
account
used for projecting out the numerical value of the transaction and the account identity respectively, and the transaction record is constructed using
money
. Then, instead of calling
op
directly, the second argument to
op
is itself a call to
account
which, when passed the result of
money
, returns an atom.

⬇

let

money

=

\lambda xy.\,(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{give},(x,y))

in

let

count

=

\lambda x.\,\mathsf{match}\,x\,\mathsf{with}\,\{(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{give},(y,z))\mapsto y\}

in

let

account

=

\lambda x.\,\mathsf{match}\,x\,\mathsf{with}\,\{(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{give},(y,z))\mapsto z\}

in

let

op

=

\lambda xy.\,x+y

in

let

tup

=

\textit{money}\,\underline{5}\,\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{you}

in

op

(
count

tup
)

(
account

tup
)

We observe that, for the body of the innermost let to reduce to a value, it
cannot
be that all of the following are true:

(1)

op
requires its second argument to evaluate to an integer

(2)

and
, for
account
to return an integer, its argument must have evaluated to a value of shape
(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{give},\,(M,\,N))
, with
N
an integer

(3)

and
,
\mathit{tup}
is not of this shape

In other words, if this term evaluates, then one of (i)–(iii) is false. So let
\Delta_{0}
be the following encoding of this situation:

\textit{op}:{(\mathsf{Top}\to\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Ok})}^{c},\,\textit{account}:{((\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{give},(\mathsf{Top},\mathsf{Int}))\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Int})}^{c},\,\mathit{tup}:(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{give},(\mathsf{Top},\mathsf{Int}))

Then, it is straightforward to prove
\textit{op}\,(\textit{count}\,\mathit{tup})\,(\textit{account}\,\mathit{tup}):\mathsf{Ok}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta_{0}
using
(AppL)
,
(App)
,
(CompR)
and
(Var)
.
Moving outwards by one let, we find the definition of
\mathit{tup}
and we observe that for (the definition of)
\mathit{tup}
to evaluate to a tuple of shape
(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{give},\,(M,\,N))
, with
N
an integer, it
cannot
be that:

(1)

For
money
to return a tuple of shape
(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{give},\,(M,\,N))
, with
N
evaluating to an integer, requires its second argument to be an integer.

This is because
money
is not given an integer as second argument in the call in the definition of
\mathit{tup}
.
We can prove this either by working backwards using
(AppL)
or we can exchange the two sides of the judgement using
(SwapL)
and
(CompR)
,
and prove the resulting judgement as we would in a traditional type system:

\frac{\frac{\textit{money}:\mathsf{Top}\to{\mathsf{Int}}^{c}\to{(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{give},\,(\mathsf{Top},\,\mathsf{Int}))}^{c}\mathrel{\vdash}\textit{money}\,\underline{5}\,\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{you}:{(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{give},\,(\mathsf{Top},\,\mathsf{Int}))}^{c}}{\mathrel{\vdash}\textit{money}:{(\mathsf{Top}\to\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{give},\,(\mathsf{Top},\,\mathsf{Int})))}^{c},\textit{money}\,\underline{5}\,\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{you}:{(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{give},\,(\mathsf{Top},\,\mathsf{Int}))}^{c}}\quad\textsc{left=CompR}}{\textit{money}\,\underline{5}\,\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{you}:(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{give},\,(\mathsf{Top},\,\mathsf{Int}))\mathrel{\vdash}\textit{money}:{(\mathsf{Top}\to\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{give},\,(\mathsf{Top},\,\mathsf{Int})))}^{c}}\quad\textsc{left=SwapL}

In either case, we have that, for the innermost let to evaluate, we must have that at least one of (i), (ii) and (iii)’ is false, so let us abbreviate this conclusion by
\Delta_{1}
:

op

\displaystyle:{(\mathsf{Top}\to\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Ok})}^{c}

account

\displaystyle:{((\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{give},(\mathsf{Top},\mathsf{Int}))\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Int})}^{c}

money

\displaystyle:{(\mathsf{Top}\to\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{give},\,(\mathsf{Top},\,\mathsf{Int})))}^{c}

Then we can combine the above derivations, using
(LetL)
and the weakening rule, to prove:

\frac{\textit{op}\,(\textit{count}\,\textit{tup})\,(\textit{account}\,\textit{tup}):\mathsf{Ok}\mathrel{\vdash}\mathit{tup}:(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{give},(\mathsf{Top},\mathsf{Int})),\,\Delta_{1}\textit{money}\,\underline{5}\,\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{you}:(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{give},\,(\mathsf{Top},\,\mathsf{Int}))\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta_{1}}{\mathsf{let}\,\textit{tup}=\textit{money}\,\underline{5}\,\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{you}\,\mathsf{in}\,\textit{op}\,(\textit{count}\,\textit{tup})\,(\textit{account}\,\textit{tup}):\mathsf{Ok}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta_{1}}

This, in turn, becomes the left premise of
(LetL)
for the let expression
\mathsf{let}\,\textit{op}=\lambda xy.\,x+y\,\mathsf{in}\,\ldots
, and so on, and it remains only to show that the various local identifiers
do
satisfy properties (i)–(iii)’, thus contradicting that the program as a whole reaches a value. Let us write
\Delta_{2}
for the environment
\Delta_{1}\setminus\{\textit{op}:{(\mathsf{Top}\to\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Ok})}^{c}\}
, and
P
for
\mathsf{let}\,\textit{tup}=\textit{money}\,\underline{5}\,\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{you}\,\mathsf{in}\,\textit{op}\,(\textit{count}\,\textit{tup})\,(\textit{account}\,\textit{tup})
:

\frac{P:\mathsf{Ok}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta_{1}\frac{\mathrel{\vdash}\lambda xy.\,x+y:\mathsf{Top}\to\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Ok},\,\Delta_{2}}{\lambda xy.\,x+y:{(\mathsf{Top}\to\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Ok})}^{c}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta_{2}}\quad\textsc{left=CompL}}{\mathsf{let}\,\textit{op}=\lambda xy.\,x+y\,\mathsf{in}\,P:\mathsf{Ok}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta_{2}}\quad\textsc{left=LetL}

where the judgement
\mathrel{\vdash}\lambda xy.\,x+y:\mathsf{Top}\to\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Ok}
can be proven as above. Eventually, by this process, we can refute that the program as a whole evaluates.

The final program in Hébert’s sequence is one for which Dialyzer’s automatic type inference fails, but nevertheless will crash at runtime. It is a small modification of the former program where, instead of two distinct functions
count
and
account
, there is a single function
item
whose behaviour is either like
count
or like
account
depending on a switch (the full program can be seen in the introduction to this paper).

⬇

item

=

\lambda x.

match

x

with

\left|\begin{array}[]{lcl}(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{count},\,(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{give},\,(y,\,z)))&\mapsto&y;\\
(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{account},\,(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{give},\,(y,\,z)))&\mapsto&z\end{array}\right.

Since the program has essentially the same structure as the previous, we may adopt the same strategy for refuting that it can reach a value. Here we can deduce that, for the body of the innermost let to have reduced to a value, it
must not
be that all of the following are true:

(1)

op
is a function that requires that its second argument evaluates to an integer

(2)

and,
item
is a function that, to return an integer, requires an input that does not evaluate to a value of shape
(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{account},\,(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{give},\,(P,\,Q)))
, with
Q
not an integer

(3)

and,
tup
does evaluate to a value of shape
(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{give},\,(P,\,Q))
with
Q
not an integer.

As previously, our argument proceeds by showing that, in fact,
all
of (i)–(iii) do in fact hold, contradicting the assumption that the program did evaluate.
The approach is similar to the previous two examples, but of particular interest is the change from separate
count
and
account
to a single function
item
. However, using our sophisticated
(MatchL)
rule we can prove (ii):

\mathrel{\vdash}\lambda x.\,\mathsf{match}\,x\,\mathsf{with}\,\{\ldots\}:{(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{account},\,(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{give},\,(\mathsf{Ok},\,{\mathsf{Int}}^{c}\wedge\mathsf{Ok})))}^{c}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Int}

Note: we have to use
{\mathsf{Int}}^{c}\wedge\mathsf{Ok}
to ensure that the type
(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{account},\,(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{give},\,(\mathsf{Ok},\,{\mathsf{Int}}^{c}\wedge\mathsf{Ok})))
is a subtype of
\mathsf{Ok}
, according to the side-condition on the rule.
To do so, we assume that the body of the function evaluates to an integer and show that the argument
x
therefore did not match the
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{account}
branch without
z
being an integer. Of course, we could also show that it follows that
x
did not match the
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{count}
branch without
y
being an integer, but this is not relevant to the overall argument. By
(CompR)
, it suffices to assume that
x

does
match the
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{account}
branch without
z
being an integer, and then use
(MatchL)
to refute that the match expression evaluated to an integer.

x:(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{account},\,(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{give},\,(\mathsf{Ok},\,{\mathsf{Int}}^{c}\wedge\mathsf{Ok}))),\,\mathsf{match}\,x\,\mathsf{with}\,\{\ldots\}:\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\vdash}

By taking
\theta_{1}=[{\mathsf{Int}}^{c}\wedge\mathsf{Ok}/y,\,\mathsf{Ok}/z]
and
\theta_{2}=[\mathsf{Ok}/y,\,{\mathsf{Int}}^{c}\wedge\mathsf{Ok}/z]
, this assumption on
x
, let us write
\Delta_{x}
for short, ensures that we can prove that the match expression did evaluate some branch:

\Delta_{x}\mathrel{\vdash}x:(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{count},\,(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{give},\,({\mathsf{Int}}^{c}\wedge\mathsf{Ok},\,\mathsf{Ok})))\vee(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{account},\,(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{give},\,(\mathsf{Ok},\,{\mathsf{Int}}^{c}\wedge\mathsf{Ok})))

Then it is straightforward to refute that some branch returned an integer, by showing that in each of the two branches, returning an integer is inconsistent with this assignment to the free pattern variables
y
and
z
(i.e. that
{\theta_{i}}^{c}
follows in each case). For example, in the second branch:

\frac{\frac{}{x:(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{account},\,(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{give},\,(\mathsf{Ok},\,{\mathsf{Int}}^{c}\wedge\mathsf{Ok}))),\,z:\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\vdash}y:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c},\,z:\mathsf{Int}}\quad\textsc{left=Var}}{x:(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{account},\,(\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{give},\,(\mathsf{Ok},\,{\mathsf{Int}}^{c}\wedge\mathsf{Ok}))),\,z:\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\vdash}y:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c},\,z:{({\mathsf{Int}}^{c}\wedge\mathsf{Ok})}^{c}}\quad\textsc{left=Sub}

Having derived this type for
item
, the rest of the argument follows in the same way as the two previous examples, so that overall, we can refute that the program reduces to a value.

{remark}

\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{account}
in the input and the atom
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{you}
in the output has been lost.
However, its analysis can be guided by extending the example with the following “overloaded type specification”, which explicitly enumerates the dependencies between the two calls and their return values, and then it can certify the bug.
