5.
The CoArrow Type

In the discussion of Hébert’s first example, we used the type
\mathsf{Top}\to\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Ok}
to assert the property that a curried function requires its second argument to be an integer. This works because the type mixes the two kinds of arrows in a specific way: it is the type of those functions that, if you give them any first argument will either diverge or return a function that requires an integer in order to return a value.

However, it is not clear there is a general pattern to forming assertions of the form “is a curried function which, to return an
A
, requires that its
n^{\text{th}}
argument is a
B
” using the necessity arrow. For example,
neither
of the following two types express that, to return a value, the first argument of a two-argument curried function must be an integer:

\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Top}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Ok}

This is the type of functions that, if they return a function of type
\mathsf{Top}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Ok}
, their argument must have been an integer. Hence, this property holds of any function that does not return a function, such as
\lambda x.\,\underline{1}
.

\mathsf{Int}\to\mathsf{Top}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Ok}

This is the type of functions that, if they are given an integer as the first argument, guarantee to return a function, about which we have no further guarantees (if the function returns a value, then it’s argument must have had type
\mathsf{Top}
, which is no information at all). An example is
\lambda xy.\,\underline{1}
.

By unfolding the abbreviations, one can see that such a property
could
be expressed by the type
{\mathsf{Int}}^{c}\to\mathsf{Top}\to{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}
. However, it is not at all clear how to use this type in conjunction with the
(AppL)
rule.

The problem is that the necessity arrow does not curry well. Apart from the difficulties illustrated above, this can be seen clearly in the proof theory. When we type a curried abstraction using the traditional arrow, the two uses of the
(Abs)
rule follow directly on one another, shown below left.

\frac{\frac{x:A,\,y:B\mathrel{\vdash}M:C}{x:A\mathrel{\vdash}\lambda y.\,M:B\to C}\quad\textsc{Left=Abs}}{\mathrel{\vdash}\lambda xy.\,M:A\to B\to C}\quad\textsc{left=Abs}\frac{\lambda y.\,M:B\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}C\mathrel{\vdash}x:A}{\mathrel{\vdash}\lambda xy.\,M:A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}C}\quad\textsc{left=Abn}

In the conclusion of the rule, the typing formula is of shape
\lambda z.\,P:A_{1}\to A_{2}
and occurs on the right of the judgement and in the premise we have a formula of exactly the same shape, also occurring on the right of the judgement. However, this is not the case for the abstraction rule for the necessity function type
(Abn)
(which was included in
(
Ramsay and Walpole 2024
)
as a primitive rule, but is easy to derive in our system), shown above on the right.
This rule doesn’t apply to the judgement
\lambda y.\,M:B\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}C\mathrel{\vdash}x:A
. Indeed, we can only proceed by subsumption on the left, which is not helpful.

\frac{\Gamma,\,M:A\mathrel{\vdash}x:B,\,\Delta}{\Gamma,\,\lambda x.\,M:B\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$/\penalty$\cr$\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}$\crcr}}}}A\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}\quad\textsc{CoAbs}\frac{\Gamma,\,M:B\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$/\penalty$\cr$\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}$\crcr}}}}A\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta\Gamma,\,N:B\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}{\Gamma,\,M\,N:A\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}\quad\textsc{CoApp}

Figure 5
.
Derivable abstraction and application rules
a

la
bi-intuitionistic logic.

Although the necessity arrow
A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B
appears natural, it is not, on its own, quite the right concept through which to properly understand the property that a function requires a certain property of its argument. In fact, a better choice is to use the
complement
of the necessity arrow.

At first this may seem strange, because the complement of the necessity arrow,
{(A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B)}^{c}
, is hardly a function type at all. This type, viewed as a set under Definition
3.1
, contains integers and pairs and so on - even stuck terms belong to this type! However,
refuting
that a term has this type amounts to showing that the term is a function that requires an
A
as input if it is to return a
B
. Moreover, we can chain this type to describe the properties of curried functions in a meaningful way. Let us call this function type the
coarrow
, or
coto
for short. Let us also introduce the abbreviation:
A\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$/\penalty$\cr$\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}$\crcr}}}}B\coloneqq{(A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B)}^{c}
. Then the typing rule
(CoAbs)
of Figure
5
is derivable.
Using the coarrow, we can type curried abstractions as easily as we type them with the traditional arrow type, because of the implicit negation that surrounds the type of the returned function.

This derivation also helps to clarify the meaning of a type of shape
A\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$/\penalty$\cr$\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}$\crcr}}}}B\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$/\penalty$\cr$\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}$\crcr}}}}C
. We can
refute
that a curried function
\lambda xy.\,M
satisfies this type whenever we can show that
M
evaluating to a
C
implies that
either

x
must have been an
A

or

y
must have been a
B
. In particular, by using
\mathsf{Bot}
, we can therefore state
the negation of
properties of the form desired above. For example:

\mathsf{Bot}\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$/\penalty$\cr$\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}$\crcr}}}}\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$/\penalty$\cr$\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}$\crcr}}}}\mathsf{Ok}

This is the type of normal forms that are
not
functions that, to return a value, require their second argument to be an integer. It is easy to see, by unfolding the abbreviations, that it is subtype equivalent to the complement of the type
\mathsf{Top}\to\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Ok}
, which we used in Hébert’s first example.

\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$/\penalty$\cr$\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}$\crcr}}}}\mathsf{Bot}\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$/\penalty$\cr$\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}$\crcr}}}}\mathsf{Ok}

This is the type of normal forms that are
not
functions that, to return a value, require their first argument to be an integer. Unfolding the abbreviations, this type is easily seen to be subtype equivalent to the complement of that we suggested above:
{\mathsf{Int}}^{c}\to\mathsf{Top}\to{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}
.

Refuting that a term has one of these types, therefore, verifies that it satisfies the corresponding property of functions. Moreover, the
(CoApp)
variant of the
(AppL)
rule, shown in Figure
5
, is easily derivable.

Hopefully the reader can agree that the proof theory works out quite smoothly (see also the examples below), but the nature of this type is so unusual – the ‘function type’
\mathsf{Top}\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$/\penalty$\cr$\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}$\crcr}}}}\mathsf{Bot}
doesn’t contain any functions – that one might want some independent confirmation that it is really a natural choice.

In fact, the logical counterpart to this type is the key feature in the extension of intuitionistic logic to bi-intuitionistic logic (originally called Heyting-Brouwer logic
(
Rauszer 1974
)
, and sometimes subtractive logic
(
Crolard 2001
)
). As is well known, the usual function type
A\to B
is related to the propositional implication,
A
implies
B
, by the Curry-Howard Correspondence. Propositional implication has a natural dual, which is variously called co-implication, subtraction or pseudo-difference. Where implication can be characterised by the adjunction below left (whose forward direction corresponds to the usual
(Abs)
rule), co-implication can be defined by the below right
4
4

4

Some authors prefer to define coimplication rather as the converse to this relation, i.e. with the
A
and
B
exchanged, see the second footnote of
(
Tranchini 2017
)
.
, whose correspondence with our
(CoAbs)
rule is immediately clear.

A\wedge B\mathrel{\vdash}C\quad\text{iff}\quad A\mathrel{\vdash}B\to CB\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$/\penalty$\cr$\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}$\crcr}}}}A\mathrel{\vdash}C\quad\text{iff}\quad A\mathrel{\vdash}B\vee C

Example 5.1.

The following example is a good illustration of how to use the coarrow. Observe that we can refute that the
twice
function,
\lambda fx.\,f\,(f\,x)
, can be assigned the type
(\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$/\penalty$\cr$\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}$\crcr}}}}\mathsf{Int})\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$/\penalty$\cr$\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}$\crcr}}}}\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$/\penalty$\cr$\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}$\crcr}}}}\mathsf{Int}
. Writing
\Delta
for
\{x:\mathsf{Int},\,f:\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$/\penalty$\cr$\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}$\crcr}}}}\mathsf{Int}\}
, we have the derivation:

\frac{\frac{\frac{\frac{}{f:\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$/\penalty$\cr$\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}$\crcr}}}}\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}\hskip 7.3612pt\textsc{Left=Var}\frac{\frac{}{f:\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$/\penalty$\cr$\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}$\crcr}}}}\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}\hskip 7.3612pt\textsc{Left=Var}\frac{}{x:\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}\hskip 7.3612pt\textsc{Left=Var}}{f\,x:\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}\hskip 7.3612pt\textsc{left=CoApp}}{f\,(f\,x):\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{Left=CoApp}}{\lambda x.\,f\,(f\,x):\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$/\penalty$\cr$\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}$\crcr}}}}\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\vdash}f:\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$/\penalty$\cr$\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}$\crcr}}}}\mathsf{Int}}\quad\textsc{Left=CoAbs}}{\lambda fx.\,f\,(f\,x):(\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$/\penalty$\cr$\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}$\crcr}}}}\mathsf{Int})\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$/\penalty$\cr$\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}$\crcr}}}}\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$/\penalty$\cr$\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}$\crcr}}}}\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\vdash}}\quad\textsc{left=CoAbs}

From the conclusion of this refutation, we are assured that, if the twice function returns an integer, it must be that
either
:

(1)

its second argument was an integer

(2)

or
, its first argument was not a function that required an integer in order to return one

Another way to read this implication is that, if the twice function returns an integer and its second argument was
not
an integer, then it must be that its first argument didn’t require an integer in order to return one.

Moreover, it is straightforward to show that
\lambda x.\,x+\underline{2}
is a function that, to return an integer, requires an integer as input. That is, we can
refute
that it has type
\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$/\penalty$\cr$\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}$\crcr}}}}\mathsf{Int}
. Therefore, we can prove that
(\lambda fx.\,f\,(f\,x))\,(\lambda x.\,x+\underline{2})
really requires an integer in order to evaluate to an integer. That is, we have the following refutation:

\frac{\lambda fx.\,f\,(f\,x):(\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$/\penalty$\cr$\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}$\crcr}}}}\mathsf{Int})\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$/\penalty$\cr$\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}$\crcr}}}}\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$/\penalty$\cr$\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}$\crcr}}}}\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\vdash}\lambda x.\,x+\underline{2}:\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$/\penalty$\cr$\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}$\crcr}}}}\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\vdash}}{(\lambda fx.\,f\,(f\,x))\,(\lambda x.\,x+\underline{2}):\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$/\penalty$\cr$\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}$\crcr}}}}\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\vdash}}\quad\textsc{left=CoApp}

6.
Metatheory of Subtyping

\frac{}{A\leq A}\quad\textsc{Refl}\frac{}{A\leq\mathsf{Top}}\quad\textsc{Top}\frac{A\leq B^{\prime}}{(A,B)\leq(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})}\quad\textsc{Pair}\frac{A^{\prime}\leq AB\leq B^{\prime}}{A\to B\leq A^{\prime}\to B^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{Fun}\frac{A\leq B_{j}}{A\leq\textstyle\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}B_{i}}\quad\textsc{UnionR}\;j\in[1,k]\frac{B_{i}\leq A\;(\forall i\in[1,k])}{\textstyle\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}B_{i}\leq A}\quad\textsc{UnionL}\frac{}{{\mathsf{Top}}^{c}\leq B}\quad\textsc{CompLTop}\frac{{A}^{c}\leq{A^{\prime}}^{c}{B}^{c}\leq{B^{\prime}}^{c}}{{(A,B)}^{c}\leq{(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})}^{c}}\quad\textsc{CompLPair}\frac{{A^{\prime}}^{c}\leq{A}^{c}{B}^{c}\leq{B^{\prime}}^{c}}{{(A\to A}{{(\textstyle\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}B_{i})}^{c}\leq A}\quad\textsc{CompLUn}\;j\in[1,k]\frac{A\leq B}{{({A}^{c})}^{c}\leq B}\quad\textsc{CompLC}

Figure 6
.
Alternative rules for subtyping.

The rules of the subtype relation are natural from our understanding of types as certain sets of normal forms, so it is not surprising that these sets form a model of the theory, as revealed by
Theorem

6.1
. The contrapositive of the theorem allows us to use the set-theoretic model to exclude that certain subtype inequalities are derivable. For example, we cannot have
A\to B\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Int}
for any types
A
and
B
, nor can we derive
A\to B\sqsubseteq{(B\to C)}^{c}
for any
B
and
C
, since the set on the left always contains
\lambda x.\,\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{div}}}
but the set on the right does not.

Theorem 6.1 (Soundness of Subtyping).

If
A\sqsubseteq B
then
[\![A]\!]\subseteq[\![B]\!]
.

However the axiomatisation itself is not straightforward to analyse because of the unrestricted transitivity rule and the general rules regarding complementation. Yet it is essential to understand certain invariants of this relation to properly argue the soundness of the type system. For example, for soundness, we require that whenever
A\to B\sqsubseteq C\to D
is derivable, then it must be that already
C\sqsubseteq A
and
B\sqsubseteq D
. If this inequality is derived using the
(Fun)
rule, then all is well, but as usual the unrestricted transitivity rule allows for other more convoluted proofs. For example, we can prove
\mathsf{Int}\to\mathsf{Int}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Int}\to\mathsf{Ok}
using transitivity with the mid-point
{(\textstyle\bigvee_{i=1}^{2}{({{\mathsf{Int}}^{c}}^{c}\to(\mathsf{Int}\vee\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}\vee\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{b_{i}}))}^{c})}^{c}
. Clearly, there is no need to invoke transitivity at all in the proof of this inequality, but even supposing we decide to invoke transitivity, this chosen mid-point is excessively complicated – through a combination of the union and complementation rules it can be seen equivalent to the simpler
\mathsf{Int}\to(\mathsf{Int}\vee\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a})
.

Thus we are led to present an alternative characterisation of the subtyping relation in which the space of possible proofs is significantly more constrained, although, as we shall see, the provable inequalities are the same. The rules of Figure
6
are obtained from the the rules of the original subtyping relation by ‘baking in’ transitivity, and enforcing a more principled use of the
(CompL)
and
(CompR)
rules.

Definition 6.2 (Alternative Subtyping).

The alternative subtyping relation
A\leq B
is defined by the rules of Figure
6
. Here
\mathcal{B}
abbreviates the set
\{\mathsf{Int},\,\mathsf{PairVal},\,\mathsf{Fun},\,\mathsf{Atom}\}
.

The motivation for restricting the complementation rules is as follows. When constructing a proof of
A\sqsubseteq{B}^{c}
, bottom-up, one may sometimes find a choice between using disjointness
(Disj)
,
(PairC)
or the
(CompR)
rule. If the
(CompR)
rule is chosen, then there are many choices with how to conclude the subproof of
B\sqsubseteq{A}^{c}
. For example, one may use disjointness with
A
, or some rule that exploits the structure of
B
, or even using
(CompR)
again to uselessly return to the original conclusion. In our alternative system of Figure
6
, we split
(CompR)
into a large number of cases, and we restrict that proofs should follow a particular strategy based on the syntax of
B
. In particular, in most cases when the form of the judgement
A\sqsubseteq{B}^{c}
would call for a use of
(CompR)
in the original system, the alternative system will insist that this should be followed by some rule exploiting the structure of
B
, followed by a use of
(CompL)
to ‘undo’ the rearrangement. For example, the rule
(CompRUn)
of the alternative system corresponds to the following derivation in the original:

\frac{\frac{\frac{A\sqsubseteq{B_{1}}^{c}}{B_{1}\sqsubseteq{A}^{c}}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{left=CompL}\cdots\frac{A\sqsubseteq{B_{k}}^{c}}{B_{k}\sqsubseteq{A}^{c}}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{left=CompL}}{\textstyle\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}B_{i}\sqsubseteq{A}^{c}}\quad\textsc{Left=UnionL,vcenter}}{A\sqsubseteq{(\textstyle\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}B_{i})}^{c}}\quad\textsc{left=CompR}

The alternative characterisation of subtyping makes it much easier to prove the invariants we need in order to establish the soundness of the type system. For example, a brief inspection of the rules reveals that
(Fun)
is now the
only
way to derive
A\to B\leq C\to D
. In particular, there is no transitivity rule and the complementation rules are restricted by the syntax of the complemented type. However, it is straightforward to show that the original rules are admissible, and then equivalence with the original system follows by induction.

Theorem 6.3 (Equivalence of Subtyping Relations).

A\sqsubseteq B
iff
A\leq B

As well as making it easier to establish certain invariants, the alternative characterisation gives us a straightforward approach to argue its decidability.

Theorem 6.4 (Decidability).

The problem, given
A
and
B
determine derivability of
A\sqsubseteq B
, is decidable.

Proof.

Let
A
and
B
be types given as input. By the equivalence theorem, it suffices to decide
A\leq B
. Let
\mathsf{SubExp}(A)
be the set of all subexpressions
5
5

5

As usual, we consider
A
to be a subexpression of itself.
of the type
A
and
\mathsf{SubExp}(B)
be the set of all subexpressions of the type
B
. Then consider the following set of types:

\mathcal{A}_{A,B}\coloneqq\mathsf{SubExp}(A)\cup\mathsf{SubExp}(B)\cup\{\mathsf{Top},\,\mathsf{Int},\,\mathsf{Pair},\,\mathsf{PairVal},\,\mathsf{Fun},\,\mathsf{Atom},\,\mathsf{Ok}\}\\

This set consists of all the finitely many types that occur as subexpressions of the input types
A
and
B
as well as certain fixed types such as
\mathsf{Top}
,
\mathsf{Int}
and so on. Then define the following finite set of types:

\mathcal{U}_{A,B}\coloneqq\mathcal{A}_{A,B}\cup\{D^{c}\mid D\in\mathcal{A}_{A,B}\}

One can see, by inspection of the alternative subtyping rules in Figure
6
, that whenever a rule instance is concluded by some inequality
C\leq D
, and
C
and
D
are in
\mathcal{U}_{A,B}
, then every type occurring in the premises of the rule instance are also contained in
\mathcal{U}_{A,B}
. Hence, in any proof of
A\leq B
it must be that every subproof is concluded by an inequality of shape
C\leq D
, where both
C
and
D
are in
\mathcal{U}_{A,B}
, of which there are only finitely many. Consequently, a bottom-up proof search that avoids cycles will decide
A\leq B
.
∎

7.
Metatheory of Type System

\frac{\Gamma,\,x:A\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}x:{A}^{c},\,\Delta}\quad\textsc{Comp}\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:\textstyle\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\theta_{i},\,\Delta\quad\Gamma,\,\theta_{i}\mathrel{\vdash}M:{(p_{i}\theta_{i})}^{c},\,N_{i}:A,\,\Delta\;\;(\forall i)}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\mathsf{match}\,M\,\mathsf{with}\,\{\mid_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\mapsto N_{i}\}:A,\,\Delta}\quad\textsc{Match}\\
\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:{\mathsf{Int}}^{c},\,N:{\mathsf{Int}}^{c},\,\Delta}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M\Join N:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c},\,\Delta}\quad\textsc{OpE}\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:{\mathsf{Int}}^{c},\,N:{\mathsf{Int}}^{c},\,\Delta}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M\otimes N:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c},\,\Delta}\quad\textsc{RelOpE}\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c},\,N:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c},\,\Delta}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}(M,N):{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c},\,\Delta}\quad\textsc{PairE}\\
\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:{\mathsf{Fun}}^{c},\,\Delta}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\,M\,N:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c},\,\Delta}\quad\textsc{AppE}\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:{(\textstyle\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})])}^{c},\,\Delta}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\mathsf{match}\,M\,\mathsf{with}\,\{|_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\mapsto N_{i}\}:{{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}},\,\Delta}\quad\textsc{MatchE}

Figure 7
.
One-sided typing rules (differences).

For developing the metatheory of the type system, we first simplify the situation. Two-sided type systems have arbitrary typing formulas on the left- and right-hand sides of the judgement. However, we show that it is possible to reformulate the type system so that arbitrary typings are needed only on the right (though we may still need any number of them).

Definition 7.1 (One-Sided System).

Judgements of the one-sided system have shape
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta
in which every typing in
\Gamma
is a variable typing, of shape
x:A
. The rules are those of the two-sided system but with
(SubL)
removed, and
(CompL)
,
(Match)
,
(OpE)
,
(RelOpE)
,
(PairR)
,
(AppE)
and
(MatchE)
replaced by the rules of Figure
7
.

The idea is as follows. It is easy to see that, the one-sided and two-sided versions of
(Match)
,
(OpE)
,
(RelOpE)
,
(PairE)
,
(AppE)
and
(MatchE)
are inter-derivable in the two-sided system by bracketing rule uses with
(CompL)
and
(CompR)
. Therefore, one can suppose that the original, two-sided system uses rules in
Figure

7
instead. In that case, a short inspection of the remaining two-sided rules shows that the only way to conclude a typing on the left-hand side is by
(SubL)
or
(CompL)
, and thus the latter must eventually be used. This implies that to refute
M:A
, we must have already verified
M:B
on the right, for some
B
such that
A\sqsubseteq{B}^{c}
. Therefore, every refutation
M:A
corresponds to a verification of some
M:B
, for which we did not need any left rules. Formally, the proof is by induction.

Theorem 7.2 (Equivalence).

Let us write
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}_{2}\Delta
for provability in the two-sided system and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}_{1}\Delta
for provability in the one-sided system. Then both of the following:

(i) If
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}_{2}\Delta
then
\mathrel{\vdash}_{1}{\Gamma}^{c},\,\Delta
.   (ii) If
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}_{1}\Delta
then
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}_{2}\Delta
.

Note: a judgement like
x:A,\,x:{A}^{c}\mathrel{\vdash}
, although provable in the two-sided system is
not
provable in the one-sided system. On the other hand, the theorem shows that the “equivalent” judgement
\mathrel{\vdash}x:A,\,x:{A}^{c}

is
provable in the one-sided system. The asymmetry stems from the fact that the one-sided rules have
(Comp)
that may faithfully simulate
(CompR)
on variable typings, but no rule to faithfully simulate
(CompL)
on variable typings.

This is a useful simplification, but there still remains the problem of multiple conclusions on the right. This is a significant barrier to a standard progress and preservation style argument for the soundness of the type system. Consider progress for a judgement of shape
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:\mathsf{Ok},\,\Delta
. When
\Gamma
and
\Delta
are arbitrary, and not only variable typings, it is not true that
M
in normal form will be a value. This is the case in, for example, the judgement:

\mathrel{\vdash}\underline{2}\,(\lambda z.\,z):\mathsf{Ok},\,\lambda x.\,x:\mathsf{Int}\to\mathsf{Int}

This judgement expresses the property:
either

\underline{2}\,(\lambda z.\,z)
diverges or yields an integer value,
or

\lambda x.\,x
is an integer function. Since the second conclusion is true, the truth of the judgement as a whole simply does not depend on the drivel in the first conclusion, and so we cannot expect to be able to rely on anything about it.

{remark}

In fact, this highlights a deficiency in the original work. Due to this difficulty, progress and preservation in
(
Ramsay and Walpole 2024
)
was proven only for judgements satisfying the
single-subject constraint
, that is, in which at most one term in the judgement is not a variable. Thus, they establish soundness only for fragments of their two-sided type systems.

Here, rule
(App)
is applied at the root of the derivation tree to obtain
\underline{2}\,(\lambda z.\,z)
. However, the validity of the judgement does not rely on the validity of
\underline{2}\,(\lambda z.\,z):\mathsf{Ok}
, but the validity of
\lambda x.\,x
. For example, we can have a smaller proof where
\underline{2}\,(\lambda z.\,z)
in untouched.

For preservation, although it is true that
Q\,\ \mathord{\rhd}\,\ Q^{\prime}
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}Q:A,\,\Delta
implies
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}Q^{\prime}:A,\,\Delta
holds unconditionally, the proof is not straightforward. In a typical preservation proof, we take the typing derivation for the term
to be
reduced (
Q
), and adjust it to a typing derivation for the term
after
reduction(
Q
). For a single-succedent system, a derivation that assigns a type to
Q
is always decided by
Q
itself. When the term reduces, we can construct a derivation of
Q^{\prime}
by using the subderivation that types the (perhaps reduced) subterm of
Q
. However, in a multi-succedent system, the derivation of
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}Q:A,\Delta
might be completely independent from the term
Q
. Consider the derivation:

\frac{\frac{\frac{}{x:\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\vdash}\underline{2}:\mathsf{Ok}\to\mathsf{Ok},\,x:\mathsf{Int}}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{Left=Var}}{\mathrel{\vdash}\underline{2}:\mathsf{Ok}\to\mathsf{Ok},\,\lambda x.\,x:\mathsf{Int}\to\mathsf{Int}}\quad\textsc{Left=Abs}\frac{\frac{}{x:\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\vdash}x:\mathsf{Int},\,\underline{3}:\mathsf{Ok}}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{Left=Var}}{\mathrel{\vdash}\underline{2}:\mathsf{Ok},\,\lambda x.\,x:\mathsf{Int}\to\mathsf{Int}}\quad\textsc{left=Abs}}{\mathrel{\vdash}\underline{2}\ \underline{3}:\mathsf{Ok},\,\lambda x.\,x:\mathsf{Int}\to\mathsf{Int}}\quad\textsc{left=App}

In such cases, reconstructing a derivation for
Q^{\prime}
becomes problematic because the subderivations might bear no relation to
Q
. To address this problem, we need a way to identify which conclusion formulas are
relevant
to the proof.

However, this task is far from straightforward. Vertically, the
principal
(or
focused
) term can shift arbitrarily among conclusions at each inference step, introducing redundancy at varying levels. Horizontally, the relevance can interfere among terms: a typing assignment might be only meaningful when another typing assignment in the same conclusion is meaningful. Moreover, a typing judgement may also have many different derivations, each with different relevant formula in their conclusion, for example when, in
\mathrel{\vdash}M:A,\,N:B
, both
\mathrel{\vdash}M:A
and
\mathrel{\vdash}N:B
are independently provable. This reflects a deeper issue: the typing system lacks an internal mechanism to track the semantic justification (i.e., the root cause) of a typing judgment.

To bridge the gap between derivations and their semantics, we introduce an additional precondition
\Gamma\nvdash\Delta
, meaning
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta
is not derivable
, for the preservation proof. That is, we show
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}Q:A,\,\Delta
implies
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}Q^{\prime}:A,\,\Delta
whenever
\Gamma\nvdash\Delta
. This allows us to prove preservation via standard induction on typing rules just as in a single-succedent (traditional) type system, without delving into the complexities of tracking stepwise dependencies across all possible derivations. Finally, we demonstrate that this assumption can be eliminated, restoring the standard preservation theorem without additional premises. This approach provides a purely syntactic method to handle irrelevance in multi-succedent systems, bypassing the need for explicit relevance constraints.

Theorem 7.3 (Progress and Preservation).

Suppose all terms in the following are closed:

Preservation:

Suppose
Q\,\ \mathord{\rhd}\,\ Q^{\prime}
. If
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}Q:A,\,\Delta
, then
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}Q^{\prime}:A,\,\Delta
.

Progress:

If
\ \mathrel{\vdash}\Delta
, then there is some formula
M:A\in\Delta
such that, if
M
is a normal form, then:

•

A\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}
implies
M
is a value

•

and,
A\sqsubseteq{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}
implies
M
is stuck.

A corollary of this theorem is that
M:\mathsf{Ok}\mathrel{\vdash}
implies that
M
either diverges or gets stuck, since
\mathrel{\vdash}M:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}
follows from this judgement by
(CompR)
.

Two properties of our system distinguish its progress theorem from conventional formulations. The first its the multi-succedent nature. Unlike preservation, which applies uniformly to all conclusion terms, progress only guarantees that some term in the conclusion satisfies the progress statement. This reflects a reliance on the underlying semantics that justify why the statement holds. The second is its ability to type normal forms in general. In standard systems, progress rules out non-value normal forms (i.e., stuck terms) by appealing to canonical forms of values, ensuring all non-values are reducible. By contrast, our system does not exclude non-value normal forms. Instead, our progress theorem adopts a different perspective: a normal form of the type
{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}
is guaranteed to go wrong, and a normal form a value type is guranteed to not go wrong. This motivated the revealing of another property of our system: any normal-form cannot be given both a type and its complement.

Theorem 7.4 (Normal Form Cut Admissibility).

If
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}U:A,\,\Delta
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}U:{A}^{c},\,\Delta
, then
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta

From progress and preservation we obtain the soundness of the type system, that is, if
\mathrel{\vdash}M:\mathsf{Ok}
then
M
cannot go wrong – it either diverges or reduces to a value – and if
\mathrel{\vdash}M:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}
, then
M
cannot evaluate – it either diverges or goes wrong. As is typical, the system is not
complete
, that is, there exist closed terms
M
for which neither
\mathrel{\vdash}M:\mathsf{Ok}
, nor
\mathrel{\vdash}M:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}
. An example is
\mathsf{if}\ \underline{2}<\underline{3}\ \mathsf{then}\ \underline{1}\ \mathsf{else}\ (\underline{1}\ \underline{1})
, for which deducing a type of
\mathsf{Ok}
would need more precise information about the behaviour of the relation
<
in the guard than is available in the system.

However, a remarkable fact about our system is that it
is
complete for
M
in normal form. Given that the type system is designed to allow us to prove properties
A
of the reduction behaviour of terms
M
, expressed as
M:A
, it seems quite basic that we should want that the system is, at the very least, capable of proving properties of terms in normal form, whose reduction behaviour is completely trivial. However, this basic property is not satisfied by most traditional type systems, even for values! A function value whose body happens to be a stuck term, e.g.
\lambda x.\,\underline{2}\,x
, is not usually typable, since the standard
(Abs)
rule would require that we can type the stuck term
\underline{2}\,x
.

Theorem 7.5 (Complete Classification of Normal Forms).

For all normal forms
U
, both of the following are true:

(i) If
U
is a value then
\ \mathrel{\vdash}U:\mathsf{Ok}
.   (ii) If
U
is stuck then
\ \mathrel{\vdash}U:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}

For values, this is perhaps not so interesting, because we are very familiar with the typing of values. For stuck terms, it is more illuminating, because here it is not so clear
a priori
that we have the “right” set of typing and subtyping rules.
Consider a stuck term of shape
\mathsf{match}\,V\,\mathsf{with}\,\{\mid_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\mapsto N_{i}\}
, which is stuck by virtue of the fact that value
V
doesn’t match any of the patterns
p_{i}
. Completeness requires that we can certify that this term is stuck by a derivation. To do this, say in the one-sided system (which is equivalent to the two-sided system), there is essentially no choice but to use
(MatchE)
, and hence it must be shown that: from
\forall i.\ \forall\sigma.\ V\neq p_{i}\sigma
it follows that
\mathrel{\vdash}V:{(\textstyle\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}[\mathsf{Ok}/x\mid x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})])}^{c}
. This latter judgement is the single premise of (MatchE). In other words, Theorem 7.6 reveals, amongst other things, that pattern (non-)matching of
V
, i.e. the condition
\forall i.\ \forall\sigma.\ V\neq p_{i}\sigma
can be captured precisely by the type system.

8.
Conclusion and Related Work

We introduced a new, two-sided type system that overcomes some significant weaknesses of the systems in
(
Ramsay and Walpole 2024
)
. The system supports a wide range of refutation principles, including a type-theoretic of the coimplication from bi-intuitionistic logic. These were put to use certifying that a number of Erlang-like programs go wrong. The system is sound and, in a sense, complete for normal forms.

Type inference

We did not consider type inference, but we can observe that, barring the
(Comp)
and
(SubR)
rules, in all of the rules of the one-sided system the terms in the premises are strictly smaller than those in the conclusion. Hence, the system naturally suggests a standard approach in which subsumption is first absorbed into the other rules, and then a notion of canonical derivation(s) defined, in which fresh type variables are introduced at synthesis points and constrained according to subtyping. However, since the rules are not syntax directed – one may freely choose between typings on the right – there is some challenge to make such a scheme work efficiently.

8.1.
Related Work

Two-sided type systems

Of course, the most closely related work is the two-sided type systems of
(
Ramsay and Walpole 2024
)
, on which our work is based. Compared to the systems in that paper, our system overcomes the three weaknesses listed in the introduction and in more detail in Remarks
3.2
,
4
and
7
. Thanks to the notion of relevance and the cut theorem for normal forms, we were able to prove soundness for all judgements, and not only those that satisfy the single-subject constraint. Our system contains both
(CompL)
and
(CompR)
and yet still enjoys both
well-typed programs cannot go wrong
and
ill-typed programs cannot evaluate
. Our system is made from a small number of well-motivated rules, with the key left rules of
(
Ramsay and Walpole 2024
)
all derivable.
Moreover, our
(Match)
rule exploits the two-sidedness of the system and the general soundness result. However, we don’t consider type inference.

By encoding atoms as unary constructors, the constrained type system of
(
Ramsay and Walpole 2024
, Section 6)
can handle the first two of Héberts examples, but not the third one. The third example is out of reach because, when typing an expression of shape
\mathsf{match}\,x\,\mathsf{with}\,\{\mid_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\mapsto M_{i}\}
, a type
A
must be given that can be refuted for both of the branches simultaneously, since there is no capability to refute that a variable
x
in the scrutinee can match any particular pattern.

The system of
(
Ramsay and Walpole 2024
, Section 6)
could be extended with such a capability by adding e.g. an axiomatisation of the notion of type disjointness in the subtype system. This way, assuming a type
x:A
would be sufficient to refute an assignment
x:B\mathrel{\vdash}
whenever
A
and
B
disjoint, which could be used to ignore a case in the match and thus derive a typing expressing the dependency of just a single branch. Currently, the system of
(
Ramsay and Walpole 2024
, Section 6)
only allows reasoning about type disjointness for concrete values, but not for variables. With this extension, their system would be able to verify the third example.

Typing for incorrectness.

One half of our system, and the part we have concentrated on in this paper, has as its purpose to reason about incorrectness by refuting type assignments. Probably the best known type system for reasoning about incorrectness is Lindahl and Sagonas’
success types
, which have been popularised in the Erlang community through the
Dialyzer
tool
(
Lindahl and Sagonas 2006
)
.
As argued by Sagonas in his Erlang’21 keynote, the Dialyzer tool has been very successful
(
Sagonas 2021
)
. It is part of the standard Erlang platform OTP
(
Erlang/OTP 28 2025
)
, and is widely used by the community. Yet, outside of this community (and those of Erlang’s sibling languages), type systems for finding bugs, that is for reasoning about incorrectness, are largely unknown. One may wonder why the single most popular developer tool for Erlang programmers
(
Nagy and Nagyné Víg 2008
)
, has not been replicated elsewhere, for example through success typing for other dynamically typed programming languages? Or why there is no parallel to the substantial and varied research that we see into new and more powerful type systems for correctness? One reason could be that there lacks a unified foundation for typing for (in)correctness, and we hope that the present work can contribute there.

Alternative type systems for incorrectness, include work by
Jakob and Thiemann 2015
, who attempt to characterise success types as a kind of refutation in a system in which one constructs a logical formula to describe inputs that guarantee to crash a function, and the
coverage types
of
(
Zhou et al. 2023
)
, which embed “must-style”, underapproximate reasoning designed for checking the coverage of generators in property-based testing.

There are also works in which types are used as a language for properties, but the verification that a program satisfies such a property is is based on the semantics directly, rather than via a proof (type) system. For example,
Carrott et al. 2025
and
Qian et al. 2024
, both concern type safety bugs. However, rather than deriving a proof in a type system, they instead reduce the problem to deciding a reachability problem concerning a transformation of the program. Both works show the effectiveness of solving their reachability problem by symbolic execution.

Logics for incorrectness.

Incorrectness logic of
O’Hearn 2019
has sparked a new interest in systems for reasoning about programs that go wrong. Subsequent work has extended its scope to languages with concurrency
(
Raad et al. 2022
)
, demonstrated its real-world effectiveness
(
Le et al. 2022
)
and spawned alternatives such as the
Outcome Logic
of
Zilberstein et al. 2023
, which additionally supports reasoning about correctness. A key feature of these logics is
under-approximation
as a means to achieve true positives, and this allows for a more specific notion of ‘positive’, which excludes divergence. In incorrectness logic, states in a postcondition must be
reachable
. This leads to the need to reason about termination, but it also allows for an analysis to dynamically drop disjunctions in order to scale well. The foundation of our system is not under-approximation, but necessity (via complement) – when
M:A
is provable, it remains the case that the type
A
is an
over-approximation
of
M
. This makes the system closer to the works of
Cousot et al. 2011
;
Cousot et al. 2013
and
Mackay et al. 2022
. The former considers the problem of inferring
necessary preconditions
in order to detect contract violations that will lead to assertion failures. The latter uses necessity as a means to specify the
robustness
of module specifications in object-oriented programming.

Better type errors for debugging.

One application of our system is to provide more precise type errors.
Webbers et al. 2024
improve the type error messages from refinement type checking and inference. For each non-provable judgment, an explicit “refutation” is produced to explain why the judgement is not provable, but it does not necessarily imply something about the behaviour of the subject (e.g. that it will crash at runtime).

Type systems with complement.

A small number of systems in the literature are equipped with a complement, or negation type operator. However, to the best of our knowledge, none allow for reasoning about incorrectness by proving that programs go wrong.
A complement operator appears in the system of
Aiken et al. 1994
and a negation in the work of
Parreaux and Chau 2022
. Both systems are more sophisticated than ours, with the latter giving a treatment of a realistic programming language and a detailed and insightful account of type inference – in fact this is the main motivation for the complement in both of these papers. A complement operator is also present in the line of work on set-theoretic types and semantic subtyping (see e.g.
(
Castagna and Xu 2011
;
Castagna et al. 2022
;
Castagna 2024
)
) in which the subtyping relation is defined directly as inclusion of the induced sets. A much simpler system is that of
(
Pearce 2013
)
, which lacks function types, and axiomatises a subtyping relation that is shown complete with respect to a set theoretic model. Finally, in the dependent type system of
Unno et al. 2017
, no typable program can go wrong, but the system is so expressive that one can compute directly the complements of arbitrary types without the need for a formal operator.

Coimplication and bi-intuitionistic logic.

Bi-intuitionistic logic extends intuitionistic logic with a coimplication (also called subtraction, or pseudo-difference) operator which is dual to implication. It was first studied by
Rauszer 1974
, but the cut-elimination result was later shown to be flawed by
Pinto and Uustalu 2009
. A version of the Curry-Howard correspondence has been given for a version of bi-intuitionistic logic by
Crolard 2004
, but it is for a natural deduction system, and is in the style of Parigot’s
\lambda\mu
-calculus, with negation via continuations rather than as an explicit refutation. Coimplication is at the heart of the work of
Choudhury and Gay 2025
, though in the context of language design. Whereas our paper views a type as a property, they are interested in designing a calculus that corresponding to the logical content of the type. In particular, rather than viewing coimplication as a property of functions, they design a calculus in which the proofs of coimplications have their own term construction, the coabstraction.

Appendix A
Supplementary Materials Related to Subtyping

This appendix contains proofs of:

•

The fact that complement is an order-reversing involution.

•

The soundness of the subtyping relation (Theorem
6.1
).

•

The equivalence of the original and alternative characterisations of subtyping (Theorem
6.3
).

A.1.
Proof of complement as an order-reversing involution

We justify the claim that complement acts as an order-reversing involution.

Lemma A.1.

For all
A
and
B
:
{{A}^{c}}^{c}\mathrel{\sim}A
and, if
A\sqsubseteq B
, then
{B}^{c}\sqsubseteq{A}^{c}
.

Proof.

For the first part, we show that
{{A}^{c}}^{c}\sqsubseteq A
and
A\sqsubseteq{{A}^{c}}^{c}
:

\frac{\frac{}{{A}^{c}\sqsubseteq{A}^{c}}\quad\textsc{Left=Refl}}{{{A}^{c}}^{c}\sqsubseteq A}\quad\textsc{Left=CompL}\frac{\frac{}{{A}^{c}\sqsubseteq{A}^{c}}\quad\textsc{Left=Refl}}{A\sqsubseteq{{A}^{c}}^{c}}\quad\textsc{Left=CompR}

For the second part, assume
A\sqsubseteq B
. Then, since
B\sqsubseteq{{B}^{c}}^{c}
,

\frac{\frac{A\sqsubseteq BB\sqsubseteq{{B}^{c}}^{c}}{A\sqsubseteq{{B}^{c}}^{c}}\quad\textsc{Left=Trans}}{{B}^{c}\sqsubseteq{A}^{c}}\quad\textsc{Left=CompR}

∎

A.2.
Proof of Subtyping Soundness, Theorem
6.1

Theorem
6.1
:

If
A\sqsubseteq B
then
[\![A]\!]\subseteq[\![B]\!]
.

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of
A\sqsubseteq B
.

(Refl)
:

Immediate.

(Top)
:

Immediate from the definition of
\mathsf{Top}
as the set of all closed normal forms.

(Ok)
:

Suppose
\mathsf{Int}\vee\mathsf{PairVal}\vee\mathsf{Fun}\vee\mathsf{Atom}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}
. Then
[\![\mathsf{Int}\vee\mathsf{PairVal}\vee\mathsf{Fun}\vee\mathsf{Atom}]\!]=[\![\mathsf{Int}]\!]\cup[\![\mathsf{PairVal}]\!]\cup[\![\mathsf{Fun}]\!]\cup[\![\mathsf{Atom}]\!]
. Now, we just need to check that each of these four sets are subsets of
[\![\mathsf{Ok}]\!]
i.e they are sets of values, which they are by the definition of the grammar of this language.

(Disj)
:

Suppose
A\sqsubseteq{B}^{c}
and
A\neq B,\,A\mathrel{||}B
.
If
A
and
B
are atoms, then let
[\![A]\!]=\{\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}\}
and
[\![B]\!]=\{\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{b}\}
where
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}\neq\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{b}
. Then
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}\in[\![\mathsf{Top}]\!]
so
\{\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}\}\subseteq[\![\mathsf{Top}]\!]\setminus\{\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{b}\}
as required.
If
A,B\in\{\mathsf{Int},\mathsf{Pair},\mathsf{Fun},\mathsf{Atom}\}
, by definition of
[​[]​]
for these types, these sets are pairwise disjoint. Thus, for each pair of types,
[\![A]\!]\subseteq[\![{B}^{c}]\!]
.

(Atom)
:

Suppose
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Atom}
. Then
[\![\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}]\!]=\{\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}\}\subseteq[\![\mathsf{Atom}]\!]
.

(PairC)
:

Suppose
({A}^{c},\,\mathsf{Top})\vee(\mathsf{Ok},\,{B}^{c})\sqsubseteq{(A,\,B)}^{c}
.
Then, by definition
[\![({A}^{c},\,\mathsf{Top})\vee(\mathsf{Ok},\,{B}^{c})]\!]=[\![({A}^{c},\,\mathsf{Top})]\!]\cup[\![(\mathsf{Ok},\,{B}^{c})]\!]
,
where we have

(1)

\displaystyle[\![({A}^{c},\,\mathsf{Top})]\!]

\displaystyle=\{(M,\,N)\mid M\in[\![{A}^{c}]\!]\cap[\![\mathsf{Ok}]\!],N\in[\![\mathsf{Top}]\!]\}\cup\{(M,\,N)\mid M\in[\![{A}^{c}]\!]\cap[\![{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}]\!],N\in[\![\mathsf{Top}]\!]_{\ast}\}

and, since
[\![\mathsf{Ok}]\!]\cap[\![{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}]\!]=\varnothing
,

(2)

\displaystyle[\![(\mathsf{Ok},\,{B}^{c})]\!]

\displaystyle=\{(P,\,Q)\mid P\in[\![\mathsf{Ok}]\!],Q\in[\![{B}^{c}]\!]\}\cup\{(P,\,Q)\mid P\in[\![\mathsf{Ok}]\!]\cap[\![{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}]\!],Q\in[\![{B}^{c}]\!]_{\ast}\}

(3)

\displaystyle=\{(P,\,Q)\mid P\in[\![\mathsf{Ok}]\!],Q\in[\![{B}^{c}]\!]_{\ast}\}

Let
N=(R,S)\in[\![({A}^{c},\,\mathsf{Top})\vee(\mathsf{Ok},\,{B}^{c})]\!]
.
Then either

(1):

R\in[\![{A}^{c}]\!]\cap[\![\mathsf{Ok}]\!]

or

(2):

R\in[\![{A}^{c}]\!]\cap[\![{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}]\!]

or

(3):

S\in[\![{B}^{c}]\!]_{\ast}
.

In any case, this means
N\notin[\![(A,\,B)]\!]
.
Since
N\in[\![\mathsf{Top}]\!]
, we have
N\in[\![\mathsf{Top}]\!]\setminus[\![(A,\,B)]\!]=[\![{(A,\,B)}^{c}]\!]
.

(Pair)
:

Suppose
(A,B)\sqsubseteq(A^{\prime}\,B^{\prime})
. The induction hypotheses give:

(1):

[\![A]\!]\subseteq[\![A^{\prime}]\!]

(2):

[\![B]\!]\subseteq[\![B^{\prime}]\!]

Note that by definition of
[\![]\!]_{\ast}
, for types
A
and
B
, if
[\![A]\!]\subseteq[\![B]\!]
, then
[\![A]\!]_{\ast}\subseteq[\![B]\!]_{\ast}
also.
Then, since set intersection is monotonic,

(4)

\displaystyle[\![(A,\,B)]\!]

\displaystyle=\{(M,\,N)\mid M\in[\![A]\!]\cap[\![\mathsf{Ok}]\!],N\in[\![B]\!]\}\cup\{(M,\,N)\mid M\in[\![A]\!]\cap[\![{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}]\!],N\in[\![B]\!]_{\ast}\}

(5)

\displaystyle\subseteq\{(M,\,N)\mid M\in[\![A^{\prime}]\!]\cap[\![\mathsf{Ok}]\!],N\in[\![B^{\prime}]\!]\}\cup\{(M,\,N)\mid M\in[\![A^{\prime}]\!]\cap[\![{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}]\!],N\in[\![B^{\prime}]\!]_{\ast}\}

(6)

\displaystyle=[\![(A^{\prime},\,B^{\prime})]\!]

(Fun)
:

Suppose
A\to B\sqsubseteq A^{\prime}\to B^{\prime}
. The induction hypotheses give:

(1):

[\![A^{\prime}]\!]\subseteq[\![A]\!]

(2):

[\![B]\!]\subseteq[\![B^{\prime}]\!]

Again, by definition of
[\![]\!]_{\ast}
, by (IH1) we have
[\![A^{\prime}]\!]_{\ast}\subseteq[\![A]\!]_{\ast}
. Similarly, by (IH2) we have
[\![B]\!]_{\ast}\subseteq[\![B^{\prime}]\!]_{\ast}
. Then,
[\![A\to B]\!]=\{\lambda x.\,M\mid\forall N\in[\![A]\!]_{\ast}.\>M[N/x]\in[\![B]\!]_{\ast}\}\subseteq\{\lambda x.\,M\mid\forall N\in[\![A^{\prime}]\!]_{\ast}.\>M[N/x]\in[\![B^{\prime}]\!]_{\ast}\}=[\![A^{\prime}\to B^{\prime}]\!]
.

(UnionL)
:

Suppose
{\textstyle\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}B_{i}}\sqsubseteq A
. The induction hypothesis gives:

(1):

[\![B_{j}]\!]\subseteq[\![A]\!](\forall j\in[1,k])

Then, by basic properties of set inclusion,
[\![\textstyle\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}B_{i}]\!]=\textstyle\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}[\![B_{i}]\!]\subseteq[\![A]\!]
.

(UnionR)
:

Suppose
A\sqsubseteq{\textstyle\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}B_{i}}
. The induction hypothesis gives:

(1):

[\![A]\!]\subseteq[\![B_{j}]\!],j\in[1,k]

Then, by basic properties of set inclusion,
[\![A]\!]\subseteq[\![B_{j}]\!]\subseteq\textstyle\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}[\![B_{i}]\!]=[\![\textstyle\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}B_{i}]\!]
.

(CompL)
:

Suppose
{A}^{c}\sqsubseteq B
. The induction hypothesis gives:

(1):

[\![{B}^{c}]\!]\subseteq[\![A]\!]

Let
N\in[\![{A}^{c}]\!]=[\![\mathsf{Top}]\!]\setminus[\![A]\!]
. By definition, this means
N\notin[\![A]\!]
. By the induction hypothesis
[\![{B}^{c}]\!]=[\![\mathsf{Top}]\!]\setminus[\![B]\!]\subseteq[\![A]\!]
, we must also have that
N\notin[\![\mathsf{Top}]\!]\setminus[\![B]\!]
. By
(Top)
,
N\in[\![\mathsf{Top}]\!]
, so we must have
N\in[\![B]\!]
.

(CompR)
:

Suppose
A\sqsubseteq{B}^{c}
. The induction hypothesis gives:

(1):

[\![B]\!]\subseteq[\![{A}^{c}]\!]

Let
N\in[\![A]\!]
. By
(Top)
,
N\in[\![\mathsf{Top}]\!]
.
By the induction hypothesis
[\![B]\!]\subseteq[\![\mathsf{Top}]\!]\setminus[\![A]\!]
and that
N\in[\![A]\!]
, we must have
N\notin[\![B]\!]
.
Thus,
N\in[\![\mathsf{Top}]\!]\setminus[\![B]\!]=[\![{B}^{c}]\!]
as required.

(Trans)
:

Suppose
A\sqsubseteq B
. The induction hypotheses give:

(1):

[\![A]\!]\subseteq[\![C]\!]

(2):

[\![C]\!]\subseteq[\![B]\!]

Then, by transitivity of set inclusion,
[\![A]\!]\subseteq[\![C]\!]\subseteq[\![B]\!]
.

∎

A.3.
Proof of Equivalence of the Subtyping Systems, Theorem
6.3

We begin by showing that the alternative characterisation admits transitivity.

Lemma A.2 (Admissibility of Transitivity).

If
A\leq B
and
B\leq C
then
A\leq C
.

Proof.

The proof is by strong induction on the sum of the heights of the two derivations in the antecedent. We analyse cases on the rules that can be used to conclude two judgements of the required form.

To begin, we note the following. Whenever the judgement on the left is concluded using a rule whose conclusion admits an arbitrary type on the RHS, then
A\leq C
can be deduced no matter which rule was used to justify the judgement on the right.

(Refl)
:

In this case,
A=B
and hence the second premise is already a proof of
A\leq C
.

(UnionL)
:

In this case,
A
is of shape
\textstyle\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}A_{i}^{\prime}
and we may assume smaller proofs of
A_{i}^{\prime}\leq B
for all
i\in[1,k]
. Then it follows from the induction hypothesis that there are proofs of
A_{i}^{\prime}\leq C
for all
i\in[1,k]
and thus the result follows by
(UnionL)
.

(CompLTop)
:

In this case,
A
is of shape
\mathsf{Bot}
and the result follows immediately by
(CompLTop)
.

(CompLOk)
:

In this case,
A={\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}
and we may assume a smaller proof of
D\sqsubseteq B
where
D
is one of the types allowed by the side condition. Then it follows from the induction hypothesis that
D\sqsubseteq C
and so the result follows by
(CompLOk)
.

(CompLUn)
:

In this case,
A
is of shape
{(\textstyle\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}A_{i}^{\prime})}^{c}
and we may assume a smaller proof of
A_{j}\leq B
for some
j
. Then it follows from the induction hypothesis that
A_{j}\leq C
and the conclusion follows from
(CompLUn)
.

(CompLC)
:

In this case,
A
is of shape
{{A^{\prime}}^{c}}^{c}
and we may assume a smaller proof of
A^{\prime}\leq B
. It follows from the induction hypothesis that
A^{\prime}\leq C
from which the result follows by
(CompLC)
.

We note similarly that, whenever the judgement on the right is obtained using one of the rules that admits an arbitrary type on left, then
A\leq C
can be deduced independently of how the judgement on the left is justified.

(Refl)
:

In this case
B=C
and so the first premise is actually a proof of
A\leq C
.

(Top)
:

In this case,
C=\mathsf{Top}
and and the result follows immediately from another application of
(Top)
.

(OkR)
:

In this case
C=\mathsf{Ok}
and we may assume some type
D
of those allowed by the side condition, and a smaller proof of
B\leq D
. It follows from the induction hypothesis that
A\leq D
and thus the result follows by
(OkR)
.

(CompRInt)
,
(CompRPair)
,
(CompRArr)
,
(CompRAtoms)
,
(CompRAtom2)
:

These cases follow by the same argument to the previous.

(CompRUn)
:

In this case,
C
has shape
{(\textstyle\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}C_{i})}^{c}
and we may assume a smaller proof of
B\sqsubseteq{C_{i}}^{c}
for each
i
. Then it follows from the induction hypothesis that
A\leq{C_{i}}^{c}
for each
i
, and the result follows by
(CompRUn)
.

(CompRC)
:

In this case,
C
has shape
{{C^{\prime}}^{c}}^{c}
and we may assume a smaller proof of
B\leq C^{\prime}
. Then it follows from the induction hypothesis that
A\leq C^{\prime}
and the result follows by
(CompRC)
.

(UnionR)
:

In this case,
C
has shape
\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}C_{i}
and we may assume a smaller proof of
B\sqsubseteq C_{j}
for some
j
. Then it follows from the induction hypothesis that
A\sqsubseteq C_{j}
and the result follows by
(UnionR)
.

Having discharged all those combinations where the rule for the left premise had an arbitrary type
B
on the RHS in the conclusion and the rule justifying the right premise has an arbitrary type
B
on the LHS in the conclusion, all that remains to consider those combinations of rules that require agreement of a specific syntactic shape for the pivot type
B
.

(CompLAtoms / CompRAtoms)
:

In this case,
B
is of shape
{\mathsf{Atom}}^{c}
and
C
is of shape
{\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}}^{c}
we may assume some type
D
allowed by the side condition and a smaller proof of
A\leq D
. Then
A\leq{\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}}^{c}
follows from
(CompRAtom2)
.

(CompRPair)
/
(CompLPair)
:

In this case,
B
is of shape
{(B^{\prime},C^{\prime})}^{c}
and
C
is of shape
{(B^{\prime\prime},C^{\prime\prime})}^{c}
and we may assume some type
D
as allowed by the side condition on the left rule, such that
A\sqsubseteq D
. Then the result follows immediately by
(CompRPair)
.

(CompRPairL)
/
(CompLPair)
:

In this case,
A
is of shape
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
,
B
is of shape
{(C^{\prime},D^{\prime})}^{c}
and
C
is of shape
{(C^{\prime\prime},D^{\prime\prime})}^{c}
and we may assume smaller proofs of
A^{\prime}\leq{C^{\prime}}^{c}
and
{C^{\prime}}^{c}\leq{C^{\prime\prime}}^{c}
and
{D^{\prime}}^{c}\leq{D^{\prime\prime}}^{c}
. Then it follows from the induction hypothesis that
A^{\prime}\leq{C^{\prime\prime}}^{c}
and the result follows by
(CompRPairL)
.

(CompRPairR)
/
(CompLPair)
:

This case is symmetrical to the previous.

(Pair)
/
(CompRPairL)
:

In this case,
A
has shape
(A_{1},A_{2})
,
B
has shape
(B_{1},B_{2})
,
C
has shape
{(C_{1},C_{2})}^{c}
and we may assume smaller proofs of
A_{1}\leq B_{1}
,
A_{2}\leq B_{2}
and
B_{1}\leq{C_{1}}^{c}
. Then it follows from the induction hypothesis that
A_{1}\leq{C_{1}}^{c}
and the result follows from
(CompRPairL)
.

(Pair)
/
(CompRPairR)
:

This case is symmetrical to the previous.

(CompRArr)
/
(CompLArr)
:

In this case,
B
is of shape
{(B_{1}\to B_{2})}^{c}
and
C
is of shape
{(B_{1}^{\prime}\to B_{2}^{\prime})}^{c}
and we may assume some type
D
of those allowed by the side condition of the first rule, and a smaller proof of
A\sqsubseteq D
. Then the result follows immediately by
(CompRArr)
.

(CompRUn)
/
(CompLUn)
:

In this case,
B
is of shape
{(\textstyle\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}B_{i})}^{c}
and we may assume smaller proofs of
A\leq B_{i}
for each
i\in[1,k]
and
B_{j}\leq C
for some
j\in[1,k]
. It follows from the induction hypothesis that
A\sqsubseteq C
, as required.

(CompRC)
/
(CompLC)
:

In this case,
B
is of shape
{{B^{\prime}}^{c}}^{c}
and we may assume smaller proofs of
A\sqsubseteq B^{\prime}
and
B^{\prime}\sqsubseteq C
from which the result follows by the induction hypothesis.

(Pair)
/
(Pair)
:

In this case,
A
is of shape
(A_{1},A_{2})
,
B
is of shape
(B_{1},B_{2})
,
C
is of shape
(C_{1},C_{2})
and we may assume smaller proofs of
A_{1}\leq B_{1}
,
A_{2}\leq B_{2}
,
B_{1}\leq C_{1}
and
B_{2}\leq C_{2}
. It follows from the induction hypothesis that
A_{1}\leq C_{1}
and
A_{2}\leq C_{2}
and so the result follows from
(Pair)
.

(Fun)
/
(Fun)
:

This case is analogous to the previous.

(CompLPair)
/
(CompLPair)
:

This case is analogous to the previous.

(CompLArr)
/
(CompLArr)
:

This case is analogous to the previous.

(UnionR)
/
(UnionL)
:

In this case,
B
has shape
\textstyle\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}B_{i}
and we may assume smaller proofs of
A\leq B_{j}
for some
j\in[1,k]
and
B_{i}\sqsubseteq C
for all
i\in[1,k]
. Therefore, the result follows directly from the induction hypothesis.

∎

We can now see that the complement of the alternative characterisation is an order involution in the following sense.

Lemma A.3.

If
A\leq{{B}^{c}}^{c}
or
{{A}^{c}}^{c}\leq B
then
A\leq B

Proof.

Suppose
A\leq{{B}^{c}}^{c}
(the other case is analogous). Then we have
B\leq B
by reflexivity and hence
{{B}^{c}}^{c}\leq B
by
(CompLC)
. Then the result follows by transitivity.
∎

We next show that the complement operator in the alternative characterisation is also order-reversing.

Lemma A.4.

If
A\leq B
then
{B}^{c}\leq{A}^{c}
.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on the derivation of
A\leq B
.

(Refl)
:

In this case the result is immediate.

(Top)
:

In this case, the result follows immediately by
(CompLTop)
.

(OkR)
:

In this case,
B=\mathsf{Ok}
. It follows from the induction hypothesis that there is some type
C
as required by the side condition, such that
{C}^{c}\leq{A}^{c}
and hence the result follows by
(CompLOk)
.

(Atom)
:

In this case,
A
is of shape
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}
and
B=\mathsf{Atom}
. Then, since
{\mathsf{Atom}}^{c}\leq{\mathsf{Atom}}^{c}
by
(Refl)
,
{\mathsf{Atom}}^{c}\leq{\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}}^{c}
by
(CompRAtom2)
.

(CompRAtom1)
:

In this case,
A
is of shape
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{b}
and
B
of shape
{\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}}^{c}
and we may assume
a\neq b
. Then also by
(CompRAtom1)
,
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}\leq{\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{b}}^{c}
and the result follows by
(CompLC)
.

(CompRInt)
,
(CompRPair)
,
(CompRArr)
,
(CompRAtoms)
,
(CompRAtom2)
:

These cases are similar, so we will only argue the first. In this case,
B={\mathsf{Int}}^{c}
and it follows from the induction hypothesis that there is some type
D
of those allowed by the side condition, such that
{D}^{c}\sqsubseteq{A}^{c}
. Whichever type
D
was chosen, it follows that
\mathsf{Int}\leq{D}^{c}
by one of the rules in this case. Therefore, by transitivity,
\mathsf{Int}\leq{A}^{c}
and
{{\mathsf{Int}}^{c}}^{c}\leq{A}^{c}
by
(CompLC)
.

(CompRPairL)
,
(CompRPairR)
:

We will just argue the first, since the second is similar. In this case,
A
is of shape
(A_{1},A_{2})
and
B
of shape
{(B_{1},B_{2})}^{c}
and it follows from the induction hypothesis that
{{B_{1}}^{c}}^{c}\leq{A_{1}}^{c}
. Then
B_{1}\leq{A_{1}}^{c}
by the order involution property of complement. By
(CompRPairL)
we can then conclude
(B_{1},B_{2})\leq{(A_{1},A_{2})}^{c}
and, finally,
{{(B_{1},B_{2})}^{c}}^{c}\leq{(A_{1},A_{2})}^{c}
follows by
(CompLC)
.

(CompRUn)
,
(CompLUn)
:

These cases are symmetrical, so we only argue the former. In this case,
B
is of shape
{(\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}B_{i}^{\prime})}^{c}
and it follows from the induction hypothesis that
{{B_{i}^{\prime}}^{c}}^{c}\leq{A}^{c}
for all
i\in[1,k]
. Then, by the order involution property,
B_{i}^{\prime}\leq{A}^{c}
for all
i\in[1,k]
. Hence,
\textstyle\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}B_{i}\leq{A}^{c}
by
(UnionL)
. Finally, the desired result is obtained from this by
(CompLC)
.

(CompRC)
,
(CompLC)
:

These cases are symmetrical, so we only argue the former. In this case,
B
is of shape
{{B^{\prime}}^{c}}^{c}
and it follows from the induction hypothesis that
{B^{\prime}}^{c}\sqsubseteq{A}^{c}
. Then
{{{B^{\prime}}^{c}}^{c}}^{c}\leq{A}^{c}
follows by
(CompLC)
.

(Pair)
,
(Fun)
:

These cases are similar, so we only argue the former. In this case,
A
is of shape
(A_{1},A_{2})
and
B
of shape
(B_{1},B_{2})
, and it follows from the induction hypothesis that
{B_{1}}^{c}\sqsubseteq{A_{1}}^{c}
and
{B_{2}}^{c}\sqsubseteq{A_{2}}^{c}
. Then the result follows by
(CompLPair)
.

(UnionR)
,
(UnionL)
:

These cases are symmetrical, so we only argue the former. In this case,
B
is of shape
\textstyle\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}B_{i}^{\prime}
and it follows from the induction hypothesis that there is some
j
such that
{B_{j}^{\prime}}^{c}\leq{A}^{c}
. Then the result is obtained by
(CompLUn)
.

(CompLPair)
,
(CompLArr)
:

These cases are analogous, so we only argue the former. In this case,
A
is of shape
{(A_{1},A_{2})}^{c}
and
B
of shape
{(B_{1},B_{2})}^{c}
and it follows from the induction hypothesis that
{{B_{1}}^{c}}^{c}\leq{{A_{1}}^{c}}^{c}
and
{{B_{2}}^{c}}^{c}\leq{{A_{2}}^{c}}^{c}
. Then it follows from the order involution property that
B_{1}\leq A_{1}
and
B_{2}\leq A_{2}
, and by
(Pair)
,
(B_{1},\,B_{2})\leq(A_{1},A_{2})
. Finally, the result follows by
(CompLC)
and
(CompRC)
.

(CompLOk)
:

In this case,
A
is of shape
{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}
and it follows from the induction hypothesis that there is some type
{C}^{c}
of the set allowed by the side condition, such that
{B}^{c}\leq{{C}^{c}}^{c}
. Then
{B}^{c}\sqsubseteq C
by the order involution property, and
{B}^{c}\leq\mathsf{Ok}
follows from
(OkR)
. Finally, the result can be obtained by
(CompRC)
.

(CompLAtoms)
:

. In this case,
A
is of shape
{atomty}^{c}
and
B
is of shape
{\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}}^{c}
the result can be obtained from
(Atom)
by
(CompLC)
and
(CompRC)
.

∎

This makes it easy to recover
(CompL)
and
(CompR)
, i.e. to show that these are admissible in the alternative system.
Having recovered
(Trans)
,
(CompL)
and
(CompR)
, it is straightforward to see that the two systems are equivalent.

Theorem
6.3
:

A\sqsubseteq B
iff
A\leq B

Proof.

In the forward direction, the proof is by induction on
A\sqsubseteq B
.

(Ok)
:

In this case,
A
is
\mathsf{Int}\vee\mathsf{PairVal}\vee\mathsf{Fun}\vee\mathsf{Atom}
and
B
is
\mathsf{Ok}
. Then, since each of the disjuncts
X
can be shown a subtype of
\mathsf{Ok}
by
(Refl)
and
(OkR)
, the result follows by
(UnionL)
.

(Disj)
:

In this case,
B
is of shape
{C}^{c}
,
A\neq C
and either
A
and
C
are atoms, or they are both drawn from the set
\{\mathsf{Int},\,\mathsf{Pair},\,\mathsf{Fun},\,\mathsf{Atom}\}
. For each such pair of
A
and
C
, the required conclusion can be derived using one of
(CompRAtom1)
,
(CompRInt)
,
(CompRPair)
,
(CompRArr)
or
(CompRAtoms)
.

(PairC)
:

In this case,
A
is of shape
({A^{\prime}}^{c},\mathsf{Top})\vee(\mathsf{Ok},{B^{\prime}}^{c})
and
B
is
{(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})}^{c}
. By
(Refl)

{A^{\prime}}^{c}\leq{A^{\prime}}^{c}
and so
({A^{\prime}}^{c},\mathsf{Top})\leq{(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})}^{c}
by
(CompRPairL)
. A similar argument shows
(\mathsf{Ok},{B^{\prime}}^{c})\leq{(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})}^{c}
. Hence, the required conclusion follows by
(UnionL)
.

(CompR)
:

In this case,
B
is of shape
{C}^{c}
. It follows from the induction hypothesis that
C\leq{A}^{c}
. Then, by the order-reversing property of complement,
{{A}^{c}}^{c}\leq{C}^{c}
and the result follows by the involution property.

(CompL)
:

This is symmetrical to the previous case.

(Refl)
,
(Top)

(Atom)
,
(Pair)
,
(Fun)
,
(UnionL)
,
(UnionR)
:

These rules are identical in both systems.

In the backward direction, the proof is by induction on
A\leq B
.

(OkR)
:

In this case,
B=\mathsf{Ok}
. It follows from the induction hypothesis that there is some
C\in\{\mathsf{Int},\mathsf{PairVal},\mathsf{Fun},\mathsf{Atom}\}
such that
A\sqsubseteq C
. Then
C\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Int}\vee\mathsf{PairVal}\vee\mathsf{Fun}\vee\mathsf{Atom}
follows by
(UnionR)
and
A\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}
follows by transitivity.

(CompRAtom1)
:

In this case
A
is of shape
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{b}
and
B
is of shape
{\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}}^{c}
. Then the result follows from
(Disj)
.

(CompRAtom2)
:

In this case
B
is of shape
{\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}}^{c}
and it follows from the induction hypothesis that there is some type
C
allowed by the side condition and such that
A\sqsubseteq C
. In all cases,
A\sqsubseteq{\mathsf{Atom}}^{c}
follows by
(Disj)
,
{\mathsf{Atom}}^{c}\sqsubseteq{\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}}^{c}
follows from
(Atom)
by the order-reversing property of complement, and then the result follows by transitivity.

(CompRInt)
,
(CompRPair)
,
(CompRArr)
,
(CompRAtoms)
:

These cases are similar so we only show the first. In this case,
B={\mathsf{Int}}^{c}
and it follows from the induction hypothesis that there is some
C\in\{\mathsf{Pair},\mathsf{Fun},\mathsf{Atom}\}
such that
A\sqsubseteq C
. Then the result follows from
(Disj)
and transitivity.

(CompRPairL)
,
(CompRPairR)
:

These cases are similar, so we only show the former. In this case,
A
is of shape
(C,D)
and
B
is of shape
{(C^{\prime},D^{\prime})}^{c}
and the induction hypothesis yields
C\sqsubseteq{C^{\prime}}^{c}
. Since
D\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Top}
by
(Top)
,
(C,D)\sqsubseteq({C^{\prime}}^{c},\mathsf{Top})
by
(Pair)
. Then
(C,D)\sqsubseteq({C^{\prime}}^{c},\mathsf{Top})\vee(\mathsf{Ok},{D^{\prime}}^{c})
by
(UnionR)
and transitivity. Finally, the result is obtained by
(PairC)
and transitivity.

(CompRUn)
,
(CompLUn)
:

These cases are symmetrical, so we only show the former. In this case,
B
is of shape
{(\textstyle\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}B_{i})}^{c}
and it follows from the induction hypothesis that
A\sqsubseteq{B_{i}}^{c}
for all
i
. Then it follows from
(CompR)
that
{{B_{i}}^{c}}^{c}\sqsubseteq{A}^{c}
for all
i
, and therefore that
B_{i}\sqsubseteq{A}^{c}
for all
i
. Consequently,
\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}B_{i}\sqsubseteq{A}^{c}
and the result follows by
(CompR)
.

(CompRC)
,
(CompLC)
:

These cases are symmetrical, so we only show the former. In this case,
B
is of shape
{{D}^{c}}^{c}
and it follows from the induction hypothesis that
A\sqsubseteq D
. Then it follows from the involution property of complement (
Lemma

A.1
) that
A\sqsubseteq{{D}^{c}}^{c}
.

(CompLPair)
,
(CompLArr)
:

These cases are similar, so we show only the former. In this case,
A
is of shape
{(C_{1},C_{2})}^{c}
and
B
is of shape
{(D_{1},D_{2})}^{c}
and it follows from the induction hypothesis that
{C_{1}}^{c}\sqsubseteq{D_{1}}^{c}
and
{C_{2}}^{c}\sqsubseteq{D_{2}}^{c}
. Then since complement is order-reversing,
D_{1}\sqsubseteq C_{1}
and
D_{2}\sqsubseteq C_{2}
. Hence,
(D_{1},D_{2})\sqsubseteq(C_{1},C_{2})
by
(Pair)
and the result follows by the order-reversing property of complement (
Lemma

A.1
).

(CompLOk)
:

In this case,
A={\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}
and it follows from the induction hypothesis that there is some
C\in\{\mathsf{Int},\mathsf{Fun},\mathsf{Atom},\mathsf{PairVal}\}
such that
{C}^{c}\sqsubseteq B
. Then
{B}^{c}\sqsubseteq C
by
(CompL)
and
C\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}
by
(Ok)
, so
{B}^{c}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}
by transitivity. Then
(CompL)
gives
{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}\sqsubseteq B
as required.

(CompLAtoms)
:

In this case
A
is of shape
{\mathsf{Atom}}^{c}
,
B
is of shape
{\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}}^{c}
. Then the result follows from
(Atom)
by the order-reversing property of complement.

(Refl)
,
(Top)
,
(Atom)
,
(Pair)
,
(Fun)
,
(UnionL)
,
(UnionR)
:

These rules are identical in both systems.

∎

Appendix B
Derivability of the Refutation Rules, Theorem
4.1

Theorem
4.1
:

All the rules of Figure
4
are derivable.

Proof.

First observe that the swapping rules are derivable:

\frac{\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:{A}^{c}\Delta}{\Gamma,\,M:{({A}^{c})}^{c}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}\quad\textsc{left=CompL}}{\Gamma,\,M:A\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}\quad\textsc{left=SubL}\frac{\frac{\Gamma,\,M:{A}^{c}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:{({A}^{c})}^{c},\,\Delta}\quad\textsc{left=CompR}}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:A,\,\Delta}\quad\textsc{left=SubR}

We derive each of the remaining rules in turn (inline).

(BotL)
:

This judgement follows from
(Top)
by
(CompL)
.

(NumL)
:

This judgement follows from
(Num)
by
(CompL)
.

(FunL)
:

This judgement follows from
(Abs)
and
(Top)
by
(CompL)
.

(AtomL)
:

This judgement follows from
(Atom)
by
(CompL)
.

(PairL1)
:

This judgement follows from
(Pair)
and
(Top)
by
(CompL)

(PairLL)
:

Suppose
\Gamma,\,M_{1}:A_{1}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta
(the other case is similar). We have
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M_{1}:{A_{1}}^{c},\,\Delta
by
(CompR)
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M_{2}:\mathsf{Top},\,\Delta
by
(Top)
. Then,
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}(M_{1},M_{2}):({A_{1}}^{c},\mathsf{Top}),\,\Delta
by
(Pair)
. Since
(A_{1},\mathsf{Top})\sqsubseteq{(A_{1},A_{2})}^{c}
, we obtain
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}(M_{1},M_{2}):{(A_{1},A_{2})}^{c}
by
(SubL)
and finally the result is obtained by
(SwapL)
.

(PrjL1)
:

Suppose
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:({A}^{c},\,B),\,\Delta
. It follows from
(Prj
1
)
that
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\pi_{1}(M):{A}^{c},\,\Delta
and the result follows from
(SwapL)
.

(PrjL2)
:

Suppose
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:(A,\,{B}^{c}),\,\Delta
. It follows from
(Prj
2
)
that
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\pi_{2}(M):{B}^{c},\,\Delta
and the result follows from
(SwapL)
.

(AppL)
:

Suppose
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:B\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A,\,\Delta
and
\Gamma,\,N:B\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta
. Then it follows from
(CompR)
that
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}N:{B}^{c},\,\Delta
and hence, by
(App)
, that
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M\,N:{A}^{c}
. Then the result follows from
(SwapL)
.

(FixL)
:

Suppose
\Gamma,\,M:A\mathrel{\vdash}x:A,\,\Delta
. Then by
(CompL)
and
(CompR)
,
\Gamma,\,x:{A}^{c}\mathrel{\vdash}M:{A}^{c},\,\Delta
. Then
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\mathsf{fix}\,x.\,M:{A}^{c},\,\Delta
by
(Fix)
, and the result follows from
(SwapL)
.

(MatchL)
:

Suppose
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\theta_{i},\,\Delta
and, for each
i\in[1,k]
,
\Gamma,\,M:p_{i}\theta_{i},\,N_{i}:A\mathrel{\vdash}{\theta_{i}}^{c},\,\Delta
. Then, for each
i\in[1,k]
,
\Gamma,\,\theta_{i},\,M:p_{i}\theta_{i}\mathrel{\vdash}N_{i}:{A}^{c},\,\Delta
by
(SwapL)
and
(CompR)
. Hence, it follows from
(Match)
that
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\mathsf{match}\,M\,\mathsf{with}\,\{|_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\mapsto N_{i}\}:{A}^{c},\,\Delta
and the result can be obtained by
(SwapL)
.

∎

Appendix C
The Equivalence of the Two- and One-Sided Type Systems, Theorem
7.2

\frac{}{\Gamma,\,x:A\mathrel{\vdash}x:A,\,\Delta}\quad\textsc{Var}\frac{}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:\top,\,\Delta}\quad\textsc{Top}\frac{}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\underline{n}:\mathsf{Int},\,\Delta}\quad\textsc{Num}\frac{}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}:\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a},\,\Delta}\quad\textsc{Atom}\\
\frac{\Gamma,\,x:A\mathrel{\vdash}M:B,\,\Delta}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\lambda x.\,M:A\to B,\,\Delta}\quad\textsc{Abs}\;x\notin\mathsf{FV}{(\Gamma,\Delta)}\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:A,\,\Delta}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:B,\,\Delta}\quad\textsc{Sub}\;A\sqsubseteq B\frac{\Gamma,\,x:A\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}x:{A}^{c},\,\Delta}\quad\textsc{Comp}\\
\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:A,\,\Delta\quad\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}N:B,\,\Delta}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}(M,\,N):(A,\,B),\,\Delta}\quad\textsc{Pair}\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:\mathsf{Int},\,\Delta\quad\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}N:\mathsf{Int},\,\Delta}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M\otimes N:\mathsf{Int},\,\Delta}\quad\textsc{BinOp}\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:\mathsf{Int},\,\Delta\quad\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}N:\mathsf{Int},\,\Delta}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M\Join N:\mathsf{Bool},\,\Delta}\quad\textsc{RelOp}\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:B\to A,\,\Delta\quad\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}N:B,\,\Delta}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M\,N:A,\,\Delta}\quad\textsc{App}\frac{\Gamma,\,x:A\mathrel{\vdash}M:A,\,\Delta}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\mathsf{fix}\,x.\,M:A,\,\Delta}\quad\textsc{Fix}\;x\notin\mathsf{FV}{(\Gamma,\Delta)}\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:\textstyle\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\theta_{i},\,\Delta\quad\Gamma,\,\theta_{i}\mathrel{\vdash}M:{(p_{i}\theta_{i})}^{c},\,N_{i}:A,\,\Delta\;\;(\forall i)}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\mathsf{match}\,M\,\mathsf{with}\,\{\mid_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\mapsto N_{i}\}:A,\,\Delta}\quad\textsc{Match}\;\begin{array}[]{l}\forall i\in[1,k].\>\mathsf{PatSubst}(p_{i},\,\theta_{i})\\
\mathsf{FV}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k})\cap\mathsf{FV}(\Gamma,\Delta)=\varnothing\end{array}\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:{\mathsf{Int}}^{c},\,N:{\mathsf{Int}}^{c},\,\Delta}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M\otimes N:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c},\,\Delta}\quad\textsc{BinOpE}\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:{\mathsf{Int}}^{c},\,N:{\mathsf{Int}}^{c},\,\Delta}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M\Join N:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c},\,\Delta}\quad\textsc{RelOpE}\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c},\,N:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c},\,\Delta}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}(M,N):{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c},\,\Delta}\quad\textsc{PairE}\\
\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:{\mathsf{Fun}}^{c},\,\Delta}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\,M\,N:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c},\,\Delta}\quad\textsc{AppE}\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:{(\textstyle\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})])}^{c},\,\Delta}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\mathsf{match}\,M\,\mathsf{with}\,\{|_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\mapsto N_{i}\}:{{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}},\,\Delta}\quad\textsc{MatchE}

Figure 8
.
One-sided Typing.

The full typing rules of the one-sided system are given in Figure
8
. We begin by showing that the one-sided system admits a version of subtyping on the left (only for variables, since we do not allow terms on the left in the one-sided system), a version of complement on the left (again, only for variables) and a version of swap on the left (likewise).

Lemma C.1.

The following rules are admissible in the system of Figure
8
:

\frac{\Gamma,\,x:B\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}{\Gamma,\,x:A\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}\quad\textsc{SubL}\;A\sqsubseteq B\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}x:A,\,\Delta}{\Gamma,\,x:{A}^{c}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}\quad\textsc{CompL${}_{0}$}\;x\notin\textbf{dom}(\Gamma)\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}x:{A}^{c},\,\Delta}{\Gamma,\,x:A\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}\quad\textsc{SwapL${}_{0}$}\;x\notin\textbf{dom}(\Gamma)

Proof.

We start with
(SubL)
, since this is needed in the proof for
(CompL
0
)
. The proof is by induction on
\Gamma,\,x:B\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta
.

(Var)
:

Let
A\sqsubseteq B
. There are two subcases. If
x:B
is the principal formula of the rule, so that
\Delta
is of shape
x:B,\,\Delta^{\prime}
then we have
\Gamma,\,x:A\mathrel{\vdash}x:A,\,\Delta^{\prime}
by
(Var)
and then
\Gamma,\,x:A\mathrel{\vdash}x:B,\,\Delta^{\prime}
by
(Sub)
. Otherwise, there is some other
y:B\in\Gamma\cap\Delta
and so
\Gamma,\,x:A\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta
follows by
(Var)
.

Otherwise:

In all other cases, the result follows immediately from the induction hypotheses.

For the admissibility of
(CompL
0
)
we need to generalise the result to relax the condition on
\Gamma
. We instead prove that:

\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}x:A,\,\Delta
and
(\forall B\sqsubseteq A.\>x:B\notin\Gamma)
implies
\Gamma,\,x:{A}^{c}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta

by induction on the derivation of
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}x:A,\,\Delta
.

(Var)
:

Suppose
\forall B\sqsubseteq A.\,x:B\notin\Gamma
so that, in particular,
x:A\notin\Gamma
. Then it cannot be that
x:A
is the principal formula of the rule instance, and there is some other
y:B\in\Gamma\cap\Delta
which is. Consequently,
\Gamma,\,x:{A}^{c}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta
follows by
(Var)
.

(Top)
,
(Num)
,
(Atom)
:

For each of these axioms (R),
x:A
cannot be the principal formula of the rule, and so the conclusion follows by (R).

(Comp)
:

Suppose
\forall B\sqsubseteq A.\,x:B\notin\Gamma
. There are three subcases.

•:

If
x:A
is the principal formula of the rule, then
A
is of shape
{C}^{c}
and the rule hypothesis gives
\Gamma,\,x:C\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta
. Then
\Gamma,\,x:{({C}^{c})}^{c}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta
follows by
(SubL)
, which is just
\Gamma,\,x:{A}^{c}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta
, as required.

•:

Otherwise, the principal formula is some
y:{C}^{c}\in\Delta
and
\Delta
has shape
y:{C}^{c},\,\Delta^{\prime}
. If
y=x
and
C\sqsubseteq A
, then this judgement has shape
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}x:A,\,x:{C}^{c},\,\Delta^{\prime}
and our goal is to show
\Gamma,\,x:{A}^{c}\mathrel{\vdash}x:{C}^{c},\,\Delta^{\prime}
. Since
C\sqsubseteq A
, also
{A}^{c}\sqsubseteq{C}^{c}
, and the goal may be proven directly:

\frac{\frac{}{\Gamma,\,x:{A}^{c}\mathrel{\vdash}x:{A}^{c},\,\Delta^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{left=Var}}{\Gamma,\,x:{A}^{c}\mathrel{\vdash}x:{C}^{c},\,\Delta^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{left=Sub}

•:

Otherwise the principal formula is some
y:{C}^{c}\in\Delta
and
\Delta
has shape
y:{C}^{c},\,\Delta^{\prime}
but
y\neq x
or
C\not\sqsubseteq A
. Hence, there is no typing
x:B\in(\Gamma,\,y:C)
with
B\sqsubseteq A
and we may apply the induction hypothesis to obtain:
\Gamma,\,y:C,\,x:{A}^{c}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta^{\prime}
. Thus,
\Gamma,\,x:{A}^{c}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta
follows by another application of
(Comp)
.

(Sub)
:

Suppose
\forall B\sqsubseteq A.\,x:B\notin\Gamma
. There are two subcases.

•:

If
x:A
is the principal formula of the rule instance, then the induction hypothesis gives some
C\sqsubseteq A
such that, if
\forall B\sqsubseteq C.\,x:B\notin\Gamma
, then
\Gamma,\,x:{C}^{c}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta
. To establish the antecedent, suppose that
B\sqsubseteq C
and
x:B\in\Gamma
and obtain a contradiction with the assumption, since then we would have some
x:B\in\Gamma
with
B\sqsubseteq C\sqsubseteq A
. Therefore, we obtain
\Gamma,\,x:{C}^{c}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta
from the induction hypothesis. Since
C\sqsubseteq A
,
{A}^{c}\sqsubseteq{C}^{c}
and thus
\Gamma,\,x:{A}^{c}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta
follows by
(SubL)
.

•:

Otherwise,
\Delta
is of shape
M:C,\,\Delta^{\prime}
and it follows from the induction hypothesis that there is some
D
such that
D\sqsubseteq C
and
\Gamma,\,x:{A}^{c}\mathrel{\vdash}M:D,\,\Delta^{\prime}
. Then the desired
\Gamma,\,x:{A}^{c}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta
follows by
(Sub)
.

Otherwise:

For every other rule (R),
x:A
cannot be the principal formula and so the result follows immediately from the induction hypotheses and another application of (R).

Finally, we can then derive
(SwapL
0
)
. Suppose
x\notin\textbf{dom}(\Gamma)
and note that
A\sqsubseteq{({A}^{c})}^{c}
:

\frac{\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}x:{A}^{c},\,\Delta}{\Gamma,\,x:{{A}^{c}}^{c}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}\quad\textsc{left=CompL${}_{0}$}}{\Gamma,\,x:A\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta}\quad\textsc{left=SubL}

∎

Now we may prove the main equivalence theorem.

Theorem
7.2
:

Let us write
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}_{2}\Delta
for provability in the system of Figure
2
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}_{1}\Delta
for provability in the system of Figure
4
. Then both of the following:

(1)

If
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}_{2}\Delta
then
\mathrel{\vdash}_{1}{\Gamma}^{c},\,\Delta
.

(2)

If
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}_{1}\Delta
then
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}_{2}\Delta
.

Proof.

Part (i) is proven by induction on
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}_{2}\Delta
.

(Var)
:

In this case,
\Delta
is of shape
x:A,\,\Delta^{\prime}
and
\Gamma
is of shape
\Gamma^{\prime},\,x:A
. Hence,
x:A\mathrel{\vdash}_{1}{\Gamma^{\prime}}^{c},\,x:A,\,\Delta^{\prime}
by
(Var)
and
\mathrel{\vdash}_{1}{\Gamma}^{c},\,\Delta
by
(Comp)
.

(Top)
,
(Num)
,
(Atom)
:

In these cases, the result is immediate.

(SubL)
:

In this case,
\Gamma
is of shape
\Gamma^{\prime},\,M:A
