. It follows from the induction hypothesis that there is some type
A\sqsubseteq B
such that
\mathrel{\vdash}_{1}{\Gamma^{\prime}}^{c},\,M:{B}^{c},\,\Delta
and the desired conclusion follows from
(Sub)
.

(CompL)
:

In this case,
\Gamma
is of shape
\Gamma^{\prime},\,M:{A}^{c}
. It follows from the induction hypothesis that
\mathrel{\vdash}_{1}{\Gamma^{\prime}}^{c},\,M:{{A}^{c}}^{c},\,\Delta
, and the conclusion follows by
(Sub)
since
{{A}^{c}}^{c}\sqsubseteq A
.

(CompR)
:

In this case,
\Delta
is of shape
M:{A}^{c},\,\Delta^{\prime}
and it follows from the induction hypothesis that
\mathrel{\vdash}_{1}{\Gamma}^{c},\,M:{A}^{c},\,\Delta^{\prime}
, as required.

(Abs)
:

In this case,
\Delta
is of shape
\lambda x.\,M:A\to B,\,\Delta^{\prime}
. We may assume that
x
does not occur in
\Gamma
or
\Delta^{\prime}
. The induction hypothesis gives:

(1):

\mathrel{\vdash}_{1}{\Gamma}^{c},\,x:{A}^{c},\,M:B,\,\Delta^{\prime}

Then,
x:A\mathrel{\vdash}_{1}{\Gamma}^{c},\,M:B,\,\Delta^{\prime}
by
(SwapL
0
)
and the result can be obtained by
(Abs)
.

(Fix)
:

In this case,
\Delta
is of shape
\mathsf{fix}\,x.\,M:A,\,\Delta^{\prime}
. We may assume that
x
does not occur in
\Gamma
or
\Delta^{\prime}
. Then the induction hypothesis gives:

(1):

\mathrel{\vdash}_{1}{\Gamma}^{c},\,x:A,\,M:A,\,\Delta^{\prime}

Then,
x:A\mathrel{\vdash}_{1}{\Gamma}^{c},\,M:A,\,\Delta^{\prime}
follows by
(SwapL
0
)
and the result can then be obtained using
(Fix)
.

(Match)
:

In this case,
\Delta
is of shape
\mathsf{match}\,M\,\mathsf{with}\,\{|_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\mapsto N_{i}\}:A,\,\Delta^{\prime}
and we may assume that no variable in any of the patterns
p_{i}
occurs in
\Gamma
or
\Delta^{\prime}
. The induction hypotheses give some substitutions
\theta_{i}
whose domain is exactly
\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})
and such that:

(1):

\mathrel{\vdash}_{1}{\Gamma}^{c},\,M:\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\theta_{i},\,\Delta^{\prime}

(2):

for each
i\in[1,k]
:
\mathrel{\vdash}_{1}{\Gamma}^{c},\,{\theta_{i}}^{c},\,M:{(p_{i}\theta_{i})}^{c},\,N_{i}:A,\,\Delta^{\prime}

If follows by
\Sigma_{i=1}^{k}|\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})|
applications of
(SwapL
0
)
that, for each
i\in[1,k]
,
\theta_{i}\mathrel{\vdash}_{1}{\Gamma}^{c},\,M:{(p_{i}\theta_{i})}^{c},\,N_{i}:A,\,\Delta^{\prime}
. Therefore, the result can be derived by
(Match)
.

Otherwise:

For all other rules
(R)
of the first system, the result follows immediately from the induction hypotheses and the corresponding rule
(R)
of the second system.

Part (ii) can be proven by induction on
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}_{1}\Delta
.

(Comp)
:

In this case,
\Delta
has shape
x:{A}^{c},\,\Delta^{\prime}
the induction hypothesis gives
\Gamma,\,x:A\mathrel{\vdash}_{2}\Delta^{\prime}
from which we obtain the result by
(CompR)
.

(OpE)
:

In this case,
\Delta
has shape
M\otimes N:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c},\,\Delta^{\prime}
and it follows from the induction hypothesis that
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}_{2}M:{\mathsf{Int}}^{c},\,N:{\mathsf{Int}}^{c},\,\Delta^{\prime}
. Then, by two applications of
(SwapL)
, we obtain
\Gamma,\,M:\mathsf{Int},\,N:\mathsf{Int}\mathrel{\vdash}_{2}\Delta^{\prime}
from which the conclusion follows by
(OpE)
.

(AppE)
:

In this case,
\Delta
has shape
M\,N:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c},\,\Delta^{\prime}
and it follows from the induction hypothesis that
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}_{2}M:{\mathsf{Fun}}^{c},\,\Delta^{\prime}
. By
(SwapL)
,
\Gamma,\,M:\mathsf{Fun}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta^{\prime}
and the conclusion follows by
(AppE)
.

(MatchE)
:

In this case,
\Delta
has shape
\mathsf{match}\,M\,\mathsf{with}\,\{|_{i=1}^{k}P_{i}\mapsto N_{i}\}:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c},\,\Delta^{\prime}
and it follows from the induction hypothesis that
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}_{2}M:{(\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\theta_{\mathsf{Ok}})}^{c},\,\Delta^{\prime}
. By
(SwapL)
,
\Gamma,\,M:\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\theta_{\mathsf{Ok}}\mathrel{\vdash}_{2}\Delta^{\prime}
and the conclusion follows by
(MatchE)
.

(PrjE
i
)
:

In this case,
\Delta
has shape
\pi_{i}(M):{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c},\,\Delta^{\prime}
and it follows from the induction hypothesis that
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}_{2}M:{\mathsf{Pair}}^{c},\,\Delta^{\prime}
. By
(SwapL)
,
\Gamma,\,M:\mathsf{Pair}\mathrel{\vdash}_{2}\Delta^{\prime}
and the conclusion follows by
(PrjE
i
)
.

Otherwise:

For every other rule (R) of the system of Figure
8
, the conclusion follows immediately from the induction hypotheses and an application of (R) in the system of Figure
2
.

∎

Appendix D
Progress and Preservation

The proofs in this appendix relate to the one-sided system of Figure
8
.

D.1.
Preservation

Lemma D.1 (Weakening).

If
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta
is provable, then for any term
Q
and type
B
, it is also provable that
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}Q:B,\,\Delta
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that for any proof
\delta
of
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta
, we can always construct some proof
\delta^{\prime}
of
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}Q:B,\,\Delta
(with arbitrary
Q:B
). We proceed by induction on the height of
\delta
.

Case
((
0pt{\delta}=0
)). Here,
\delta
must be an instance of an axiom rule (i.e.,
Var
,
Top
,
Num
, or
Atom
). Thus
\delta
must be of one of the structures shown in
Figure

9(a)
. For every
\delta
that proves
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta
, we can construct
\delta^{\prime}
by inserting
Q:B
into the right context of the underlying rule instance of
\delta
, such that
\delta^{\prime}
proves
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}Q:B,\,\Delta
.
Figure

9(b)
shows the corresponding structures of
\delta^{\prime}
for all respective structures of
\delta
.

\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{}{\underbrace{\smash{\Gamma^{\prime},\,x:A}}_{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}\underbrace{\smash{x:A,\,\Delta^{\prime}}}_{\Delta}}\quad\textsc{left={Var}}\\[6.00006pt]
\frac{}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\underbrace{\smash{M:\top,\,\Delta^{\prime}}}_{\Delta}}\quad\textsc{left={Top}}\\[6.00006pt]
\frac{}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\underbrace{\smash{n:\mathsf{Int},\,\Delta^{\prime}}}_{\Delta}}\quad\textsc{left={Num}}\\[6.00006pt]
\frac{}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\underbrace{\smash{\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}:\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a},\,\Delta^{\prime}}}_{\Delta}}\quad\textsc{left={Atom}}\end{array}

(a)

Proof
\delta
of
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta
.

\xrightarrow{\text{Add }Q:B\text{ to the right context}}

\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{}{\underbrace{\smash{\Gamma^{\prime},\,x:A}}_{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}Q:B,\,\underbrace{\smash{x:A,\,\Delta^{\prime}}}_{\Delta}}\quad\textsc{left={Var}}\\[6.00006pt]
\frac{}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}Q:B,\,\underbrace{\smash{M:\top,\,\Delta^{\prime}}}_{\Delta}}\quad\textsc{left={Top}}\\[6.00006pt]
\frac{}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}Q:B,\,\underbrace{\smash{n:\mathsf{Int},\,\Delta^{\prime}}}_{\Delta}}\quad\textsc{left={Num}}\\[6.00006pt]
\frac{}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}Q:B,\,\underbrace{\smash{\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}:\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a},\,\Delta^{\prime}}}_{\Delta}}\quad\textsc{left={Atom}}\end{array}

(b)

Proof
\delta^{\prime}
of
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}Q:B,\,\Delta
.

Consequently, whenever
\Gamma\,\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta
has a proof of height
0
,
\Gamma\,\mathrel{\vdash}Q:B,\,\Delta
also has a proof of height
0
,

Case
((
0pt{\delta}>0
)). Suppose for every proof
\delta
of
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta
with height
\leq k\,(k\geq 0)
, we can construct a proof
\delta^{\prime}
of
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}Q:B,\,\Delta
. Now consider
0pt{\delta}=k+1
, where
\delta
must have the structure:

(Structure of
\delta
)

\frac{\Gamma,\,\Gamma_{c}^{i}\mathrel{\vdash}_{\delta_{i}}\Delta_{c}^{i},\,\Delta_{r}\quad(0<i\leq n)}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\underbrace{\smash{P:A,\,\Delta_{r}}}_{\Delta}}\quad\textsc{left={{R}},right=\normalsize\,($n>0$)}

where
R
is the rule applied at the root step,
P:A
is the principal formula of the root application and
\Gamma^{i}_{c},\,\Delta^{i}_{c}
are the constiuent formulas of the
i^{th}
premise, that is, those that are distinguished in the rule. Since each immediate subproof
\delta_{i}
has height
0pt{\delta_{i}}\leq 0pt{\delta}-1=(k+1)-1=k
, it follows by the induction hypothesis that:

(7)

\forall Q\,\forall B.\,\exists\delta^{\prime}_{i}\text{ such that }\Gamma,\,\Gamma_{c}^{i}\mathrel{\vdash}_{\delta^{\prime}_{i}}\Delta_{c}^{i},\,Q:B,\,\Delta_{r}\quad(0<i\leq n)

By applying
R
to the judgements in
Equation

7
, we combine all these modified subproofs
\delta^{\prime}_{1},\,\ldots,\,\delta^{\prime}_{n}
and construct:

(Structure of
\delta^{\prime}
)

\frac{\Gamma,\,\Gamma_{c}^{i}\mathrel{\vdash}_{\delta^{\prime}_{i}}\Delta_{c}^{i},\,Q:B,\,\Delta_{r}\quad(0<i\leq n)}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}Q:B,\,\underbrace{\smash{P:A,\,\Delta_{r}}}_{\Delta}}\quad\textsc{left={{R}},right=\normalsize\,($n>0$)}

Let
\delta^{\prime}
be this proof. It is immediate that
\delta^{\prime}
proves
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}Q:B,\,\Delta
, which completes the inductive step.

By combining the base and inductive step, we conclude that for every proof (regardless of its height) of
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta
, their exists a corresponding proof of
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}Q:B,\,\Delta
where
Q:B
is arbitrary. Thus the provability of
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta
guarantees the provability of
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}Q:B,\,\Delta
for any term
Q
and type
B
, thereby proving the weakening lemma.

∎

Lemma D.2 (Substitution).

Suppose
\Gamma,\,x:A\vdash M:B,\,\Delta
, where
x\notin\mathsf{FV}(\Delta)
. If
\Gamma\vdash N:C,\,\Delta
and
C\sqsubseteq A
,
then
\Gamma\vdash M[{N}/{x}]:B,\,\Delta
.

Proof.

By induction on the structure of
M
.
∎

Definition D.3 (Subtyping on Type Substitutions).

The subtyping relation
\sqsubseteq
is extended to type substitutions
pointwise
:

\theta\sqsubseteq\theta^{\prime}\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smash{\mbox{\tiny def}}\vphantom{=}}}{{=}}\quad\textbf{dom}(\theta^{\prime})=\textbf{dom}(\theta)\;\land\;\forall x\in\textbf{dom}(\theta^{\prime}).\,\theta(x)\sqsubseteq\theta^{\prime}(x).

Lemma D.4.

Let
p
be a pattern, and let
\theta,\,\theta^{\prime}
be type substitutions with
\textbf{dom}(\theta)=\textbf{dom}(\theta^{\prime})=\mathsf{FV}(p)
. Then:

p\theta\sqsubseteq p\theta^{\prime}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\theta\sqsubseteq\theta^{\prime}

Proof.

Both directions of
\Leftrightarrow
are proved by induction on on the shape of
p
. The induction for
\Leftarrow
direction is straightforward. Here we only show the pair case for the
\Rightarrow
direction.

Case
(
p
is of shape
(p_{1},p_{2})
).
Then from
p\theta\sqsubseteq p\theta^{\prime}
, we have:

(p_{1}\theta_{1},p_{2}\theta_{2})\sqsubseteq(p_{1}\theta^{\prime}_{1},p_{2}\theta^{\prime}_{2})

, where
\theta_{1}
,
\theta^{\prime}_{1}
and
\theta_{2}
,
\theta^{\prime}_{1}
are the restriction of
\theta
and
\theta^{\prime}
on
\mathsf{FV}(p_{1})
and
\mathsf{FV}(p_{2})
respectively.
By
Lemma

D.17
it follows that
p_{1}\theta\sqsubseteq p_{1}\theta^{\prime}_{1}
and
p_{2}\theta\sqsubseteq p_{2}\theta^{\prime}_{2}
. Then by the induction hypothosis we have
\theta_{1}\sqsubseteq\theta^{\prime}_{1}
and
\theta_{2}\sqsubseteq\theta^{\prime}_{2}
. Since
\textbf{dom}{\theta}=\textbf{dom}(\theta^{\prime})=\mathsf{FV}(p_{1})\cup\mathsf{FV}(p_{2})
,
\theta\sqsubseteq\theta^{\prime}
follows directly.

∎

Definition D.5 (Coherence of Term and Type Substitutions under Contexts).

Let
\sigma
be a term substitution (mapping term variables to terms),
\theta
a type substitution (mapping term variables to types), and
\Gamma
,
\Delta
left and right contexts respectively. We define the
coherence relation

\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\sigma\mathrel{::}\theta,\,\Delta
as:

\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\sigma\mathrel{::}\theta,\,\Delta\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smash{\mbox{\tiny def}}\vphantom{=}}}{{=}}\quad\textbf{dom}(\theta)=\textbf{dom}(\sigma)\;\land\;\forall x\in\textbf{dom}(\theta).\,\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\sigma(x):\theta(x),\,\Delta

Remark

The implication of
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\sigma\mathrel{::}\theta,\,\Delta
depends critically on the contexts:

(1)

When
\Gamma\nvdash\Delta
, the relation
\Gamma\vdash\sigma\mathrel{::}\theta,\Delta
says
\sigma
and
\theta
share the same domain, and for each variable
x
in the domain, the type
\theta(x)
assigned to
\sigma(x)
is
meaningful
under the given contexts.

(2)

By contrast, when
\Gamma\vdash\Delta
, the relation
\Gamma\vdash\sigma\mathrel{::}\theta,\Delta
is satisfied vacuously for any
\sigma
and
\theta
with
\textbf{dom}(\sigma)=\textbf{dom}(\theta)
. This follows directly from
Lemma

D.1
(
Weakening
). For example, when
\Gamma\vdash\Delta
we can have
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}[(\lambda y.\,y)/x]\mathrel{::}[\mathsf{Int}/x],\,\Delta
.

Lemma D.6 (Simultaneous Substitution).

Suppose
\Gamma,\,\theta^{\prime}\mathrel{\vdash}N:A,\,\Delta
, where
\textbf{dom}(\theta^{\prime})\cap\mathsf{FV}(\Delta)=\varnothing
.
If
\theta\sqsubseteq\theta^{\prime}
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\sigma\mathrel{::}\theta,\,\Delta
, then
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}N\sigma:A,\,\Delta
.

Proof.

By
Lemma

D.2
(
Substitution
) and induction on the size of
\textbf{dom}(\theta^{\prime})
.
∎

Proof.

By structural inspection of terms and analysis of the reduction relation.
∎

Unlike a traditional type system, we must prove inversion by induction because the rule used to prove a relevant formula may not be at the root of the tree.

Lemma D.7 (Inversion).

Let
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}Q:A,\,\Delta
and
A\mathrel{\not\sim}\mathsf{Top}
. Then,
either

\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta
is itself provable,
or

Q
satisfies the following syntax-directed conditions:

(1)

If
Q
is of shape
x
, then either:

\node

[shape=circle,
draw,
inner sep=0pt,
text depth=0.15em,
text height=0.8em,
minimum width=1em,
font=
,
anchor=base] (char)
0
;

]

(a)

\exists B.B\sqsubseteq A\land x:B\in\Gamma

(b)

\exists B.{B}^{c}\sqsubseteq A\land\Gamma,x:B\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta
.

(2)

If
Q
is of shape
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}
, then
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}\sqsubseteq A
.

(3)

If
Q
is of shape
\underline{n}
, then
\mathsf{Int}\sqsubseteq A
.

(4)

If
Q
is of shape
\lambda x.\,M
, then
\exists B,\,C
such that
B\to C\sqsubseteq A
and
\Gamma,x:B\mathrel{\vdash}M:C,\,\Delta
.

(5)

If
Q
is of shape
{M}\,{N}
, then either:

\node

[shape=circle,
draw,
inner sep=0pt,
text depth=0.15em,
text height=0.8em,
minimum width=1em,
font=
,
anchor=base] (char)
0
;

]

(a)

\exists B,C
such that
C\sqsubseteq A
,
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:B\to C,\,\Delta
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}N:B,\,\Delta

(b)

{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}\sqsubseteq A
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:{\mathsf{Fun}}^{c},\,\Delta
.

(6)

If
Q
is of shape
(M_{1},M_{2})
, then either:

\node

[shape=circle,
draw,
inner sep=0pt,
text depth=0.15em,
text height=0.8em,
minimum width=1em,
font=
,
anchor=base] (char)
0
;

]

(a)

\exists A_{1},\,A_{2}
such that
(A_{1},A_{2})\sqsubseteq A
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M_{1}:A_{1},\,\Delta
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M_{2}:A_{2},\,\Delta

(b)

{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}\sqsubseteq A
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M_{1}:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c},\,M_{2}:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c},\,\Delta
.

(7)

If
Q
is of shape
\mathsf{fix}\,x.\,M
, then
\exists A^{\prime}
such that
A^{\prime}\sqsubseteq A
and
\Gamma,\,x:A^{\prime}\mathrel{\vdash}M:A^{\prime},\,\Delta
.

(8)

If
Q
is of shape
M\otimes N
, then either:

\node

[shape=circle,
draw,
inner sep=0pt,
text depth=0.15em,
text height=0.8em,
minimum width=1em,
font=
,
anchor=base] (char)
0
;

]

(a)

\mathsf{Int}\sqsubseteq A
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:\mathsf{Int},\,\Delta
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}N:\mathsf{Int},\,\Delta

(b)

{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}\sqsubseteq A
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:{\mathsf{Int}}^{c},\,N:{\mathsf{Int}}^{c},\,\Delta
.

(9)

If
Q
is of shape
M\Join N
, then either:

\node

[shape=circle,
draw,
inner sep=0pt,
text depth=0.15em,
text height=0.8em,
minimum width=1em,
font=
,
anchor=base] (char)
0
;

]

(a)

\mathsf{Bool}\sqsubseteq A
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:\mathsf{Int},\,\Delta
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}N:\mathsf{Int},\,\Delta

(b)

{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}\sqsubseteq A
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:{\mathsf{Int}}^{c},\,N:{\mathsf{Int}}^{c},\,\Delta
.

(10)

If
Q
is of shape
\mathsf{match}\,M\,\mathsf{with}\,\{\mid_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\mapsto N_{i}\}
then either:

\node

[shape=circle,
draw,
inner sep=0pt,
text depth=0.15em,
text height=0.8em,
minimum width=1em,
font=
,
anchor=base] (char)
0
;

]

(a)

\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:\textstyle\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\theta_{i},\,\Delta
and
\forall i\in[1,k].\,\Gamma,\,\theta_{i}\mathrel{\vdash}M:{(p_{i}\theta_{i})}^{c},\,N_{i}:A,\,\Delta
and, for all
i\in[1,k]
and
x\in\textbf{dom}(\theta_{i})
,
\theta_{i}(x)\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}
.

(b)

{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}\sqsubseteq A
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:{(\textstyle\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})])}^{c},\,\Delta
.

Proof.

The proof follows the same basic idea as the proof of inversion for the sequent calculus in
Troelstra and Schwichtenberg 2000
, Proposition 3.5.4. The proof is by induction on the height of the derivation of
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}Q:A,\,\Delta
. We analyse cases on the proof rule used in the conclusion, but, in each case it is not known whether the distinguished typing
Q:A
in the statement is actually the principal formula of the rule (in which case the conclusion follows directly by inspection), or not. So we must analyse both possibilities.

•

If
Q:A
is the principal formula of the rule, then in all the cases except
(Sub)
, the conclusion follows directly. For example, in the case
(Comp)
, since
Q:A
is the principal formula,
Q
is a variable
x
and
A
is a type of shape
{D}^{c}
. We may assume the premise
\Gamma,\,x:{D}^{c}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta
and thus we have shown (1) part
2
with witness
B=D
. For the case
(Sub)
, we note that the induction hypothesis applies to the premise, of shape
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}Q:D,\,\Delta
with
D\sqsubseteq A
, and this gives that
Q:D
satisfies the conditions given. Then observe that all the conditions require only that the given type
A
is a supertype (of some type that depends on the particular case), and thus, since the syntax-directed conditions hold for
Q:D
they also hold for
Q:A
, since
D\sqsubseteq A
.

•

If
Q:A
is not the principal formula of the rule we argue as follows. Suppose
\Gamma\nvdash\Delta
. Let us say the rule is (R). Since
Q:A
is not principal, the instance of the rule used at the root of the proof has
Q:A
part of the
\Delta
-context of the conclusion of the rule (here we mean
\Delta
that occurs in the statement of the rule, not as in the statement of this lemma). That is, the conclusion is of shape
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:B,\,Q:A,\,\Delta^{\prime}
with
M:B
principal. In each rule, observe that the
\Delta
context that occurs in the conclusion also occurs in
every
premise, thus
Q:A,\,\Delta^{\prime}
occurs in every premise. Each of the, say
k
, premises therefore have shape (for
i\in[1..k]
):

\Gamma,\,\Gamma_{i}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta_{i},\,Q:A,\,\Delta^{\prime}

where
\Gamma_{i}
consists of any variable typings introduced in a premise and
\Delta_{i}
consists of any typing formulas introduced by the premise. For example in
(Abs)
,
k=1
,
\Gamma_{1}=\{x:A\}
and
\Delta_{1}=\{M:B\}
. The assumption
\Gamma\nvdash\Delta
is exactly
\Gamma\nvdash M:B,\,\Delta^{\prime}
, and thus it follows that for at least one
j
,
\Gamma,\,\Gamma_{j}\nvdash\Delta_{j},\,\Delta^{\prime}
. Otherwise, we could conclude by (R) that
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:B,\,\Delta^{\prime}
, contradicting the assumption. Thus, from the induction hypothesis applied to the proof tree of this premise
j
, and we may conclude that
Q:A
satisfies the syntax-directed conditions in the context
\Gamma,\,\Gamma_{j}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta_{j},\,Q:A,\,\Delta^{\prime}
.

The syntax directed conditions (
n
)
m
typically introduce some existentially quantified types along with formulas that constrain them. These formulas have the following shapes:

(1)

Subtype inequalities that have
A
on the right, e.g.
B\sqsubseteq A
or
\mathsf{Int}\sqsubseteq A
.

(2)

Typing judgements of shape
\Gamma,\,\Gamma^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta^{\prime\prime},\,\Delta
, i.e. which weaken the judgement
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta
by some additional assumptions
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
and consequences
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
.

(3)

In the case of (1)
1
, the condition
x:B\in\Gamma
.

(4)

In the case of (10)
1
, the conditions
\theta_{i}(x)\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}
.

In each case, we take the witnessing types for the overall goal to be exactly those given by the induction hypothesis applied to premise
j
. Then, we immediately obtain the formulas of types (i) and (iv). However, the formulas of shape (ii) and (iii) do not immediately follow, since the induction hypothesis gives us only that these formulas hold for the weaker contexts
\Gamma,\,\Gamma_{j}
and
\Delta_{j},\,\Delta^{\prime}
.

For the formula
x:B\in\Gamma
of type (iii), which occurs when
Q=x
, the induction hypothesis gives only that
x:B\in\Gamma,\,\Gamma_{j}
where
\Gamma_{j}
is introduced by the premise of this rule (R). Observe, that the only rules for which
\Gamma_{j}
is not empty are
(Abs)
,
(Fix)
and
(Match)
and in all cases, the subjects of
\Gamma_{j}
are required to be disjoint from free variables in
\Gamma
and
\Delta
. Thus, since
Q:A=x:A\in\Delta
, we can conclude that, in fact,
x:B\notin\Gamma_{j}
and it must be that
x:B\in\Gamma
, as required.

Finally, for the formulas of type (ii), the induction hypothesis gives us only that
\Gamma,\,\Gamma_{j},\,\Gamma^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta^{\prime\prime},\,\Delta_{j},\,\Delta^{\prime}
. However, we can show that
\Gamma,\,\Gamma_{i},\,\Gamma^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta^{\prime\prime},\,\Delta_{i},\,\Delta^{\prime}
holds for all premises
i\in[1..k]
, and then the desired formula
\Gamma,\,\Gamma^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta^{\prime\prime},\,\Delta
of type (ii) can be obtained by recombining these using rule (R). To see this, consider some premise
i
. Either
\Gamma,\,\Gamma_{i}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta_{i},\,\Delta^{\prime}
holds, in which case we can obtained the desired judgement by weakening, or
\Gamma,\,\Gamma_{i}\nvdash\Delta_{i},\,\Delta^{\prime}
, in which case the desired judgement can be obtained from the induction hypothesis.

Two illustrative cases are as follows.

–

Consider rule
R
being
(Abs)
and
Q
is of shape
x
. Then the conclusion has shape
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\lambda y.\,P:B\to C,\,x:A,\,\Delta^{\prime}
, with
\Delta=\lambda y.\,P:B\to C,\,\Delta
. Suppose
\Gamma\nvdash\Delta
, so it must be that
\Gamma,\,y:B\nvdash P:C,\,\Delta^{\prime}
. Since we may assume
y\notin\mathsf{FV}(\Delta)
, it follows that
y\neq x
. Therefore, it follows from the induction hypothesis that either (1)
1
or (1)
2
. Suppose the former. Then there is
D
such that
D\sqsubseteq A
and
x:D\in\Gamma,\,y:B
, but since
y\neq x
, so
x:D\in\Gamma
as required. Otherwise, there is some type
D
such that
{D}^{c}\sqsubseteq A
and
\Gamma,\,y:B,\,x:D\mathrel{\vdash}P:C,\,\Delta
and therefore
\Gamma,\,x:D\mathrel{\vdash}\lambda y.\,P:B\to C,\,\Delta^{\prime}
as required.

–

Consider rule
R
being
(App)
and
Q
being of shape
Q_{1}\otimes Q_{2}
. Then the conclusion has shape
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M\ N:B,\,Q_{1}\otimes Q_{2}:A,\,\Delta^{\prime}
and we know that
\Gamma\nvdash M\ N:B,\,\Delta^{\prime}
. If
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:D\to B,\,\Delta^{\prime}
then it must be that
\Gamma\nvdash N:D,\,\Delta^{\prime}
and it follows from the induction hypothesis that, therefore,
Q:A
satisfies (8)
1
or (8)
2
. Suppose (8)
1
, so that we obtain (a)
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}Q_{1}:\mathsf{Int},\,N:D,\,\Delta^{\prime}
and (b)
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}Q_{2}:\mathsf{Int},\,N:D,\,\Delta^{\prime}
. Since
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M:D\to B,\,\Delta^{\prime}
, it follows by weakening that (c)
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}Q_{1}:\mathsf{Int},\,M:D\to B,\,\Delta^{\prime}
and (d)
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}Q_{2}:\mathsf{Int},\,M:D\to B,\,\Delta^{\prime}
. Therefore, combining (c) and (a) using
(App)
gives
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}Q_{1}:\mathsf{Int},\,M\ N:B,\,\Delta^{\prime}
, and combining (d) and (b) using
(App)
gives
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}Q_{2}:\mathsf{Int},\,M\ N:B,\,\Delta^{\prime}
. Thus (8)
1
holds for
Q
, as required. The cases in which (8)
2
holds, and in which already
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}N:D,\,\Delta^{\prime}
(in which case we know that
\Gamma\nvdash M:D\to B,\,\Delta^{\prime}
) are symmetrical.

∎

Lemma D.8.

For any types
A
and
B
,
\lambda x.\,\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{div}}}\in[\![A\to B]\!]
.

Proof.

By the definition of
[\![\cdot]\!]_{\ast}
, for any type
B
,
\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{div}}}\in[\![B]\!]_{\ast}
holds vacuously.
Moreover, as
\mathsf{FV}(\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{div}}})=\varnothing
, we have
\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{div}}}[{N}/{x}]=\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{div}}}
for any term
N
. It follows that
\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{div}}}[{N}/{x}]\in[\![B]\!]_{\ast}
for any type
B
and any term
N
. By
Definition

3.1
, this guarantees that
\lambda x.\,\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{div}}}\in[\![A\to B]\!]
for any types
A
and
B
.
∎

Lemma D.9.

Let
V
be a value. Then
\mathrel{\vdash}V:\mathsf{Ok}
, in particular:

•

If
V
is a numeral, then
\mathrel{\vdash}V:\mathsf{Int}
.

•

If
V
is an atom
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}
, then
\mathrel{\vdash}V:\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}
.

•

If
V
is an abstraction, then
\mathrel{\vdash}V:\mathsf{Fun}
.

•

If
V
is a pair, then
\mathrel{\vdash}V:\mathsf{PairVal}
.

Proof.

Straightforward induction on
V
.
∎

Lemma D.10 (Cut Admissibility for Values).

Let
V
be a value, and
A
and
B
types with
A\sqsubseteq{B}^{c}
. If both
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}V:A,\,\Delta
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}V:B,\,\Delta
hold, then
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta
follows.

Proof.

First we consider the case that one of
A
or
B
is subtype equivalent to
\mathsf{Top}
, let us suppose it is
A
because the other case is symmetrical –
A\sqsubseteq{B}^{c}
iff
B\sqsubseteq{A}^{c}
. Then it follows that
{B}^{c}\mathrel{\sim}\mathsf{Top}
. In such a case, we can always find another type
A^{\prime}\mathrel{\not\sim}\mathsf{Top}
such that
A^{\prime}\sqsubseteq{B}^{c}
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}V:A^{\prime},\,\Delta
, and thus we may conduct the proof assuming wlog
A\mathrel{\not\sim}\mathsf{Top}
. To see this, observe that, by Lemma
D.9
, subtyping and weakening, we obtain
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}V:\mathsf{Ok},\,\Delta
and, of course,
\mathsf{Ok}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Top}\mathrel{\sim}{B}^{c}
.

So, assume
A\mathrel{\not\sim}\mathsf{Top}
,
B\mathrel{\not\sim}\mathsf{Top}
,
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}V:A,\,\Delta
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}V:B,\,\Delta
, while
\Gamma\nvdash\Delta
(i.e., the negation of
Lemma

D.10
). We show that this always leads to a contradiction by induction on the shape of
V
.

Case
(
V
is of shape
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}
).
Since
\Gamma\nvdash\Delta
, applying
Lemma

D.7
(
Inversion
) to
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}:A,\,\Delta
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}:B,\,\Delta
, we obtain
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}\sqsubseteq A
and
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}\sqsubseteq B
. By transitivity of subtyping, from
A\sqsubseteq{B}^{c}
and
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}\sqsubseteq A
, we derive
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}\sqsubseteq{B}^{c}
.

Now, applying
Theorem

6.1
(
Soundness of Subtyping
) to both
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}\sqsubseteq B
and
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}\sqsubseteq{B}^{c}
, we conclude
[\![\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}]\!]\subseteq[\![B]\!]
and
[\![\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}]\!]\subseteq[\![{B}^{c}]\!]
. This implies
[\![\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}]\!]\subseteq[\![B]\!]\cap[\![{B}^{c}]\!]
. However, by
Definition

3.1
,
[\![B]\!]\cap[\![{B}^{c}]\!]=[\![B]\!]\cap[\![\mathsf{Top}]\!]\setminus[\![B]\!]=\varnothing
. Thus
[\![\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}]\!]\subseteq\varnothing
. But since
[\![\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}]\!]=\{\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}\}\neq\varnothing
(again by
Definition

3.1
), we arrive at a contradiction.

Case
(
V
is of shape
\underline{n}
).
Since
\Gamma\nvdash\Delta
, applying
Lemma

D.7
(
Inversion
) to
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\underline{n}:A,\,\Delta
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\underline{n}:B,\,\Delta
yields
\mathsf{Int}\sqsubseteq B
and
\mathsf{Int}\sqsubseteq{B}^{c}
. This leads to a contradiction by an argument analogous to the case for
V=\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}
.

Case
(
V
is of shape
(W_{1},W_{2})
).
Here we have
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}(W_{1},W_{2}):A,\,\Delta
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}(W_{1},W_{2}):B,\,\Delta
, where both
W_{1}
and
W_{2}
are values.
Since
\Gamma\nvdash\Delta
, by
Lemma

D.7
(
Inversion
), there exists
A_{1},\,A_{2}
and
B_{1},\,B_{2}
, such that:

\begin{array}[]{c@{\;\land\;}c@{\;\land\;}c}(A_{1},A_{2})\sqsubseteq A&\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}W_{1}:A_{1},\,\Delta&\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}W_{2}:A_{2},\,\Delta\\
(B_{1},B_{2})\sqsubseteq B&\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}W_{1}:B_{1},\,\Delta&\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}W_{2}:B_{2},\,\Delta\end{array}

By transitivity of subtyping, from
(A_{1},A_{2})\sqsubseteq A
and
A\sqsubseteq{B}^{c}
, we have
(A_{1},A_{2})\sqsubseteq{B}^{c}
. Applying the subtyping rule
CompR
to this yields
B\sqsubseteq{(A_{1},A_{2})}^{c}
. Combining this with
(B_{1},B_{2})\sqsubseteq B
via transitivity gives
(B_{1},B_{2})\sqsubseteq{(A_{1},A_{2})}^{c}
. By
Lemma

D.17
, this implies either
B_{1}\sqsubseteq{A_{1}}^{c}
or
B_{2}\sqsubseteq{A_{2}}^{c}
.

If
B_{1}\sqsubseteq{A_{1}}^{c}
, the induction hypothesis applied to
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}W_{1}:A_{1},\,\Delta
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}W_{1}:B_{1},\,\Delta
implies
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta
. Similarly, if
B_{2}\sqsubseteq{A_{2}}^{c}
, the induction hypothesis applied to
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}W_{2}:A_{2},\Delta
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}W_{2}:B_{2},\Delta
again gives
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta
. In either case, we conclude
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta
, contradicting the assumption that
\Gamma\nvdash\Delta
.

Case
(
V
is
\lambda x.\,M
).
Here we have
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\lambda x.\,M:A,\,\Delta
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\lambda x.\,M:B,\,\Delta
.
Since
\Gamma\nvdash\Delta
, by
Lemma

D.7
(
Inversion
):

\begin{array}[]{c@{\text{ such that: }}c@{\;\land\;}c}\exists A_{1},A_{2}&A_{1}\to A_{2}\sqsubseteq A&\Gamma,x:A_{1}\mathrel{\vdash}M:A_{2},\Delta\\
\exists B_{1},B_{2}&B_{1}\to B_{2}\sqsubseteq B&\Gamma,x:B_{1}\mathrel{\vdash}M:B_{2},\Delta\end{array}

By transitivity of subtyping, from
A\sqsubseteq{B}^{c}
and
A_{1}\to A_{2}\sqsubseteq A
, we have
A_{1}\to A_{2}\sqsubseteq{B}^{c}
. Applying the subtyping rule
CompR
to this yields
B\sqsubseteq A_{1}\to A_{2}
. Combining this with
B_{1}\to B_{2}\sqsubseteq B
via transitivity gives
B_{1}\to B_{2}\sqsubseteq{(A_{1}\to A_{2})}^{c}
.

By
Theorem

6.1
(
Soundness of Subtyping
),
B_{1}\to B_{2}\sqsubseteq{(A_{1}\to A_{2})}^{c}
implies
[\![B_{1}\to B_{2}]\!]\subseteq[\![{(A_{1}\to A_{2})}^{c}]\!]
. Since
[\![{(A_{1}\to A_{2})}^{c}]\!]=\mathsf{Top}\setminus[\![A_{1}\to A_{2}]\!]
(by
Definition

3.1
), this requires
[\![B_{1}\to B_{2}]\!]\cap[\![A_{1}\to A_{2}]\!]=\varnothing
.

However,
Lemma

D.8
states that
\lambda x.\,\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{div}}}\in[\![C\to D]\!]
for all types
C
and
D
. In particular,
\lambda x.\,\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{div}}}\in[\![B_{1}\to B_{2}]\!]\cap[\![A_{1}\to A_{2}]\!]
. This contradicts our earlier conclusion that
[\![B_{1}\to B_{2}]\!]\cap[\![A_{1}\to A_{2}]\!]=\varnothing
.

∎

Lemma D.11.

Let
V
be a value. If
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}V:A,\,\Delta
,
then
\exists B\,(B\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}\land B\sqsubseteq A)
such that
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}V:B,\,\Delta
.

Proof.

Note, that if
A\mathrel{\sim}\mathsf{Top}
then the result follows immediately by Lemma
D.9
. Otherwise, we start with a simple case analysis on whether
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta
.

•

Suppose
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta
. Then it follows by
Lemma

D.1
(
Weakening
) that
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}V:\mathsf{Bot},\,\Delta
. By
Lemma

A.1
, we have both
\mathsf{Bot}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}
and
\mathsf{Bot}\sqsubseteq A
. Thus when taking
B=\mathsf{Bot}
, we have
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}V:B,\,\Delta
where
B\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}\land B\sqsubseteq A
.

•

Otherwise,
\Gamma\nvdash\Delta
. In this case, we proceed by induction on the shape of
V
.

Case
(
V
is of shape
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}
).
Since
\Gamma\nvdash\Delta
, applying
Lemma

D.7
(
Inversion
) to
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}:A,\,\Delta
yields
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}\sqsubseteq A
. Moreover,
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}:\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a},\,\Delta
holds by the typing rule
Atom
. Thus, let
B=\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}
, we have
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}:B,\,\Delta
where
B\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}\land B\sqsubseteq A
.

Case
(
V
is of shape
\underline{n}
).
Since
\Gamma\nvdash\Delta
, applying
Lemma

D.7
(
Inversion
) to
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\underline{n}:A,\,\Delta
yields
\mathsf{Int}\sqsubseteq A
. Moreover,
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\underline{n}:\mathsf{Int},\,\Delta
holds by the typing rule
Num
. Thus, let
B=\mathsf{Int}
, we have
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\underline{n}:B,\,\Delta
where
B\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}\land B\sqsubseteq A
.

Case
(
V
is of shape
(W_{1},W_{2})
).
Since
\Gamma\nvdash\Delta
, applying
Lemma

D.7
(
Inversion
) to
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}(W_{1},W_{2}):A,\,\Delta
, we obtain:

1

\exists A_{1},\,A_{2}
such that
(A_{1},A_{2})\sqsubseteq A
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}W_{1}:A_{1},\,\Delta
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}W_{2}:A_{2},\,\Delta

2

{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}\sqsubseteq A
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}W_{1}:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c},\,W_{2}:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c},\,\Delta
.

As
W_{1}
and
W_{2}
are values, applying the induction hypothesis to each component, we get:

\displaystyle\exists B_{1}\,(B_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}\land B_{1}\sqsubseteq A_{1})\text{ such that }\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}W_{1}:B_{1},\Delta

\displaystyle\exists B_{2}\,(B_{2}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}\land B_{2}\sqsubseteq A_{2})\text{ such that }\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}W_{2}:B_{2},\Delta

Then
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}W_{i}:\mathsf{Ok},\,\Delta
for
i\in\{1,2\}
by the typing rule
Sub
, and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}(W_{1},W_{2}):(B_{1},B_{2}),\Delta
by the typing rule
Pair
.
Assume
2
hold.

Applying rule
Pair
to
B_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}
and
B_{2}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}
yields
(B_{1},B_{2})\sqsubseteq(\mathsf{Ok},\mathsf{Ok})
.
Since
\mathsf{Pair}=(\mathsf{Ok},\mathsf{Ok})
by definition, we have
(B_{1},B_{2})\sqsubseteq\mathsf{PairVal}
.
Moreover, since
\mathsf{PairVal}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Int}\vee\mathsf{PairVal}\vee\mathsf{Fun}\vee\mathsf{Atom}
(by the subtyping rule
Ok
) and
\mathsf{Int}\vee\mathsf{PairVal}\vee\mathsf{Fun}\vee\mathsf{Atom}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}
(by the subtyping rule
UnionR
), transitivity gives
\mathsf{PairVal}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}
. Conbining this with
(B_{1},B_{2})\sqsubseteq\mathsf{PairVal}
gives
(B_{1},B_{2})\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}
.
Assume
2
is true.

Meanwhile, applying the subtyping rule
Pair
to
B_{1}\sqsubseteq A_{1}
and
B_{2}\sqsubseteq A_{2}
yields
(B_{1},B_{2})\sqsubseteq(A_{1},A_{2})
. Since we also have
(A_{1},A_{2})\sqsubseteq A
from inversion, transitivity gives
(B_{1},B_{2})\sqsubseteq A
. Therefore, when taking
B=(B_{1},B_{2})
, we have
B\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}\land B\sqsubseteq A
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}(W_{1},W_{2}):B,\,\Delta
.

Case
(
V
is of shape
\lambda x.\,M
).
Since
\Gamma\nvdash\Delta
, applying
Lemma

D.7
(
Inversion
) to
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}(W_{1},W_{2}):A,\,\Delta
, we obtain:

\exists B^{\prime},\,C^{\prime}\text{ such that }B^{\prime}\to C^{\prime}\sqsubseteq A\text{ and }\Gamma,\,x:B^{\prime}\mathrel{\vdash}M:C^{\prime},\,\Delta

Applying the typing rule
Abs
to
\Gamma,\,x:B^{\prime}\mathrel{\vdash}M:C^{\prime},\,\Delta
yields
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\lambda x.\,M:B^{\prime}\to C^{\prime},\,\Delta
. From
Lemma

A.1
, we have
\mathsf{Bot}\sqsubseteq B^{\prime}
and
C^{\prime}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Top}
. By the subtyping rule
Fun
, it follows that
B^{\prime}\to C^{\prime}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Fun}
(recall that
\textsf{Fun}=\mathsf{Bot}\to\mathsf{Top}
by definition). By transitivity, combining
B^{\prime}\to C^{\prime}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Fun}
with
\mathsf{Fun}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}
,
B^{\prime}\to C^{\prime}\sqsubseteq A
gives
B^{\prime}\to C^{\prime}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}
.
Since we also know
B^{\prime}\to C^{\prime}\sqsubseteq A
from inversion, when taking
B=B^{\prime}\to C^{\prime}
, we have
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}(W_{1},W_{2}):B,\,\Delta
where
B\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}\land B\sqsubseteq A
.

∎

Lemma D.12 (Canonical Forms of Value Types).

Let
V
be a (closed) value, and let
\Gamma
and
\Delta
be contexts such that
\Gamma\nvdash\Delta
.

(1)

If
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}V:\mathsf{Int},\,\Delta
, then
V=\underline{n}
for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(2)

If
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}V:\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a},\,\Delta
, then
V=\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}
.

(3)

If
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}V:\mathsf{Pair},\,\Delta
, then
V=(V_{1},V_{2})
for some values
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
, and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}V:\mathsf{PairVal},\,\Delta
.

(4)

If
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}V:A\to B,\,\Delta
, then
V=\lambda x.\,M
from some term
M
.

Proof.

We present the proof (1) and (3) of
Lemma

D.12
; the remaining (2) and (4) follow by similar reasoning.

(1)

Assume that
\forall n\in\mathbb{Z}.\,V\neq\underline{n}
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}V:\mathsf{Int},\,\Delta
where
\Gamma\nvdash\Delta
(i.e., the negation of
Lemma

D.12
.(1)). We show that this always leads to a contradiction, by induction on the structure of the closed value
V
.

Case
(
V
is of shape
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}
).
By
Lemma

D.7
(Inversion) applied to
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}:\mathsf{Int}
with
\Gamma\nvdash\Delta
, we obtain
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Int}
. Then
Theorem

6.1
implies
[\![\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}]\!]\subseteq[\![\mathsf{Int}]\!]
. However, this contradicts
Definition

3.1
since
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}\in[\![\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}]\!]
while
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}\in[\![\mathsf{Int}]\!]
.

Case
(
V
is of shape
(V_{1},V_{2})
).
By
Lemma

D.7
(
Inversion
) on
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}(V_{1},V_{2}):\mathsf{Int},\,\Delta
with
\Gamma\nvdash\Delta
, either:

1

\exists A_{1},\,A_{2}
such that
(A_{1},A_{2})\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Int}
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}V_{1}:A_{1},\,\Delta
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}V_{2}:A_{2},\,\Delta

2

{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Int}
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}V_{1}:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c},\,V_{2}:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c},\,\Delta
.

Case
1
.

By
Theorem

6.1
,
(A_{1},A_{2})\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Int}
implies
[\![(A_{1},A_{2})]\!]\sqsubseteq[\![\mathsf{Int}]\!]
. However, by
Definition

3.1
,
[\![(A_{1},A_{2})]\!]
contains pairs only, while
[\![\mathsf{Int}]\!]
contains
\underline{n}\,(n\in\mathbb{Z})
only. This contradicts
[\![(A_{1},A_{2})]\!]\sqsubseteq[\![\mathsf{Int}]\!]
, which would require pairs belong to
[\![\mathsf{Int}]\!]
.

Case
2
.

By
Theorem

6.1
,
{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Int}
implies
[\![{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}]\!]\sqsubseteq[\![\mathsf{Int}]\!]
. However, by
Definition

3.1

\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}\in[\![{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}]\!]
while
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}\notin[\![\mathsf{Int}]\!]
, contradicting
[\![{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}]\!]\sqsubseteq[\![\mathsf{Int}]\!]
.

Case
(
V
is of shape
\lambda x.\,M
).
Applying
Lemma

D.7
(
Inversion
) to
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\lambda x.\,M:\mathsf{Int},\,\Delta
with
\Gamma\nvdash\Delta
, we obtain:

\exists B,\,C\text{ such that }B\to C\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Int}\land\Gamma,x:B\mathrel{\vdash}M:C,\,\Delta

By
Theorem

6.1
,
B\to C\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Int}
implies
[\![B\to C]\!]\sqsubseteq[\![\mathsf{Int}]\!]
. However, while
Lemma

D.8
gives
\lambda x.\,\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{div}}}\in[\![B\to C]\!]
,
Definition

3.1
gives
\lambda x.\,\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{div}}}\notin[\![\mathsf{Int}]\!]
, contradicting
[\![B\to C]\!]\sqsubseteq[\![\mathsf{Int}]\!]
.

Since all possible cases lead to contradictions, we conclude that the original assumption must be false. Therefore, if
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}V:\mathsf{Int}
and
\Gamma\nvdash\Delta
, then
V
must be of the form
\underline{n}
for some integer
n
.

(3)

Assume that
V\neq(V_{1},V_{2})
for any values
V_{1}
,
V_{2}
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}V:\mathsf{Pair},\,\Delta
where
\Gamma\nvdash\Delta
(i.e., the negation of
Lemma

D.12
.(3)). We show that this always leads to a contradiction, by induction on the structure of the closed value
V
.

Case
(
V
is of shape
\underline{n}
for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}
).
Applying
Lemma

D.7
(
Inversion
) to
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\underline{n}:\mathsf{Pair},\,\Delta
with
\Gamma\nvdash\Delta
yields
\underline{n}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Pair}
. Since
\mathsf{Pair}=(\mathsf{Top},\mathsf{Top})
, by
Theorem

6.1
, it follows that
[\![\mathsf{Int}]\!]\sqsubseteq[\![(\mathsf{Top},\mathsf{Top})]\!]
. This would repuire
\underline{n}\in[\![(\mathsf{Top},\mathsf{Top})]\!]
, contradicting
Definition

3.1
.

Case
(
V
is of shape
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}
).
Applying
Lemma

D.7
(
Inversion
) to
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}:\mathsf{Pair},\,\Delta
with
\Gamma\nvdash\Delta
yields
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Pair}
. Since
\mathsf{Pair}=(\mathsf{Top},\mathsf{Top})
, by
Theorem

6.1
, it follows that
[\![\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}]\!]\sqsubseteq[\![(\mathsf{Top},\mathsf{Top})]\!]
. This would repuire
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}\in[\![(\mathsf{Top},\mathsf{Top})]\!]
, contradicting
Definition

3.1
.

Case
(
V
is of shape
\lambda x.\,M
).
Applying
Lemma

D.7
(
Inversion
) to
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\lambda x.\,M:\mathsf{Pair},\,\Delta
with
\Gamma\nvdash\Delta
, we obtain:

\exists B,\,C\text{ such that }B\to C\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Pair}\land\Gamma,x:B\mathrel{\vdash}M:C,\,\Delta

By
Theorem

6.1
with
\mathsf{Pair}=(\mathsf{Top},\mathsf{Top})
,
B\to C\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Pair}
implies
[\![B\to C]\!]\sqsubseteq[\![(\mathsf{Top},\mathsf{Top})]\!]
. However,
Lemma

D.8
gives
\lambda x.\,\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{div}}}\in[\![B\to C]\!]
, which would require
\lambda x.\,\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{div}}}\in[\![(\mathsf{Top},\mathsf{Top})]\!]
. This contradicts
Definition

3.1
.

Since all possible cases lead to contradictions, we conclude that the original assumption must be false. Thus, if
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}V:\mathsf{Pair},\,\Delta
and
\Gamma\nvdash\Delta
, then
V=(V_{1},V_{2})
for some values
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
. Then, by
Lemma

D.7
applied to
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}(V_{1},V_{2}):\mathsf{Pair},\,\Delta
with
\Gamma\nvdash\Delta
, either:

1

\exists A_{1},\,A_{2}
such that
(A_{1},A_{2})\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Pair}
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}V_{1}:A_{1},\,\Delta
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}V_{2}:A_{2},\,\Delta

2

{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Pair}
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}S^{\prime}:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c},\,M:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c},\,\Delta
.

By
Theorem

6.1
(
Soundness of Subtyping
),
{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Pair}
implies
[\![{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}]\!]\sqsubseteq{\mathsf{Pair}}
, which contradicts
Definition

3.1
. Thus case
2
is impossible, and case
1
must be true.
By
Lemma

D.11
,
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}V_{1}:A_{1},\,\Delta
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}V_{2}:A_{2},\,\Delta
implies
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}V_{1}:\mathsf{Ok},\,\Delta
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}V_{2}:\mathsf{Ok},\,\Delta
. Applying the typing rule
Pair
then gives
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}(V_{1},V_{2}):(\mathsf{Ok},\mathsf{Ok}),\,\Delta
. Given
V=(V_{1},V_{2})
and
\mathsf{PairVal}=(\mathsf{Ok},\mathsf{Ok})
,we conclude
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}V:\mathsf{PairVal},\,\Delta
.

∎

Lemma D.13.

For any pattern
p
,
p{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})]\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}
holds.

Proof.

By induction on the shape of pattern
p
.
∎

Lemma D.14.

Let
V
be a closed value and
p
a pattern. If
V\neq p\sigma
for any
\sigma
, then
\mathrel{\vdash}V:{(p{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p)])}^{c}
.

Proof.

By induction on the shape of
V
.

Case
(
V
is of shape
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}
).
Given
V\neq p\sigma
, there must be
p\neq x
(otherwise
V=x[\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}/x]
) and
p\neq\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}
(otherwise
V=^{\prime}a\sigma
for all
\sigma
). Thus, it has to be
p=(p_{1},p_{2})
for some patterns
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
. Consequently,
(p{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p)])=(p_{1},p_{2})\,{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p)]=(p_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p)],p_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p)])
. By
Lemma

D.13
, we have
p_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p)]\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}
and
p_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p)]\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}
. Thus the subtyping rule
Pair
gives
(p_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p)],p_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p)])\sqsubseteq(\mathsf{Ok},\mathsf{Ok})
, i.e.,
(p{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p)])\sqsubseteq\mathsf{PairVal}
. Applying
Lemma

A.1
then yields
{\mathsf{PairVal}}^{c}\sqsubseteq{(p{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p)])}^{c}
.

By
Lemma

D.9
, we have
\mathrel{\vdash}\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}:\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}
. Since
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}\sqsubseteq{\mathsf{PairVal}}^{c}
(by the subtyping rule
Disj
), transitivity then gives
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}\sqsubseteq{(p{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p)])}^{c}
. Therefore, applying the typing rule
Sub
to
\mathrel{\vdash}\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}:\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}
with
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}\sqsubseteq{(p{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p)])}^{c}
, we get
\mathrel{\vdash}V:{(p{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p)])}^{c}
.

Case
(
V
is of shape
\underline{n}
).
By an argument analogous to the case where
V
is of shape
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}
.

Case
(
V
is of shape
\lambda x.\,M
).
By an argument analogous to the case where
V
is of shape
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}
.

Case
(
V
is of shape
(W_{1},W_{2})
).
If
p
is not of shape
(p_{1},p_{2})
, the lemma follows by a similar reasoning with other cases. Otherwise,
p\sigma=(p_{1},p_{2})\,\sigma=(p_{1}\sigma,p_{2}\sigma)
. Since
\forall\sigma.\,(W_{1},W_{2})\neq(p_{1}\sigma,p_{2}\sigma)
, either
\forall\sigma.\,W_{1}\neq p_{1}\sigma
or
\forall\sigma.\,W_{2}\neq p_{2}\sigma
. In the following we prove for the former case, and the latter case follows by an analogous reasoning/

If
\forall\sigma.\,W_{1}\neq p_{1}\sigma
, the induction hypothesis applied to
W_{1}
gives
\mathrel{\vdash}{p_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{1})]}^{c}
, and
Lemma

D.9
gives
\mathrel{\vdash}W_{2}:\mathsf{Ok}
. Applying the typing rule
Pair
then yields
\mathrel{\vdash}(W_{1},W_{2}):({p_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{1})]}^{c},\mathsf{Top})
.

Next we show
({p_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{1})]}^{c},\mathsf{Top})\sqsubseteq{(p_{1},p_{2}){\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p)]}^{c}
. For any type
B
, by the subtyping rule
PairC
we have:

({p_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{1})]}^{c},\mathsf{Top})\lor(\mathsf{Ok},{B}^{c})\sqsubseteq{(p_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{1})],B)}^{c}

and by the subtyping rule
UnionR
we have:

({p_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{1})]}^{c},\mathsf{Top})\sqsubseteq({p_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{1})]}^{c},\mathsf{Top})\lor(\mathsf{Ok},{B}^{c})

Thus by transitivity, for any type
B
:

(8)

({p_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{1})]}^{c},\mathsf{Top})\sqsubseteq{(p_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{1})],B)}^{c}

Moreover, since
\mathsf{FV}(p_{1})\subseteq\mathsf{FV}(p)
,
{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{1})]
is the restriction of
{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p)]
on
\mathsf{FV}(p_{1})
. Thus
p{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p)]=(p_{1},p_{2})\,{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p)]=(p_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p)],p_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p)])=(p_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{1})],p_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p)])
, i.e.,

p{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p)]=({p_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{1})]},p_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p)])

Let
B=p_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p)]
in
Equation

8
to get:

(9)

({p_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{1})]}^{c},\mathsf{Top})\sqsubseteq{(p_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{1})],p_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p)])}^{c}\equiv{p{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p)]}^{c}

Finally, applying the typing rule
Sub
to
\mathrel{\vdash}(W_{1},W_{2}):({p_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{1})]}^{c},\mathsf{Top})
with
Equation

9
, we conclude that
\mathrel{\vdash}V:{p{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p)]}^{c}
.

∎

For the following, it is useful to have a more explicit description of the possible shapes of stuck terms.

Lemma D.15 (Stuck Terms).

The (closed) stuck terms may equivalently be described by the following grammar:

\begin{array}[]{lll}S,\,T\;\Coloneqq\;(S,\,M)&\mid(V,\,S)\mid S\otimes M\mid S\Join M\mid S\,M\mid\underline{n}\otimes S\mid\underline{n}\Join S&\\
&\mid V\otimes M\mid V\Join M\mid\underline{n}\otimes V\mid\underline{n}\Join V&\text{when $V$ is not of shape $\underline{n}$}\\
&\mid V\,M&\text{when $V$ is not of shape $\lambda x.\,M$}\\
&\mid\mathsf{match}\,S\,\mathsf{with}\,\{|_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\mapsto N_{i}\}\\
&\mid\mathsf{match}\,V\,\mathsf{with}\,\{\mid_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\mapsto N_{i}\}&\text{when $\forall i\in[1,k].\ \forall\sigma.\ V\neq p_{i}\sigma$}\end{array}

Lemma D.16.

For any stuck term
S
, we can derive the typing judgement
\mathrel{\vdash}S:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}
.

Proof.

By induction on the shape of
S
.

Case
(
S
is of shape
(S^{\prime},M)
).
Applying the induction hypothesis to
S^{\prime}
yields
\mathrel{\vdash}S:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}
. By
Lemma

D.1
(
Weakening
), it follows that
\mathrel{\vdash}S:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c},\,M:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}
, to which we apply the typing rule
PairE
to derive
\mathrel{\vdash}(S^{\prime},M):{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}
.

Case
(
S
is of shape
(V,S^{\prime})
).
By an analogous argument with the case where
S
is of shape
(S^{\prime},M)
.

Case
(
S
is of shape
S^{\prime}\otimes M
).
Applying the induction hypothesis to
S^{\prime}
yields
\mathrel{\vdash}S^{\prime}:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}
. Moreover, from the subtyping rule
Ok
, we have
\mathsf{Int}\vee\mathsf{PairVal}\vee\mathsf{Fun}\vee\mathsf{Atom}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}
. We also have
\mathsf{Int}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Int}\vee\mathsf{PairVal}\vee\mathsf{Fun}\vee\mathsf{Atom}
from the subtyping rule
UnionR
. Then, transitivity gives
\mathsf{Int}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}
. By Lemma
A.1
, this implies
{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}\sqsubseteq{\mathsf{Int}}^{c}
.

Consequently, applying the typing rule
Sub
to
\mathrel{\vdash}S^{\prime}:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}
gives
\mathrel{\vdash}S^{\prime}:{\mathsf{Int}}^{c}
. By
Lemma

D.1
(
Weakening
), it follows that
\mathrel{\vdash}S^{\prime}:{\mathsf{Int}}^{c},\,M:{\mathsf{Int}}^{c}
, to which we apply the typing rule
OpE
to derive
\mathrel{\vdash}S^{\prime}\otimes M:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}
.

Case
(
S
is of shape
V\otimes M
, where
\forall n\in\mathbb{Z}.\,V\neq\underline{n}
).
It follows from Lemma
D.9
that
\mathrel{\vdash}V:{\mathsf{Int}}^{c}
. By
(Weakening)
,
\mathrel{\vdash}V:{\mathsf{Int}}^{c},\,M:{\mathsf{Int}}^{c}
. Thus, applying the typing rule
OpE
yields
\mathrel{\vdash}V\otimes M:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c},\,\Delta
as desired.

Case
(
S
is of shape
\underline{n}\otimes S^{\prime}
).
By an analogous argument with the case where
S
is of shape
S^{\prime}\otimes M
.

Case
(
S
is of shape
\underline{n}\otimes V
, where
V\neq\underline{n}
for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
).
By an analogous argument with the case where
S
is of shape
V\otimes M
and
\forall n\in\mathbb{Z}.\,V\neq\underline{n}
.

Case
(
S
is of shape
{S^{\prime}}\,{M}
).
By the induction hypothesis,
\mathrel{\vdash}S^{\prime}:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}
. Moreover, from the subtyping rule
Ok
, we have
\mathsf{Int}\vee\mathsf{PairVal}\vee\mathsf{Fun}\vee\mathsf{Atom}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}
. We also have
\mathsf{Fun}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Int}\vee\mathsf{PairVal}\vee\mathsf{Fun}\vee\mathsf{Atom}
from the subtyping rule
UnionR
. Then, transitivity gives
\mathsf{Fun}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}
. By the subtyping rule
CompR
, this implies
{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}\sqsubseteq{\mathsf{Fun}}^{c}
. Thus, applying the typing rule
App
to
\mathrel{\vdash}S^{\prime}:{\mathsf{Fun}}^{c}
gives
\mathrel{\vdash}{S^{\prime}}\,{M}:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}
.

Case
(
S
is of shape
{V}\,{M}
, where
V
is not of shape
\lambda x.\,M
).
Then it follows from Lemma
D.9
that
\mathrel{\vdash}V:{\mathsf{Fun}}^{c}
, to which we apply the typing rule
App
to conclude
\mathrel{\vdash}{V}\,{M}:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}
.

Case
(
S
is of shape
\mathsf{match}\,S^{\prime}\,\mathsf{with}\,\{|_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\mapsto N_{i}
}).
By the induction hypothesis,
\mathrel{\vdash}S^{\prime}:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}
holds. From
Lemma

D.13
,
p_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})]\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}
holds for any
i\in[1,k]
. Consequently,
UnionL
gives
\textstyle\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})]\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}
. Applying Lemma
A.1
, we get
{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}\sqsubseteq{(\textstyle\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})])}^{c}
.

Therefore, applying the typing rule
Sub
to
\mathrel{\vdash}S^{\prime}:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}
yields
\mathrel{\vdash}S^{\prime}:{\textstyle\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})]}^{c}
. By the typing rule
MatchE
, it follows that
\mathrel{\vdash}\mathsf{match}\,S^{\prime}\,\mathsf{with}\,\{|_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\mapsto N_{i}\}:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}
.

Case
(
S
is of shape
\mathsf{match}\,V\,\mathsf{with}\,\{|_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\mapsto N_{i}
}, where
\forall i.\,\sigma.\,V\neq p_{i}\sigma
).
By
Lemma

D.14
, since
\forall i.\,\sigma.\,V\neq p_{i}\sigma
, it follows that
\forall i.\,\mathrel{\vdash}V:{(p_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})])}^{c}
for any
i
. If
k=1
then the result follows immediately by subtyping and
(MatchE)
. Otherwise,
k>1
and, by disjointness of patterns, it must be that each pattern is either a pair or an atom. Consideration of the proof of Lemma
D.11
shows that there is some type
B
(e.g. for
V
a numeral, this can be taken to be
\mathsf{Int}
, for
V
a function this may be taken to be any arrow, and so on) such that
\mathrel{\vdash}V:B
and
B\sqsubseteq{(p_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})])}^{c}
. Therefore
B\sqsubseteq{(\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})])}^{c}
and thus
\mathrel{\vdash}V:{(\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})])}^{c}
. Therefore, the result is obtained by
(MatchE)
.

∎

Lemma D.17.

The subtyping relation satisfies the following invariants:

(1)

If
A\to B\sqsubseteq A^{\prime}\to B^{\prime}
, then
A^{\prime}\sqsubseteq A
and
B\sqsubseteq B^{\prime}
.

(2)

If
A\sqsubseteq\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}B_{i}
, and
A
is
not
a complement or union, then
\exists j\,(1\leq j\leq k)
such that
A\sqsubseteq B_{j}
.

(3)

If
\textstyle\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}B_{i}\sqsubseteq A
, then
\forall j\,(1\leq j\leq k)
,
B_{j}\sqsubseteq A
.

(4)

If
(A,B)\sqsubseteq(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
, then
A\sqsubseteq A^{\prime}
and
B\sqsubseteq B^{\prime}
.

(5)

If
(A,B)\sqsubseteq{(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})}^{c}
, then
A\sqsubseteq{A^{\prime}}^{c}
or
B\sqsubseteq{B^{\prime}}^{c}
.

(6)

If
(A,B)\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}
, then
A\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}
and
B\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}
.

Proof.

By the equivalence of
\sqsubseteq
and
\leq
, for the proof we will use
\leq
and the rules from
Figure

6
.

(1)

We consider which rule could have been used to derive
A\to B\leq A^{\prime}\to B^{\prime}
.

(Refl)
:

We have
A\to B\leq A\to B
, and by
(Refl)
,
A\leq A
and
B\leq B
as required.

(Fun)
:

Immediate from the premises.

Otherwise:

The conclusion could not have been derived by any other subtyping rule.

(2)

By inspection of the subtyping rules, we see the only rule that could have been used to derive the conclusion is
(UnionR)
, whose premise is exactly the result required.

(3)

We proceed by induction on the derivation of
\textstyle\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}B_{i}\leq A
.

(Refl)
:

In this case, we have
\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}B_{i}\leq\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}B_{i}
. By
(Refl)
,
\forall j\in[1,k],B_{j}\leq B_{j}
. Then, by applying
(UnionR)

k
times, we have
\forall j\in[1,k],B_{j}\leq\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}B_{i}
as required.

Otherwise:

For every other rule (R) that could have derived the conclusion, the result follows immediately from the induction hypotheses and
k
applications of (R).

(4)

We consider which rule could have been used to derive
(A,B)\leq(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
.

(Refl)
:

By
(Refl)
,
A\leq A
and
B\leq B
as required.

(Pair)
:

Immediate from the premises.

Otherwise:

The conclusion could not have been derived by any other subtyping rule.

(5)

We consider which rule could have been used to derive
(A,B)\leq{(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})}^{c}
.

(CompRPair)
:

Not possible since
(A,B)
is disjoint from
\mathsf{Int},\mathsf{Fun},\mathsf{Atom}
, so the premise cannot be true.

(CompRPairL)
:

Immediate from premise.

(CompRPairR)
:

Immediate from premise.

Otherwise:

The conclusion could not have been derived by any other subtyping rule.

(6)

The only rule that could have been used to derive
(A,B)\leq\mathsf{Ok}
is
(OkR)
with the premise
(A,B)\leq\mathsf{PairVal}
. The only rule we could have used to derive the premise is either
(Refl)
, in which case
A=\mathsf{Ok},B=\mathsf{Ok}
(since
\mathsf{PairVal}=(\mathsf{Ok},\mathsf{Ok})
), so it follows by
(Refl)
that
A\leq\mathsf{Ok},B\leq\mathsf{Ok}
; or
(Pair)
, in which case the result is immediate from the premises.

∎

For the following, it is useful to have a more explicit description of normal forms:

Lemma D.18 (Normal Forms).

The (closed) normal forms may equivalently be described by the following grammar:

\begin{array}[]{lll}U\;\Coloneqq\;\underline{n}&\mid\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}\mid\lambda x.\,M\mid(U_{1},\,U_{2})&\\
&\mid(U,\,M)&\textup{{when $U$ is not of shape $V$}}\\
&\mid U\otimes M\mid U\Join M&\textup{{when $U$ is not of shape $\underline{n}$}}\\
&\mid\underline{n}\otimes U\mid\underline{n}\Join U&\textup{{when $U$ is not of shape $\underline{n}$}}\\
&\mid U\,M&\textup{{when $U$ is not of shape $\lambda x.\,M$}}\\
&\mid\mathsf{match}\,U\,\mathsf{with}\,\{\mid_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\mapsto N_{i}\}&\textup{{when $\forall i\in[1,k].\ \forall\sigma.\ U\neq p_{i}\sigma$}}\end{array}

Proof.

By structural inspection of terms and analysis of the reduction relation.
∎

Lemma D.19 (Cut Admissibility of Normal Forms).

Let
U
be a closed normal form, and
A
and
B
types with
A\sqsubseteq{B}^{c}
. If both
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}U:A,\,\Delta
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}U:B,\,\Delta
hold, then
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta
follows.

Proof.

First we observe that it suffices to consider the case in which
A\mathrel{\not\sim}\mathsf{Top}
and
B\mathrel{\not\sim}\mathsf{Top}
. To see this, suppose that one of
A
or
B
is subtype equivalent to
\mathsf{Top}
, let us suppose it is
A
because the other case is symmetrical. Then it follows that
{B}^{c}\mathrel{\sim}\mathsf{Top}
. By Lemma
D.9
and Lemma
D.16
, we obtain
\mathrel{\vdash}U:A^{\prime}
where
A^{\prime}
is either the type
\mathsf{Ok}
or the type
{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}
. Since
\mathsf{Ok}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Top}\mathrel{\sim}{B}^{c}
and
{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Top}\mathrel{\sim}{B}^{c}
, this
A^{\prime}
can be used in place of
A
.

So, assume
A\mathrel{\not\sim}\mathsf{Top}
,
B\mathrel{\not\sim}\mathsf{Top}
,
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}U:A,\,\Delta
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}U:B,\,\Delta
, while
\Gamma\nvdash\Delta
(i.e., the negation of
Lemma

D.10
). We show that this always leads to a contradiction by induction on the shape of
U
.

Case
(
U
is of shape
\underline{n}
).
Since
\Gamma\nvdash\Delta
, applying
Lemma

D.7
(
Inversion
) to
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\underline{n}:A,\,\Delta
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\underline{n}:B,\,\Delta
yields
\mathsf{Int}\sqsubseteq B
and
\mathsf{Int}\sqsubseteq{B}^{c}
. This leads to a contradiction by an argument analogous to the case for
U=\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}
.

Case
(
U
is of shape
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}
).
Since
\Gamma\nvdash\Delta
, applying
Lemma

D.7
(
Inversion
) to
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}:A,\,\Delta
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}:B,\,\Delta
, we obtain
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}\sqsubseteq A
and
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}\sqsubseteq B
. By transitivity of subtyping, from
A\sqsubseteq{B}^{c}
and
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}\sqsubseteq A
, we derive
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}\sqsubseteq{B}^{c}
.

Now, applying
Theorem

6.1
(
Soundness of Subtyping
) to both
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}\sqsubseteq B
and
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}\sqsubseteq{B}^{c}
, we conclude
[\![\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}]\!]\subseteq[\![B]\!]
and
[\![\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}]\!]\subseteq[\![{B}^{c}]\!]
. This implies
[\![\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}]\!]\subseteq[\![B]\!]\cap[\![{B}^{c}]\!]
. However, by
Definition

3.1
,
[\![B]\!]\cap[\![{B}^{c}]\!]=[\![B]\!]\cap[\![\mathsf{Top}]\!]\setminus[\![B]\!]=\varnothing
. Thus
[\![\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}]\!]\subseteq\varnothing
. But since
[\![\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}]\!]=\{\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}\}\neq\varnothing
(again by
Definition

3.1
), we arrive at a contradiction.

Case
(
U
is of shape
(M_{1},M_{2})
).
Here we have
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}(M_{1},M_{2}):A,\,\Delta
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}(M_{1},M_{2}):B,\,\Delta
. By
Lemma

D.7
(
Inversion
) on
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}(M_{1},M_{2}):A,\,\Delta
with
\Gamma\nvdash\Delta
, either:

1

\exists A_{1},\,A_{2}
such that
(A_{1},A_{2})\sqsubseteq A
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M_{1}:A_{1},\,\Delta
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M_{2}:A_{2},\,\Delta

2

{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}\sqsubseteq A
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M_{1}:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c},\,M_{2}:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c},\,\Delta
.

Similarly, by
Lemma

D.7
(
Inversion
) on
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}(M_{1},M_{2}):B,\,\Delta
with
\Gamma\nvdash\Delta
, either:

i

\exists B_{1},\,B_{2}
such that
(B_{1},B_{2})\sqsubseteq B
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M_{1}:B_{1},\,\Delta
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M_{2}:B_{2},\,\Delta

ii

{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}\sqsubseteq B
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M_{1}:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c},\,M_{2}:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c},\,\Delta
.

Moreover, since
U=(M_{1},M_{2})
for some terms
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
, for
U
to be a normal form, there must be either

(1)

M_{1}=U_{1}
and
M_{2}=U_{2}
(i.e.,
U=(U_{1},U_{2})
), for some normal forms
U_{1}
and
U_{2}
.

(2)

M_{1}=S
and
M_{2}=M
(i.e.,
U=(S,M)
), for some stuck term
S
and some term
M
.

Case
1

i
.

By transitivity, from
(A_{1},A_{2})\sqsubseteq A
and
A\sqsubseteq{B}^{c}
, we have
(A_{1},A_{2})\sqsubseteq{B}^{c}
. Apply
Lemma

A.1
to
(B_{1},B_{2})\sqsubseteq B
gives
{B}^{c}\sqsubseteq{(B_{1},B_{2})}^{c}
. Combining this with
(A_{1},A_{2})\sqsubseteq{B}^{c}
via transitivity gives
(A_{1},A_{2})\sqsubseteq{(B_{1},B_{2})}^{c}
. By
Lemma

D.17
, this implies either
A_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}\land A_{2}\sqsubseteq{B_{2}}^{c}
or
A_{1}\sqsubseteq{B_{1}}^{c}
. Then we proceed by further case analasis on the shape of
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
.

(1)

If
M_{1}=U_{1}
and
M_{2}=U_{2}
(i.e.,
U=(U_{1},U_{2})
), for some normal forms
U_{1}
and
U_{2}
:

1

If
A_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}\land A_{2}\sqsubseteq{B_{2}}^{c}
, the induction hypothesis on
U_{1}
(with
\Gamma\vdash U_{2}:A_{2},\Delta
and
\Gamma\vdash U_{2}:B_{2},\Delta
) gives
\Gamma\vdash\Delta
.

2

If
A_{1}\sqsubseteq{B_{1}}^{c}
, the induction hypothesis on
U_{1}
(with
\Gamma\vdash U_{1}:A_{1},\Delta
and
\Gamma\vdash U_{1}:B_{1},\Delta
) also gives
\Gamma\vdash\Delta
.

In both cases, this contradicts the assumption that
\Gamma\nvdash\Delta
.

(2)

M_{1}=S
and
M_{2}=M
(i.e.,
U=(S,M)
), for some stuck term
S
and some term
M
. By
Lemma

D.16
we have
\mathrel{\vdash}S:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}
. Then
Weakening
gives
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}S:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c},\,\Delta
.

1

If
A_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}\land A_{2}\sqsubseteq{B_{2}}^{c}
, applying the typing rule
Sub
to
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}A_{1}:\mathsf{Ok},\,\Delta
gives
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}S:\mathsf{Ok},\,\Delta
. Thus, the induction hypothesis applied to
S
(with
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}S:\mathsf{Ok},\,\Delta
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}S:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c},\,\Delta
) gives
\Gamma\vdash\Delta
.

2

If
A_{1}\sqsubseteq{B_{1}}^{c}
, applying the induction hypothesis to
U_{1}
(with
\Gamma\vdash U_{1}:A_{1},\Delta
and
\Gamma\vdash U_{1}:B_{1},\Delta
) gives
\Gamma\vdash\Delta
directly.

Again, both cases contradict the assumption that
\Gamma\nvdash\Delta
.

Case
1

ii
.

By transitivity of subtyping, from
(A_{1},A_{2})\sqsubseteq A
and
A\sqsubseteq{B}^{c}
, we have
(A_{1},A_{2})\sqsubseteq{B}^{c}
. Applying the subtyping rule
CompL
to
{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}\sqsubseteq B
yields
{B}^{c}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}
. Combining
(A_{1},A_{2})\sqsubseteq{B}^{c}
and
{B}^{c}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}
via transitivity gives
(A_{1},A_{2})\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}
. By
Lemma

D.17
, this implies
A_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}
and
A_{2}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}
.

(1)

If
M_{1}=U_{1}
and
M_{2}=U_{2}
(i.e.,
U=(U_{1},U_{2})
), for some normal forms
U_{1}
and
U_{2}
:

With
A_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}
and
A_{2}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}
, the typing rule
Sub
applied to
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}U_{1}:A_{1},\,\Delta
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}U_{2}:A_{2},\,\Delta
gives
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}U_{1}:\mathsf{Ok},\,\Delta
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}U_{2}:\mathsf{Ok},\,\Delta
. By
Lemma

D.1
(
Weakening
) on
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}U_{1}:\mathsf{Ok},\,\Delta
, we have:

(10)

\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}U_{1}:\mathsf{Ok},\,U_{2}:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c},\,\Delta

Moreover, from
ii
, we also have

(11)

\Gamma\vdash U_{1}:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c},U_{2}:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c},\Delta

Thus, by the induction hypothesis (with
A=\mathsf{Ok}
and
B={\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}
) on
U_{1}
,
Equation

10
and
Equation

11
implies
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}U_{2}:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c},\,\Delta
. Since we have already shown
\Gamma\vdash U_{2}:\mathsf{Ok},\Delta
, by the induction hypothesis on
U_{2}
(again with
A=\mathsf{Ok}
and
B={\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}
), it follows that
\Gamma\vdash\Delta
. This contradicts our assumption that
\Gamma\nvdash\Delta
.

(2)

If
M_{1}=S
and
M_{2}=M
(i.e.,
U=(S,M)
), for some stuck term
S
and some term
M
:

Applying
Lemma

D.16
to
S
gives
\mathrel{\vdash}S:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}
, which implies
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}S:{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c},\,\Delta
by
Weakening
. Meanwhile, applying the typing rule
Sub
to
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}S:A_{1},\,\Delta
with
A_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}
gives
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}S:\mathsf{Ok},\,\Delta
. Thus, the induction hypothesis on
S
gives
\Gamma\nvdash\Delta

Therefore, both cases contradict the assumption that
\Gamma\nvdash\Delta
.

Case
2

i
.

This case follows symmetrically from
Case

1

ii
via
A\sqsubseteq{B}^{c}\Leftrightarrow B\sqsubseteq{A}^{c}
.

Case
2

ii
.

Applying the subtyping rule
CompL
to
{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}\sqsubseteq B
gives
{B}^{c}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}
. By transitivity, chaining
{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}\sqsubseteq A
,
A\sqsubseteq{B}^{c}
, and
{B}^{c}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}
yields
{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}
. By
Theorem

6.1
(
Soundness of Subtyping
), this implies
[\![{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}]\!]\subseteq[\![\mathsf{Ok}]\!]
.

Now, combining
[\![{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}]\!]\subseteq[\![\mathsf{Ok}]\!]
and
[\![{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}]\!]=[\![\mathsf{Top}]\!]\setminus[\![\mathsf{Ok}]\!]
(from
Definition

3.1
), we derive
[\![{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}]\!]\subseteq[\![\mathsf{Ok}]\!]\cap([\![\mathsf{Top}]\!]\setminus[\![\mathsf{Ok}]\!])=\varnothing
. Thus
[\![{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}]\!]=[\![\mathsf{Top}]\!]\setminus[\![\mathsf{Ok}]\!]=\varnothing
. This requires all normal forms to be values (i.e.,
[\![\mathsf{Top}]\!]\subseteq[\![\mathsf{Ok}]\!]
), contradicting the existence of stuck terms.

Case
(
U
is of shape
M_{1}\otimes M_{2}
).
Here we have
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M_{1}\otimes M_{2}:A,\,\Delta
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M_{1}\otimes M_{2}:B,\,\Delta
. By
Lemma

D.7
(
Inversion
) on
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M_{1}\otimes M_{2}:A,\,\Delta
with
\Gamma\nvdash\Delta
, either:

1

\mathsf{Int}\sqsubseteq A
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M_{1}:\mathsf{Int},\,\Delta
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M_{2}:\mathsf{Int},\,\Delta

2

{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}\sqsubseteq A
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M_{1}:{\mathsf{Int}}^{c},\,M_{2}:{\mathsf{Int}}^{c},\,\Delta
.

Similarly, by
Lemma

D.7
(
Inversion
) on
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M_{1}\otimes M_{2}:B,\,\Delta
with
\Gamma\nvdash\Delta
, either:

i

\mathsf{Int}\sqsubseteq B
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M_{1}:\mathsf{Int},\,\Delta
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M_{2}:\mathsf{Int},\,\Delta

ii

{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}\sqsubseteq B
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M_{1}:{\mathsf{Int}}^{c},\,M_{2}:{\mathsf{Int}}^{c},\,\Delta

In cases
1

ii
and
2

i
,
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta
follows immediately from the induction hypothesis.
Moreover, since
U=M_{1}\otimes M_{2}
for some terms
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
, for
U
to be a normal form, there must be either

(1)

M_{1}=U_{1}
and
U_{1}
is not a numeral.

(2)

M_{1}=\underline{n}
for some
n
and
M_{2}=U_{2}
and
U_{2}
is not a numeral.

It follows from (1) via Lemmas
D.9
and
D.16
that
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M_{1}:{\mathsf{Int}}^{c},\,\Delta
and similarly from (2) that
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}M_{2}:{\mathsf{Int}}^{c},\,\Delta
. Therefore, in both cases, consideration of
1

i
yields
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta
by the induction hypothesis.
Therefore, all that remains is to consider
2

ii
. In this case, we have that
{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}\sqsubseteq A\sqsubseteq{B}^{c}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}
, which is impossible by Theorem
6.1
.

Case
(
U
is of shape
\mathsf{match}\,U^{\prime}\,\mathsf{with}\,\{\mid_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\mapsto N_{i}\}
and
\forall i.\sigma.\,U^{\prime}\neq p_{i}\sigma
).
Here we have
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\mathsf{match}\,U^{\prime}\,\mathsf{with}\,\{\mid_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\mapsto N_{i}\}:A,\,\Delta
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\mathsf{match}\,U^{\prime}\,\mathsf{with}\,\{\mid_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\mapsto N_{i}\}:B,\,\Delta
. By
Lemma

D.7
(
Inversion
) on
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\mathsf{match}\,U^{\prime}\,\mathsf{with}\,\{\mid_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\mapsto N_{i}\}:A,\,\Delta
with
\Gamma\nvdash\Delta
, either:

1

\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}U^{\prime}:\textstyle\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\theta_{i},\,\Delta
and
\forall i\in[1,k].\,\Gamma,\,\theta_{i}\mathrel{\vdash}U^{\prime}:{(p_{i}\theta_{i})}^{c},\,N_{i}:A,\,\Delta
and, for all
i\in[1,k]
and
x\in\textbf{dom}(\theta_{i})
,
\theta_{i}(x)\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}

2

{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}\sqsubseteq A
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}U^{\prime}:{(\textstyle\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})])}^{c},\,\Delta
.

Similarly, by
Lemma

D.7
(
Inversion
) on
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\mathsf{match}\,U^{\prime}\,\mathsf{with}\,\{\mid_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\mapsto N_{i}\}:B,\,\Delta
with
\Gamma\nvdash\Delta
, either:

i

\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}U^{\prime}:\textstyle\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\theta_{i},\,\Delta
and
\forall i\in[1,k].\,\Gamma,\,\theta_{i}\mathrel{\vdash}U^{\prime}:{(p_{i}\theta_{i})}^{c},\,N_{i}:B,\,\Delta
and, for all
i\in[1,k]
and
x\in\textbf{dom}(\theta_{i})
,
\theta_{i}(x)\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}

ii

{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}\sqsubseteq B
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}U^{\prime}:{(\textstyle\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.3984,0.1992,0.3984}{[}}\mathsf{Ok}/x|x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})])}^{c},\,\Delta

In case
2

ii
, we obtain
{\mathsf{Ok}}^{c}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}
and thus
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta
follows from the induction hypothesis. In cases
1

ii
and
2

i
, it follows that
\textstyle\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\theta_{i}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}
and so we may apply the induction hypothesis with
U^{\prime}
to obtain
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta
. Finally, in case
1

i
, for each
i\in[1,k]
, we may apply the induction hypothesis to
N_{i}
to obtain
\Gamma,\,\theta_{i}\mathrel{\vdash}U^{\prime}:{(p_{i}\theta_{i})}^{c},\,\Delta
. Applying the induction hypothesis again to
U^{\prime}
yields
\Gamma,\,\theta_{i}\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta
. Finally, since every type in the range of
\theta_{i}
is a subtype of
\mathsf{Ok}
, every type in
\mathsf{Ok}
is inhabited by a closed value and
\textbf{dom}(\theta_{i})\cap\mathsf{FV}(\Gamma,\,\Delta)=\varnothing
, it follows from Lemma
D.6
that
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta
.

Case
(
U
is of shape
\lambda x.\,M
).
Here we have
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\lambda x.\,M:A,\,\Delta
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\lambda x.\,M:B,\,\Delta
.
Since
\Gamma\nvdash\Delta
, by
Lemma

D.7
(
Inversion
):

\begin{array}[]{c@{\text{ such that: }}c@{\;\land\;}c}\exists A_{1},A_{2}&A_{1}\to A_{2}\sqsubseteq A&\Gamma,x:A_{1}\mathrel{\vdash}M:A_{2},\Delta\\
\exists B_{1},B_{2}&B_{1}\to B_{2}\sqsubseteq B&\Gamma,x:B_{1}\mathrel{\vdash}M:B_{2},\Delta\end{array}

By transitivity of subtyping, from
A\sqsubseteq{B}^{c}
and
A_{1}\to A_{2}\sqsubseteq A
, we have
A_{1}\to A_{2}\sqsubseteq{B}^{c}
. Applying the subtyping rule
CompR
to this yields
B\sqsubseteq A_{1}\to A_{2}
. Combining this with
B_{1}\to B_{2}\sqsubseteq B
via transitivity gives
B_{1}\to B_{2}\sqsubseteq{(A_{1}\to A_{2})}^{c}
.

By
Theorem

6.1
(
Soundness of Subtyping
),
B_{1}\to B_{2}\sqsubseteq{(A_{1}\to A_{2})}^{c}
implies
[\![B_{1}\to B_{2}]\!]\subseteq[\![{(A_{1}\to A_{2})}^{c}]\!]
. Since
[\![{(A_{1}\to A_{2})}^{c}]\!]=\mathsf{Top}\setminus[\![A_{1}\to A_{2}]\!]
(by
Definition

3.1
), this requires
[\![B_{1}\to B_{2}]\!]\cap[\![A_{1}\to A_{2}]\!]=\varnothing
.

However,
Lemma

D.8
states that
\lambda x.\,\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{div}}}\in[\![C\to D]\!]
for all types
C
and
D
. In particular,
\lambda x.\,\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{div}}}\in[\![B_{1}\to B_{2}]\!]\cap[\![A_{1}\to A_{2}]\!]
. This contradicts our earlier conclusion that
[\![B_{1}\to B_{2}]\!]\cap[\![A_{1}\to A_{2}]\!]=\varnothing
.

∎

Note: a corollary of
Lemma

D.10
is as follows.

Corollary D.20.

Let
V
be a value. If
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}V:\mathsf{Bot},\Delta
, then
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta
.

Proof.

We have
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}V:\mathsf{Bot},\Delta
from the assumption.
Meanwhile,
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}V:\mathsf{Top},\Delta
holds for any
V
by the typing rule
Top
.
By
Lemma

A.1
,
\mathsf{Bot}
is a subtype of all types. In particular we have
\mathsf{Bot}\sqsubseteq{\mathsf{Top}}^{c}
. Thus applying
Lemma

D.10
with
A=\mathsf{Bot}
and
B=\mathsf{Top}
gives
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta
.
∎

Lemma D.21.

Let
p
be a pattern, and let
\sigma
be a term substitution, such that
p\sigma
is a value with
\mathsf{FV}(p)=\textbf{dom}(\sigma)
. If
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}p\sigma:A,\,\Delta
where
\Gamma\nvdash\Delta
, then there exists a type substitution
\theta
with
\textbf{dom}(\theta)=\mathsf{FV}(p)
such that:

(1)

\forall x\in\textbf{dom}(\theta).\theta(x)\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}

(2)

\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\sigma\mathrel{::}\theta,\,\Delta

(3)

\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}p\sigma:p\theta,\,\Delta
where
p\theta\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}\land p\theta\sqsubseteq A

Proof.

By induction on the shape of
p
.

Case
(
p
has shape
y
).
In this case,
p\sigma=y\sigma
,
\mathsf{FV}(p)=\{y\}
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}y\sigma:A,\,\Delta
. Since
y\sigma
is a value, by
Lemma

D.11
,
\exists B\,(B\sqsubseteq A\land B\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok})
such that
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}y\sigma:B,\,\Delta
. Define the substitution
\theta=[y\mapsto B]
, then
\textbf{dom}(\theta)=\{y\}=\mathsf{FV}(p)
. We confirm that
\theta
satisfies:

(1)

\forall x\in\textbf{dom}(\theta).\theta(x)\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}
:

Immediate from the definition of
\theta
and
B\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}

(2)

\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\sigma\mathrel{::}\theta,\,\Delta
:

Substituting
y\sigma=\sigma(y)
and
\theta(y)=B
into
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}y\sigma:B,\,\Delta
yields
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\sigma(y):\theta(y),\,\Delta
. Moreover,
\textbf{dom}(\sigma)=\textbf{dom}(\theta)=\{y\}
. Thus all conditions required by
Definition

D.5
(
(
Coherence)) are satisfied.

(3)

\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}p\sigma:p\theta,\,\Delta
where
p\theta\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}\land p\theta\sqsubseteq A
:

Since
B\sqsubseteq A\land B\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}
, by substituting
p\sigma=y\sigma
and
p\theta=B
into
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}y\sigma:B,\Delta
, the condition follows.

Case
(
p
has shape
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}
).
In this case,
p\sigma=\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}\sigma
,
\mathsf{FV}(p)=\varnothing
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}\sigma:A,\,\Delta
. Let
\theta
be the empty substitution so that
\textbf{dom}(\theta)=\mathsf{FV}(p)=\varnothing
. We check that
\theta
satisfies:

(1)

\forall x\in\textbf{dom}(\theta).\,\theta(x)\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}
:

Immediate from
\textbf{dom}(\theta)=\varnothing
.

(2)

\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\sigma\mathrel{::}\theta,\,\Delta
:

Immediate from
\textbf{dom}(\sigma)=\textbf{dom}(\theta)=\varnothing
, by
Definition

D.5
.

(3)

\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}p\sigma:p\theta,\,\Delta
where
p\theta\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}\land p\theta\sqsubseteq A
:

By the typing rule
Atom
, we have
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}:\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a},\,\Delta
. Substituting
p\sigma=\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}\sigma=\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}
and
p\theta=\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}\theta=\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}
into
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}:\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a},\,\Delta
gives
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}p\sigma:p\theta,\,\Delta
. Since
\Gamma\nvdash\Delta
, applying
Lemma

D.7
(
Inversion
) to
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}\sigma:A,\,\Delta
yields
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}\sqsubseteq A
. Finally, since
\mbox{\textquotesingle}\mathsf{a}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Atom}
(by the subtyping rule
Atom
),
\mathsf{Atom}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Int}\vee\mathsf{Pair}\vee\mathsf{Fun}\vee\mathsf{Atom}
(by the subtyping rule
UnionR
) and
\mathsf{Int}\vee\mathsf{Pair}\vee\mathsf{Fun}\vee\mathsf{Atom}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}
(by the subtyping rule
Atom
), we have
p\theta\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}
by transitivity.

Case
(
p
has shape
(p_{1},p_{2})
). In this case
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}(p_{1},p_{2})\,\sigma:A,\,\Delta
and
\textbf{dom}(\sigma)=\mathsf{FV}(p)=\mathsf{FV}(p_{1})\cup\mathsf{FV}(p_{2})
. Let
\sigma_{1}=\sigma|_{\mathsf{FV}(p_{1})}
and
\sigma_{2}=\sigma|_{\mathsf{FV}(p_{2})}
, then
\textbf{dom}(\sigma_{1})=\mathsf{FV}(p_{1})
and
\textbf{dom}(\sigma_{2})=\mathsf{FV}(p_{2})
. Since
